 xii

 1 INTRODUCTION

 RESRAD-RDD&IND is part of the RESRAD family of codes that Argonne National
Laboratory developed for the U.S. Department of Energy (DOE). An earlier version, RESRAD-
RDD published in 2009, dealt only with radiological dispersal device (RDD) incidents (DOE
2009). This new version, RESRAD-RDD&IND, is designed to evaluate both RDD incidents and
improvised nuclear device (IND) incidents.
 This report is Volume 1 of the RESRAD-RDD&IND User’s Manual that documents the
methodology, models, and radionuclide-specific data used in RESRAD-RDD&IND code
Version 2.0. Volume 2 of the RESRAD-RDD&IND User’s Manual is called the User’s Guide
for RESRAD-RDD&IND Code. It describes how to use RESRAD-RDD&IND code Version 2.0
and includes screen shots and parameter information.

1.1 NEED FOR OPERATIONAL GUIDELINES
 Version 2.0 of the RESRAD-RDD&IND computer code is designed to support the
implementation of protective action guides (PAGs) and the derivation of operational guidelines
after a nuclear emergency incident involving a radiological dispersal device (RDD) and/or an
improvised nuclear device (IND) incident (EPA 2017). The basis and need for operational
guidelines are discussed in Section 1.1.1, and the process used to develop operational guidelines
is described in Section 1.1.2.

1.1.1 Basis and Need for Operational Guidelines
 The purpose of operational guidelines after an RDD or IND incident is to support
implementation of the Department of Homeland Security’s (DHS’s) PAGs published in the
Federal Register (73 FR 149; DHS 2008) and the Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA’s)
PAG Manual: Protective Action Guides and Planning Guidance for Radiological Incidents
(EPA-400/R 17/001; EPA 2017). As cited in the DHS PAGs, by agreement with the EPA, the
DHS PAG guidance and its substance will be incorporated without change into the revision of
the 1992 EPA Manual of Protective Action Guides and Protective Actions for Nuclear Incidents
(EPA 1992) and subsequently in the updated 2017 version of the EPA PAG Manual (EPA 2017).
As such, the operational guidelines developed using RESRAD-RDD&IND code and presented in
this report support implementation of the 2017 EPA PAG Manual.
 A PAG is the projected dose to a reference individual from an accidental or deliberate
release of radioactive material at which a specific protective action (e.g., sheltering or evacuation
of the public and restriction of water and food consumption) to reduce or avoid that dose is
recommended. Protective actions are designed to be taken before the anticipated dose is realized.
PAGs are not regulatory limits or firm distinctions between safe and unsafe. Rather, they are
guidelines for determining if protective actions for public and worker safety are warranted for a
specific radiological incident. Table 1.1 below, taken from EPA PAG Manual Table 1-1
(EPA 2017), provides the PAGs, and Table 1.2, taken from EPA PAG Manual Table 3-1,
provides the Emergency Worker Guidelines that are appropriate for use in various phases of
emergency preparedness and in response to an RDD or IND incident. For context, Figure 1.1
depicts the relationship between possible routes of exposure from an RDD or IND incident,
protective measures, and time frames for exposure effects relative to the early, intermediate, and
late or long-term recovery phases of the emergency response.

 The PAGs and Emergency Worker Guidelines are dose-based, and it is often difficult to
readily measure the dose. Therefore, a need exists for operational measurements of radiation
levels or concentrations in various media that can be related to the PAGs to facilitate decisions
regarding the need for protective actions. As a result, operational guidelines are prederived levels
of radioactivity or radionuclide concentrations in various media that can be measured in the field
and compared to the PAGs to quickly determine if protective actions are warranted. For some
situations in an RDD or IND event, quick dose calculations based on measured concentrations in
various media are needed. These dose results can be compared directly to the PAGs or can be
used to derive stay times to facilitate decisions regarding the need for protective actions. For
example, in the hours and days following an incident, there could be an urgent need for decisions
about evacuation or relocation of the public, as well as reentry stay time decisions.
Determinations regarding critical infrastructures such as access to roads and medical facilities, as
well as restoration of power, water, and sewer facilities, may also have to be made. Although
some values already exist (e.g., derived response levels [DRLs] from the Federal Radiological
Monitoring and Assessment Center [FRMAC] for generic cases, from the U.S. Environmental
Protection Agency [EPA] for water, and derived intervention levels [DILs] from the U.S. Food
and Drug Administration [FDA] for food) that could potentially serve as operational guidelines,
many more were needed for handling various situations.

1.1.2 Relationship of Operational Guidelines to PAGs and Other Guidelines

 The operational guidelines and dose calculation methods presented in this report
complement related guidelines called DRLs published by FRMAC. The DRLs support
implementation of the EPA PAG Manual especially for the early phase and the initial portion of
the intermediate phase of a radiological incident. The operational guidelines presented here and
the FRMAC DRLs are analogous, but have been developed to address different audiences and
provide an alternative for incident-specific assessment and decision making.

 The DHS PAGs were promulgated to extend the scope of existing federal guidance
(EPA’s PAGs) to specifically address RDDs and INDs. The EPA PAGs were designed to be
generic to all radiological accidents or incidents, although they were principally designed for
nuclear power plant accidents.

 The FRMAC DRLs are derived for generic radiological disasters and are designed to
address the early phase and initial portion of the intermediate phase of a radiological accident or
incident. They are valid in general and most appropriate for suburban or rural areas. The
operational guidelines and dose calculation method presented in this report are derived
specifically to address RDDs and INDs with an emphasis on issues unique to a dense urban area
during the later portion of the intermediate phase and the late phase of response and to suburban
and rural areas during the late response phases. The operational guidelines are immediately
applicable to an RDD involving any of the 11 most likely radionuclides expected to be employed
for an RDD (i.e., Am-241, Cf-252, Cm-244, Co-60, Cs-137, Ir-192, Po-210, Pu-238, Pu-239,
Ra-226, and Sr-90) or an IND event involving nuclear detonation. The code has included the top
dose-producing (more than 90) radionuclides associated with uranium-fueled detonation.

 Differences may arise between the RESRAD-RDD&IND code and the FRMAC
calculations because computations in the RESRAD-RDD&IND code have been tailored to make
them less generic and more incident-specific. A comparison of the Operational Guidelines Task
(OGT) Group method (i.e., RESRAD-RDD) and the FRMAC method was made in 2009, and the
results indicated that, after all the causes of differences in parameter values are eliminated, the
RESRAD-RDD code produced operational guidelines that are in agreement with the FRMAC
DRLs within 4% differences (DOE 2009).

1.2 OPERATIONAL GUIDELINE GROUPS AND SUBGROUPS

 The operational guidelines and dose calculations for an RDD or IND event are organized
into eight groups that are generally categorized by the phase of emergency response in which
they would be implemented or used for planning purposes. Individual groups are further
categorized into subgroups as appropriate. Table 1.3 provides a summary of groups and
subgroups. A detailed overview of these groups and subgroups is provided below.

 5

 For dose calculations, pretty much same methods are used for both RDD and IND
incidents for all groups except Group A. For an IND incident in Group A, many short-lived
radionuclides are released after a nuclear detonation. A special empirical power function method
is used to estimate the external radiation dose as a function of time in the early phase. For all
other groups, the same methods are used for RDD and IND events for dose calculations.

1.2.1 Group A: Access Control during Emergency Response Operations

 The operational guidelines and dose calculations for Group A are designed to assist
responders in decision making for worker health and safety in the early to intermediate phases of
a response to an RDD or IND incident when the situation has not been fully stabilized or

 6

characterized. They are designed to guide responders in establishing radiological control zones
or boundaries for the areas directly impacted by the RDD or IND incident where first responders
and emergency response personnel are working. They are not intended to restrict emergency
responder access, but rather to inform responders of potential radiological hazards existing in the
areas and to provide tools to those persons who are responsible for radiation protection during
response activities. These operational guidelines and dose calculation results may be used to
restrict the access of nonessential personnel and members of the public to specific areas.

 Group A operational guidelines for an RDD incident are expressed as a series of
reference “stay time” tables for responders who may have access to only limited health physics
information and personal protective equipment at the time of the response. For example, the
health physics information could include or be limited to measurements of the external exposure
rate, gross alpha surface contamination, beta/gamma surface contamination, and/or air
concentration. Radionuclide-specific correction factors as well as radionuclide-specific and
respiratory protection-specific tables are provided. A detailed discussion about these stay time
tables is provided in Chapter 3.

 For an IND incident, three options are available in Group A for calculating stay times and
dose: (1) when there is no dose rate measurement available and only the yield of the nuclear
bomb is known, (2) when a radiological dose rate value at one measurement time is available,
and (3) when dose rates at two measurement times are available. The code makes different
assumptions for different options to calculate the dose. A detailed discussion of Group A is
provided in Chapter 3.

1.2.2 Group B: Early-Phase Protective Action (Evacuation or Sheltering)

 These operational guidelines and dose calculation methods are designed to help decision
makers make timely protective action decisions, such as evacuation or sheltering of the general
public in the early response phase (i.e., the first four days following the incident). Group B
operational guidelines are typically expressed as limiting concentrations of radioactivity in
ground surface soil. Group B dose calculations and operational guidelines are discussed in
Chapter 4.

1.2.3 Group C: Relocation from Different Areas and Critical Infrastructure Utilization in
 Relocation Areas

 These operational guidelines and dose calculation methods for Group C are intended for
early- to intermediate-phase protective actions. They are designed for use in deciding whether to
relocate the public from affected areas for a protracted period of time. Screening values are
provided to delineate areas that exceed the relocation PAGs. These areas include residential
areas, commercial/industrial areas, and other areas such as parks, cemeteries, and monuments.
Group C operational guidelines also ensure that facilities critical to the public welfare can
continue to operate, if needed. These facilities include hospitals, airports, railroads and ports,
water and sewer facilities, and power and fuel facilities. These operational guidelines are
typically expressed as soil or street-surface contamination concentrations (e.g., pCi/m2). Group C
dose calculations and operational guidelines are discussed in Chapters 5 and 6.

 7

1.2.4 Group D: Temporary Access to Relocation Areas for Essential Activities

 Group D operational guidelines and dose calculations pertain to intermediate-phase
protective actions. They are designed to assist in determining the protective action decisions or
restrictions necessary to allow for temporary access to relocation areas. For example, the public
or owners/employees of businesses may need temporary access to residences or commercial,
agricultural, or industrial facilities in order to retrieve essential records, conduct maintenance to
protect facilities, prevent environmental damage, attend to animals, or retrieve pets. These
operational guidelines describe the level at which these actions can be taken without radiological
supervision. The public or employees may occasionally access (i.e., a few days per month) areas
that do not exceed these guidelines. Temporary access to relocation areas that exceed these levels
should be permitted only under the supervision or with the permission of radiation protection
personnel. Examples of these operational guidelines include worker access to businesses for
essential actions and public access to residences for retrieval of critical property, pets, or records.
They are typically expressed in terms of stay times (e.g., minutes or hours) during which the
public or employees may access the areas without receiving the prescribed dose. Group D
operational guidelines and dose calculations are discussed in Chapter 7. Note that this stay time
calculation method is referenced in the EPA PAG Manual for reentry protective action
recommendations (See Table 1.2).

1.2.5 Group E: Transportation and Access Routes

 The operational guidelines and dose calculation methods for Group E apply to
intermediate-phase protective actions. They are designed to assist in determining whether
transportation routes (e.g., bridges, highways, streets) or access ways (e.g., sidewalks and
walkways) may be used by the public for general, limited, or restricted use. The relocation PAGs
serve as the basis for comparison with the calculated dose or the operational guidelines for
general access. For example, operational guidelines may be defined for industrial or commercial
use of various roads, bridges, or access ways. These may be necessary to allow for access
between non-relocation areas via a highway that passes through a relocation area or for access to
emergency recovery activities in the immediate area of an incident. These dose calculations and
operational guidelines assume regular or periodic use and are not appropriate for one-time
events, such as evacuation or relocation actions. They are typically expressed as surface
contamination concentrations (e.g., pCi/m2). Group E operational guidelines and dose
calculations are discussed in Chapter 8.

1.2.6 Group F: Release of Property from Radiologically Controlled Areas

 Group F operational guidelines are intended for intermediate to long-term recovery-phase
protective actions. During response and recovery operations, property and wastes (e.g., vehicles,
equipment, debris and nonradiological wastes, hazardous waste, buildings and land, etc.) must be
cleared from radiologically controlled areas (relocation areas). These operational guidelines
support such actions. Because retrieval of cleared or released properties will be difficult,
wherever practicable, these levels should be similar to those likely to define late-phase goals. For
this reason, they should not be applied to property that will remain in use in controlled areas.

 8

These operational guidelines should also be used for screening property that was located outside
the controlled area. In general, the operational guidelines in this group provide reasonable
assurance that the cleared property is acceptable for long-term unrestricted use (or designated
disposition, in the case of wastes) without further reassessment. Property includes personal
property (Subgroup F-1), debris and waste (Subgroup F-2), hazardous waste (Subgroup F-3), and
real property (Subgroup F-4). The operational guidelines for Subgroup F-4 (release of buildings
and lands) are discussed in Chapter 9. The operational guidelines for Subgroup F-1 (release of
contaminated vehicles) are discussed in Chapter 12. Subgroups F-2 (release of debris and
nonhazardous waste) and F-3 (release of hazardous waste) are outside the scope of RESRAD-
RDD&IND.

 It is noteworthy to highlight that, in particular, operational guidelines for real property
(Subgroup F-4, buildings and lands) are designed to support implementation of the risk
management framework, as presented in the PAGs for RDD and IND incidents (73 FR 149, DHS
2008). The CMS risk management framework for addressing long-term cleanup and recovery
incorporates the principles of optimization, stakeholder involvement, and shared accountability.
Subgroup F-4 guidelines are unique in that there is no one specific predefined numerical criterion
(e.g., expressed in terms of dose or risk) on which to base protective action decisions. These
guidelines are intended to be applied in the optimization process, which will likely consider the
magnitude and extent of the contamination and the radionuclide(s) involved, proposed long-term
land and building use in the affected areas, degree of need for expedited recovery, public welfare
issues, cost impacts for each proposed cleanup option, ecological considerations, and other
factors. Group F-4 operational guidelines are provided as reference values (e.g., soil and building
surface concentrations or risks) that can be used as a starting point for evaluating optimization
process attributes and impacts relative to a range of dose- or risk-based benchmarks (e.g., 500,
100, 25, or 4 millirem per year; comparative lifetime risk ranges, and others) that could be
considered as part of recovery phase options. Thus, they are not regulatory dose limits or criteria,
but serve as concentration values that provide perspective and aid in the optimization analyses.

1.2.7 Group G: Food Consumption

 Group G operational guidelines apply to the early through long-term recovery phase
protective actions, as needed. They are designed to aid in decision making about the need for
placing restrictions on consumption of potentially contaminated foods or on agricultural
activities during and following an RDD or IND incident.

 Four subgroups were included in Group G, which are intended for use in conjunction
with other groups. Subgroup G-1 pertains to the early response phase immediately after the RDD
or IND incident. Subgroup G-1 results can be used to screen against measured concentrations
taken from previously harvested food or from animal products exposed during the incident.
Subgroup G-1 results can also be used to determine the need for placing an embargo and
consumption restrictions on contaminated foods. Subgroup G-2 also applies to the early phase of
response, but it is intended for use in evaluating crops or animal products exposed during the
RDD or IND incident (e.g., after the plume has passed). Subgroup G-2 results serve as a
comparison with measured concentrations taken from surface soil in which plant foods and
fodder had been growing during the incident. Subgroups G-3 and G-4 are intended for the

 9

intermediate to long-term recovery phases and can be used for placing land use restrictions on
agricultural activities after an RDD or IND incident. They can be used to determine if crops can
be grown on residually contaminated soil to produce a harvest that would be acceptable for
public consumption. Group G is discussed in detail in Chapter 10.

1.2.8 Group H: Water Consumption

 Group H operational guidelines apply to the early through long-term recovery phase
protective actions. They are designed to aid in decision making about the need for placing
restrictions on consumption of potentially contaminated drinking water during and following an
RDD or IND incident. Water consumption operational guidelines are developed for both the
general public and the sensitive population groups (e.g., infants, children, pregnant women, and
nursing women). Group H results are intended for use in conjunction with other groups. Group H
is discussed in detail in Chapter 11.

1.3 ADDITIONAL EXPOSURE SCENARIOS AND OPERATIONAL GUIDELINES

 Chapter 12 presents additional RDD and IND incident scenarios that were analyzed to
support the derivation of the operational guideline groups and subgroups described in
Section 1.2. Two of these additional scenarios involve the use of water to flush streets and clean
vehicles. Accordingly, operational guidelines for street flushing and cleaning contaminated
vehicles are provided.

1.4 MODELING SCENARIOS, CALCULATION OF TIME INTEGRATED DOSE,
 AND DERIVATION OF OPERATIONAL GUIDELINES

 The modeling scenarios and dose calculation methods used in evaluation of RDD and
IND incidents are essentially the same for the intermediate and late phases of response
(i.e., Groups B through H). The methodology for Group A is different for RDD and IND
incidents. The similarities and differences in dose modeling for RDD and IND incidents are
summarized in the following two sections.

1.4.1 RDD Incidents

 The core methodology and modeling employed in deriving the operational guidelines
were developed through a consensus-based process that involved all Operational Guidelines
Task (OGT) Group members (DOE 2009). Operational guidelines (Groups A–H), which
correspond to specific PAGs, were derived for 11 potential RDD radionuclides: Am-241,
Cf-252, Cm-244, Co-60, Cs-137, Ir-192, Po-210, Pu-238, Pu-239, Ra-226, and Sr-90. These
radionuclides were determined by a joint DOE and NRC study to be the most likely sources
available for potential terrorist use in an RDD (DOE/NRC 2003). For the early response phase
(Group A), a straightforward concentration method or an exposure-to-dose conversion method
was used to derive stay time tables. For the late response phase (Group F), the probabilistic
RESRAD-ONSITE and RESRAD-BUILD programs (Yu et al. 2001, 2022) were used to derive

 10

property release criteria. The concepts and overarching methodology used to derive operational
guidelines for RDD-related radionuclides could also be generally applied, with modifications, to
radionuclides associated with an IND.

 The methodology developed for the intermediate response phase (Groups B–E) assumes
that an outdoor RDD event would contaminate streets, soils, and building surfaces with
radioactive materials. Building interiors would also be contaminated as a result of air exchange
and human traffic. The methodology can be modified for an indoor RDD event by considering
different source partitioning factors. The model considers decay, weathering, a time-dependent
resuspension factor, and concentration ratios (partitioning factors) between contaminated areas.
A set of exposure pathways was considered in the model: external exposure from contaminants
on streets, soils, and buildings; inhalation of dust particles; external exposure from submersion;
ingestion of dust particles and plant foods; and indoor radon inhalation. Operational guidelines
for each group were derived by using a systematic approach in which (1) applicable scenarios for
each group were defined, (2) appropriate receptors for each scenario were identified, and (3) the
receptor doses from applicable exposure pathways were estimated. A detailed discussion of the
specific methodologies and approaches used to calculate time-integrated dose over the exposure
duration and to derive the operational guidelines is provided in Chapter 2. For Group H, the
measured radionuclide concentrations in drinking water is used to calculate dose. For the late
response phase (Group F), examples of release criteria for soils and buildings were derived using
the probabilistic RESRAD-ONSITE and RESRAD-BUILD codes (Yu et al. 2001, 2022) Details
of example operational guidelines for release of soils and buildings are discussed in Chapter 9.

1.4.2 IND Incidents

 The default source term in the RESRAD-RDD&IND code for an IND incident is based
on a 10 kiloton (kt) yield uranium-fueled nuclear detonation (Kraus and Foster 2013). For the
early response phase (Group A), only the direct external exposure pathway is considered, and it
is assumed that the dose rate or exposure rate follows an empirical power function behavior with
time as approximated by Equation 9.147 in Glasstone and Dolan’s 1977 report (Glasstone and
Dolan 1977).

 For Groups B–E and for Group G in an IND incident/event, there are two options to
specify ground concentrations based on: (1) the information provided in Group A for yield or
dose rate measurements and (2) measured radionuclide concentrations on the ground. The dose
calculation methodology for Groups B–H for an IND incident is essentially the same as for an
RDD incident. For both RDD and IND incidents, decay and ingrowth are considered. However,
for an RDD incident, it is assumed that short-lived progeny with a half-life less than 30 days are
in secular equilibrium with parent radionuclides.

1.5 COMPANION SOFTWARE TOOL FOR FACILITATING IMPLEMENTATION
 OF OPERATIONAL GUIDELINES

 A companion software tool, RESRAD-RDD&IND, was developed to allow calculation of
incident-specific operational guidelines through application of user-specified assumptions and
environmental transfer parameters that correspond to exposure pathways for incident-specific

 11

analyses for an RDD or IND event. The software tool uses elements of other codes already
within the RESRAD family of codes, along with newly derived models and supporting
parameters specific to the RDD and IND scenarios used to calculate time-integrated dose over
the exposure duration and determine the operational guidelines. The additional scenarios
discussed in Chapter 12 are not implemented in RESRAD-RDD&IND Version 2.0. A detailed
overview of the RESRAD-RDD&IND software tool and a general user’s guide are provided in
Volume 2 of the RESRAD-RDD&IND User’s Manual (Kamboj, S., et al. 2026).

 14

 2 METHODOLOGY FOR DOSE CALCULATION AND OPERATIONAL
 GUIDELINES DERIVATION

2.1 INTRODUCTION

 This chapter describes the model used to assess radiation doses to various receptors after
an RDD or IND event and discusses the assumptions involved in the application of this model.
The model described in this chapter is mainly for calculating dose and deriving operational
guidelines for Groups B−E (Chapters 4−8) in the early and intermediate response phases. The
methodology for Group A early phase response is presented in Chapter 3. To derive operation
guidelines for Group F (release of real property from a radiologically controlled area) in the late
recovery phase, the probabilistic RESRAD-ONSITE code (Yu et al. 2001) was used for soil
contamination, and the probabilistic RESRAD-BUILD code (Yu et al. 2022) was used for
building contamination. The probabilistic assessments were employed in part because of the
relatively large uncertainties (e.g., for assumptions and parameter values) used in deriving the
initial set of operational guidelines for Group F. These operational guidelines are intended for
use as a starting point for consideration within the optimization process. Deterministic
approaches using RESRAD-ONSITE or RESRAD-BUILD codes (or other models) can also be
used, particularly when more detailed site- and incident-specific data are available. A detailed
example of Subgroup F-4 is presented in Chapter 9. For Group G food consumption and
Group H water consumption, the operational guidelines cover all three phases of response, i.e.,
the early, intermediate, and late recovery phases. The methodologies for Group G and Group H
are presented in Chapters 10 and 11, respectively.

 The conceptual model presented below is mainly applicable to situations after the plume
phase is over. It was assumed that surface concentrations of dispersed radionuclides were known
(i.e., measured). The model was designed for flexibility and ease of application under a variety
of conditions. It was developed specifically for dose calculations and the derivation of
operational guidelines and/or stay times for Groups B−E and some subgroups of Group G and
Group H.

 The model assumes that an RDD or IND event occurred outdoors and contaminated
streets and soils, exterior walls and roofs of buildings, and interior walls and floors of buildings
with radioactive materials. The model relates the initial concentrations (via the use of
partitioning factors) between streets/soils and other contaminated areas, accounts for changes in
surface concentration over time (includes decay and weathering), and uses a time-dependent
resuspension factor to calculate air concentration. By using appropriate partitioning factors, the
model can also be used for an RDD or IND event occurring inside a building. For dose
calculations for an RDD event, the implementation of decay and ingrowth, weathering, and
resuspension as a function of time starts at the measurement time, which is assumed to
correspond to the time plume deposition is complete. For dose calculations for an IND event, the
implementation of decay and ingrowth, weathering, and resuspension as a function of time starts
from 1 hour after detonation or at the measurement time.

 To calculate dose and derive operational guidelines for each group (Groups B−H),
different receptors were considered. The doses from different exposure pathways and

 15

contaminated areas were added to arrive at the total dose for an individual receptor. For the
direct external radiation and radon pathways, the pre-calculated unit dose factors (dose per unit
concentration per year) for exposure to different contaminated structures/surfaces, which were
generated with the RESRAD-BUILD code, are scaled with the exposure time and concentration
to estimate the potential radiation doses. The dose for the maximally exposed receptor was used
to derive the operational guideline for a particular group.

 When the RDD option is selected in the RESRAD-RDD&IND code, operational
guidelines (radionuclide concentrations or stay times) are derived for the 11 RDD radionuclides:
Am-241, Cf-252, Cm-244, Co-60, Cs-137, Ir-192, Po-210, Pu-238, Pu-239, Ra-226, and Sr-90.
When the IND option is selected in the RESRAD-RDD&IND code, only dose calculations are
performed. To evaluate radiation doses, contributions from decay progenies are calculated. For
RDD radionuclides, it is assumed that short-lived progenies with half-lives <30 days are in
equilibrium with the parent nuclide, i.e., they have the same radioactivity concentrations as the
parent. For example, Y-90 is assumed to be in equilibrium with its parent nuclide Sr-90. For
long-lived progenies with half-lives >30 days, their activity concentrations over the exposure
periods are modeled and used to calculate their dose contributions. For IND radionuclides, no
cut-off half-life is used for progeny, i.e., the activity concentration of each progeny in the decay
chain is modeled and used to calculate its dose contribution.

 For Group B, which corresponds to operational guidelines for early phase protective
actions (evacuation or sheltering), the PAGs are 1 rem (for the general public) or 5 rem (for early
response workers). The early phase was defined as the first four days following the RDD or IND
event. For Groups C−E, the PAG for occupational exposure is 5 rem/yr. For the general public,
the PAGs are 2 rem/yr for the first year and 0.5 rem/yr for subsequent years.

2.2 CONCEPTUAL MODEL

 The conceptual model of radiological contaminant transport following an RDD or IND
event is illustrated in Figure 2.1 which applies to Groups B–E as well as Group G. It was
assumed that the event resulted in surface contamination on the streets (in an urban/suburban
environment) or soil (in a rural environment) and on the exterior walls and roofs of
residential/commercial buildings. In addition, the contaminants were assumed to get inside the
residential/commercial buildings and be deposited on indoor floors and walls, either directly
during the event through open windows or indirectly after the event through indoor/outdoor air
exchange or through contaminants trapped on the bottoms or surfaces of shoes of people who
entered the building.

 The model accepts input surface concentration ratios (partitioning factors) between the
outdoor walls, roof, indoor floor, and indoor walls, respectively, and the soil/street. If the surface
concentration ratios are not known, the model calculates dose values using the default relative
ratios of 0.5, 1.0, 0.1, and 0.05 for the surface concentrations for outdoor walls, roof, indoor
floor, and indoor walls with respect to the soil/street. These default concentration ratios are
selected to yield reasonably conservative doses from other surfaces. For example, the indoor
floor and outdoor street/soil concentration ratio of 10% assumes that outdoor contamination was
tracked indoors by shoes and that windows were open when the outdoor RDD or IND event

 16

occurred. The indoor wall concentration was assumed to be half (factor of 0.5) the concentration
of the floor contamination, which is consistent with indoor deposition patterns (Raunemaa et al.
1989). The outdoor wall concentration was also assumed to be a factor of 0.5 of the soil/street
contamination. The concentrations on outdoor walls actually could be much lower than the
soil/street contamination level (Andersson et al. 2003). If data are available on the concentration
ratios for various surfaces, these data can be input into RESRAD-RDD&IND for site-specific
and incident-specific analysis.

 Figure 2.1 Conceptual Model of Environmental Transport after an RDD Event

 At a minimum, soil/street surface concentration data should be available for use with this
model. If data become available for other surfaces or surface measurements indicate other
partitions than the default assumptions, these other values can be used to obtain specific
guidelines.

 The surface contamination results in external exposure and/or internal exposure through
inadvertent ingestion of contaminated dust particles to a receptor. The external dose to a receptor
is corrected by a factor that accounts for radioactive decay, ingrowth of progeny, and weathering.
In accordance with the specific scenarios developed for various operational groups,
commercial/residential buildings with different dimensions were used for the external dose
calculations.

 17

 As shown in Figure 2.1, the secondary contamination sources include the outdoor air
resulting from resuspension of deposited radionuclides on the outdoor ground surface. To
estimate the outdoor air concentrations, a time-dependent resuspension factor, a weathering
correction factor for street/soil, and a radioactive decay and ingrowth factor are applied to
multiply with the initial activity concentration on the ground surface. In some cases, the
resuspension factor was adjusted to account for vehicular traffic. The resulting outdoor air
concentration is used to calculate the outdoor inhalation and submersion dose rates.

 Another secondary contamination source shown in Figure 2.1 is the indoor air
contamination. Two components contribute to the calculated indoor air concentrations. The first
one comes from the contaminated outdoor air, and the second one comes from the resuspension
of contaminated material deposited on the interior floors. The indoor dust filtration factor
represents the fraction of outdoor contaminated dust that is available indoors. A distribution
function was developed for the indoor dust filtration factor (Yu et al. 2000). In the default case,
the median value (0.55) of this distribution was assumed for the ratio of indoor to outdoor air
concentration. The indoor resuspension factor was assumed to be constant at 10-6 m-1 (IAEA
2002; NRC 2001). The calculated contaminated indoor air concentration is then used to compute
the indoor inhalation and submersion doses. Plants also may get contaminated from foliar
deposition and root uptake, but the dose contribution from the plant ingestion pathway is not
included in Groups B−E. Radiation dose from the plant ingestion pathway is evaluated in
Groups F-4 and G.

2.3 DEFAULT SOURCE TERM AND SOURCE TERM SPECIFICATION FOR AN
 IND EVENT

 The IND component of the RESRAD-RDD&IND code addresses radionuclides
associated with uranium-fueled nuclear detonation. The default radionuclide concentrations and
dose rates (measured at 10 km downwind) are based on the areal depositions near the plume
centerline at 10 km downwind at 1 hour after a 10 kt yield uranium-fueled nuclear detonation on
a concrete/asphalt mix surface. Table 2.1 lists the default initial deposited ground concentrations
of the radionuclides in RESRAD-RDD&IND code for an IND event; the data were taken from
Table 5 of the Sandia 2013 report (Kraus and Foster 2013). Table 2.2 provides decay
characteristics of the 44 initially present radionuclides.

 According to the investigation in a 2013 Sandia report (Kraus and Foster 2013), the
default source term of 44 initially present radionuclides (Table 2.1) in the RESRAD-RDD&IND
code for an IND event produced greater than 92% of the total effective dose from the entire
fallout inventory received by a receptor located 10 km downwind over each of the 11 dose
integration time periods (times phases) considered in the investigation. The 11 time phases
considered were: (1) 0–24 hours, (2) 0–96 hours, (3) 0–365 days, (4) 6–30 hours,
(5) 6–102 hours, (6) 12–36 hours, (7) 12–108 hours, (8) 24–48 hours, (9) 24–120 hours,
(10) 1–366 days, and (11) 366–731 days.

 In the IND component of RESRAD-RDD&IND for Groups B–E and Group G, there are
two options for specifying initial ground concentrations: directly, by specifying the
concentrations at a measurement time, or indirectly, by specifying either the yield of the
detonation or a measurement time and the dose rate at that time.

 In the indirect option (Option 1), the default concentrations (C10kt,i) of different
radionuclides (i) and the total external dose rate (DR10kt) listed in Groups B–E and G are based
on the deposited concentrations of significant contributing radionuclides at measurement time =
1 hour after a uranium-fueled ground detonation that has a 10 kt yield (Table 2.1). The total
external dose rate for a 10 kt yield is 1.8 Sv/hr (1.8E5 mrem/hr). If the yield of the detonation is
changed in Group A, the ground concentrations of the radionuclides and the total external dose
rate listed in Groups B–E and G at a measurement time of 1 hour change linearly with the yield.

 Option 2 becomes available when the “Edit Table” box is checked. In this option, the
user can input the measured ground concentrations for specific individual radionuclides. For this
option, the exposure begin time cannot be less than the measurement time. For dose modeling,
the input measured concentrations are used as the initial concentrations and the implementation
of decay and ingrowth, weathering, and resuspension as a function of time starts from the
measurement time.

2.4 EXPOSURE PATHWAYS

 The model for Groups B–E considers the following 13 exposure pathways for a receptor:

 1. External exposure (groundshine) to contaminants on streets/soils while staying
 outdoors,

 2. External exposure to contaminants on exterior walls while staying indoors,

 3. External exposure to contaminants on roofs while staying indoors,

 4. External exposure to contaminants on interior walls while staying indoors,

 5. External exposure to contaminants on interior floors while staying indoors,

 6. External exposure to contaminants on streets/soils while staying indoors,

 7. Inhalation exposure while staying outdoors (resuspension of contaminants from
 streets/soils only),

 8. Inhalation exposure while staying indoors (indoor air contamination results from both
 outdoor air contamination and resuspension from contaminants on interior floors),

 9. Submersion in contaminated air while staying outdoors,

 10. Submersion in contaminated air while staying indoors,

 11. Ingestion of dust particles on streets/soils while staying outdoors,

 12. Ingestion of dust particles while staying indoors (assumed to be from the floors or
 walls, whichever is more conservative), and

 13. Radon inhalation while staying indoors.

 The outdoor radon concentration is expected to be much smaller than the indoor radon
concentration because of the large dilution in an open environment. Therefore, the radiation dose
from radon inhalation while staying outdoors is not included in the model.

 The radiation doses are calculated and operational guidelines are derived based on
specific exposure scenarios developed by considering the needs of each group. To obtain
incident-specific dose or operational guidelines for Groups B–E, the RESRAD-RDD&IND user

 22

may input measured concentrations and adjust transfer factors as that information becomes
available.

2.5 SENSITIVE PARAMETERS

 The radiation doses are calculated and/or generic guidelines are developed by applying
several assumptions and the corresponding parameter values. The common and most sensitive
scenario parameters are as follows:

 Outdoor and indoor resuspension factors,

 Multiplication factor for outdoor resuspension to account for vehicular traffic,

 Dust ingestion rate,

 Indoor/outdoor exposure duration,

 Indoor/outdoor inhalation rates, and

 Building dimensions for external exposure.

 The scenario-specific parameters used to calculate radiation doses and/or to derive the
operational guidelines for each group (Groups B−E) are discussed in the subsequent chapters for
each group.

2.6 WEATHERING CORRECTION

 Surface contamination would result in external exposure, inadvertent ingestion of
contaminated dust particles deposited on surfaces, and inhalation of contaminated air resulting
from resuspension of dust particulates from contaminated surfaces.

 It was observed from the Chernobyl accident data that in an urban environment, different
surfaces have different initial retentions when compared with the reference surface and that they
behave differently over time (Andersson et al. 2003). The reference surface was defined as an
infinite smooth (surface with no roughness associated with it) air-ground interface with
radionuclides deposited on the ground with no initial penetration. According to the Andersson et
al. (2003) study, the weathering process and the effect of migration generally follow a two-class
exponential behavior with time, as represented by Equation 2.1:

                                 w(t ) = a e − ( bt ) + (1 − a) e − ( ct ) ,                   (2.1)

where w(t) is the activity fraction retained after weathering at time t, and a, b, and c are
parameters for each surface. In this equation, there is a mobile fraction “a” with a shorter half-
life (ln 2/b) due to loose binding to the surface or due to the higher migration rate in the case of
permeable surfaces. There is also a fixed fraction “(1 − a)” with a longer half-life (ln 2/c) due to
strong binding to the surface or a lower migration rate. Table 2.3 summarizes the values for these

 23

parameters for various media. The data were collected primarily for Cs-137 but are used as
defaults for other radionuclides in the RESRAD-RDD&IND analysis. However, the user may
input radionuclide-specific values if data are available.

 Table 2.3 Weathering and Migration of Contaminants
 for Urban Surfaces

 Mobile Shorter Half- Longer Half-
 Surface Fraction (a) life (ln 2/b), yr life (ln 2/c), yr

                      Windows                0.8               0.2                 2
 Vertical walls 0.2 0.2 20
                      Roofs with tiles       0.5               1−4               25−50
                      Paved areas            0.5               0.2                 2
                      Trees                  0.8               0.2                 2
                      Lawn                  0.46               1.5                 50
 Source: Andersson et al. (2003).

 The weathering correction factors reported by Andersson et al. (2003) are for outdoor
surfaces. In RESRAD-RDD&IND, the weathering correction factors for the outdoor soil/street
are also applied to the interior floor, and those for exterior walls are also applied to interior walls,
based on the consideration that the concentrations in interior surfaces are related to the
concentrations in exterior surfaces due to the transport of contaminants from outdoors to indoors
by human activities (e.g., walking) and air exchange.

 Time-dependent radionuclide concentrations in each contaminated surface during the
exposure are used to calculate radiation doses. The modeling of time-dependent concentrations
accounts for radioactive decay, ingrowth, and weathering.

2.7 RESUSPENSION FACTOR CORRECTION

 The resuspension factor is used to calculate the outdoor air concentration based on the
ground surface concentration. The resuspension of ground surface contaminants is considered to
be enhanced by vehicular traffic in an urban environment (Walsh 2002), resulting in higher air
concentrations than without vehicular traffic. Under such conditions, a multiplication factor of
10 is used as the default in RESRAD-RDD&IND.

 The base resuspension factor, 𝐾𝑡 (𝑚−1 ), described by Equation 2.2, and the
multiplication factor are used to multiply the ground surface concentration to obtain the outdoor
air concentration used for determining radiation dose.
 −7 × 𝑡 −8 × 𝑡
             𝐾𝑡 = 1 × 10−5 × 𝑒 −8.1×10              + 7 × 10−9 × 𝑒 −2.31×10                 + 1 × 10−9   (2.2)

where t (sec) is the elapsed time after initial deposition. The resuspension factor (𝐾𝑡 ) presented in
Eq. 2.2 is from Maxwell and Anspaugh (2011). It is the sum of a constant (10-9/m) and two

 24

exponential decays in time with amplitudes of 10-5/m and 7 × 10-9/m and decay constants of
1/(14.3 days) and 1/(500 days).

 A list of “rule of thumb” multiplication factors is given by Walsh (2002) and can be
referenced to adjust for resuspended air concentrations resulting from variations in traffic and
atmospheric conditions (Table 2.4).

 The indoor air concentration is the sum of two components: one from resuspension of
interior surface contamination and the other from air exchange with outdoor contaminated air.
For the second component, the outdoor air concentration is multiplied by the “indoor dust
filtration factor” to obtain an indoor air concentration. For the first component, the indoor
resuspension factor is assumed to stay constant at 10-6 m-1 (IAEA 2002; NRC 2001).

 Table 2.4 Multiplication Factors to Be Applied to the
 Resuspension Factor Results

 Multiplication
 Condition Factor

 Rural conditions, light-medium winds 1
 Arid climate 10
 Urban conditions, light traffic, light pedestrian activitya 10
 Urban conditions, heavy traffic 100
 Plowing in dry conditions 100
 a
 Given that some countermeasures might restrict the movement of
 vehicles, this factor need not always be applied.

2.8 DOSE CALCULATION

 It is assumed for Groups B–E that a receptor could be exposed to radiation via up to
13 exposure pathways, as described in Section 2.3. To calculate radiation doses, first, the dose-
to-source ratios (DSRs) for each exposure pathway resulting from different contaminated media
(street/soil, building roof, exterior walls of the building, interior floor of the building, and interior
walls of the building) are calculated by using the relationships discussed previously among the
media and each receptor in a group/subgroup. The doses from relevant pathways are then added
to obtain the total dose for each receptor.

 The dose conversion factors (DCFs), also referred as the dose coefficients (DCs), are
used in calculating radiation doses. Options available in RESRAD-RDD&IND to choose the
DCF database are contingent on the radionuclide database selected. There are two radionuclides
databases, i.e., the International Commission on Radiological Protection’s Publication 38
(ICRP-38, ICRP 1983) and ICRP’s Publication 107 (ICRP-107, ICRP 2008). If the ICRP-107
radionuclide database is selected, the options for DCFs are “DCFPAK302” and “DOE-STD-
1196-2022.” The DCFPAK302 database contains both internal DCFs and external DCFs,
whereas the DOE-STD-1196-2022 (DOE 2022) database contains only internal DCFs. When the
“DOE-STD-1196-2022” option is selected, the FGR 15 (EPA 2025) database is accessed to

 25

provide the external DCFs. If the “ICRP-38” (ICRP 1983) radionuclide database is selected, the
ICRP-72 (ICRP 1996) DCF database is accessed to get the internal DCFs, which are used with
the external DCFs from the FGR 12 (Eckerman and Ryman 1993) database.
 The DCs in the FGR 15 (EPA 2025) database were developed based on the ICRP-103
(ICRP 2007) methodology. The database contains per capita external effective dose rate
coefficients. The DCs in the DOE-STD-1196-2022 (DOE 2022) database were also developed
based on the ICRP-103 (ICRP 2007) methodology; the DCs database contains per capita
committed effective dose coefficients for inhalation and ingestion and per capita air submersion
effective dose rate coefficients. The DCFs in both DCFPAK302 and ICRP-72 (ICRP 1996)
databases were developed based on the ICRP-60 methodology (ICRP 1991). Both DCFPAK302
and ICRP-72 databases contain DCFs for different age groups; the age-dependent DCFs are used
in Group G to derive concentration limits in food, while the adult DCFs are used in dose
calculations in the other groups.

2.8.1 External Pathway Dose

 The RESRAD-BUILD computer code (Yu et al. 2022) was used to calculate the external
exposure pathway dose factors (DFs) for all RDD and IND radionuclides (including the
11 initially present RDD radionuclides, the 44 initially present IND radionuclides, and the decay
progeny of the initially present RDD and IND radionuclides).
 For calculating the outside external pathway dose (pathway 1),3 a 10,000-m2
homogenously contaminated surface area is assumed. The outdoor receptor is assumed to be at
the center of the contaminated area. The external pathway dose calculation also includes a
correction for surface roughness; a correction factor of 0.82, based on the default value of
TurboFRMAC 2.0, was used.

 For the indoor external pathway doses (pathways 2−5), the indoor receptor is assumed to
be located at the center of the contaminated building and is exposed to contamination on the
floor, indoor walls, outdoor walls, and outdoor roof. The RESRAD-BUILD computer code is
used to calculate the external dose factors (Yu et al. 2022), which take into account source
configuration, receptor distance from the source, and shielding between the source and the
receptor. In accordance with the specific scenarios developed for various groups, different
dimensions are assumed for the commercial/residential buildings where the receptor is located.
Table 2.5 lists different dimensions (geometries) assumed to calculate the external dose from
different contaminated media (roof, exterior walls, interior walls, and interior floors).

3
 Pathway numbers correspond to the list of potential exposure pathways given in Section 2.3.

 The external dose from outside ground contamination (pathway 6) for an indoor receptor
is calculated using the dose factor from contaminated streets/soil (pathway 1) corrected by a
shielding factor. The Federal Radiological Monitoring and Assessment Center (FRMAC)
provides values of the shielding factor for different types of structures (Rhodes et al. 2003b;
Hunt et al. 2023). The values vary from 0.4 (one- and two-story wood frame house) to 0.005
(in the basement of a multistory structure). Table 2.5 lists the shielding factors used in different
exposure configurations.

 The dose rate from each of the external exposure pathways (pathways 1–6) is calculated
by using Equation 2.3. The dose is calculated for each radionuclide in the decay chain, including
the parent radionuclide (Section 2.8.6). The receptor could spend some time outdoors and some
time indoors inside the contaminated building and could be exposed to all contaminated media.
The total external pathway dose rate to the receptor is the sum of all external exposures to these
media. The total external dose is calculated by integrating the external pathway dose rates over
the exposure duration (Section 2.8.7).

2.8.5 Radon Inhalation Pathway Dose

 The dose from the radon inhalation pathway (pathway 13) is calculated only for indoor
exposure from Ra-226. The calculation uses the radon inhalation dose-to-source ratios (RDSRs)
which were pre-calculated with the RESRAD-BUILD code (Yu et al. 2022). To calculate the
RDSRs with RESRAD-BUILD, the radon emanation fraction is set at 0.1. The RDSR (mrem/yr
per pCi of radium on interior surfaces) was calculated for each of the different building
geometries and the air exchange rates assumed in RESRAD-RDD&IND. The assumed building
geometries and air exchange rates are listed in Table 2.5. The radon pathway dose rate for an
indoor receptor can be calculated with Equation 2.11. First, the total radium (Ra-226) activity at
time t, corrected for weathering, is calculated for a given building. The total activity is then
multiplied by the corresponding RDSR and exposure factor to get the radon inhalation pathway
dose rate:

 Equation 2.14 is used to derive the concentration guideline for the operational group:

where PAG is the protective action guide (rem or mSv) and p is the index for each pathway, as
discussed in Sections 2.4 and 2.8.

 Unless otherwise stated, the operational guidelines are derived as the limiting surface
activity concentration of each parent nuclide initially deposited on soil or streets (pCi/m2).
Depending on the analyzed scenario within the operational group, other guidelines may be

 33

expressed as the volume concentration (pCi/g), as for Group F, or in stay times (the maximum
time a person could remain within the PAG, given specific radionuclide concentrations in a
contaminated media) as for Groups A and D.

2.10 Receptor Scenarios and Default Exposure Parameter Values

 To provide assistance in the decision making of a protective action, a systematic
approach was applied to design the RESRAD-RDD&IND code to evaluate the level of potential
radiation exposure that could be encountered at different phases after an RDD or IND event.
First, scenarios applicable to each group were defined, with appropriate receptors
(workers/public as applicable) identified for each group. Then, for each receptor, doses from all
applicable exposure pathways were computed in accordance with the methodology described in
Sections 2.8.1–2.8.7. Finally, the pathway doses were added to get the total dose for each
individual receptor. The dose of the critical receptor (the receptor [worker or public as
applicable] that gives the highest total dose) in a group can then be compared with the applicable
protective action guide (PAG) to define protective action (EPA 2019).

 Table 2.6 provides brief descriptions of the receptor scenarios identified for Groups B–E.
Table 2.7 summarizes the exposure pathways considered applicable for each receptor. Table 2.8
lists the exposure parameters and their default values used in the dose calculations for different
scenarios.

 The default inhalation rates listed in Table 2.8 were developed by considering different
activities that may be undertaken by the receptors while staying indoors and outdoors, as
explained in Table 2.9. Corresponding inhalation rates for the different activities were taken
from the ICRP-66 (ICRP 1994) report.

 3 GROUP A: ACCESS CONTROL DURING EMERGENCY
 RESPONSE OPERATIONS

 The dose calculation and operational guideline derivation method for an RDD event is
presented in Section 3.1. Because many short-lived radionuclides are released during an IND
event, a different method is used as presented in Section 3.2.

3.1 GROUP A FOR AN RDD EVENT

 The release of radionuclides in an RDD event could occur inside or outside a building
and could result from different dispersion mechanisms, such as the explosion of a conventional
bomb, spray from an aircraft, or spray from an injection into an air-handling system. Due to the
potential risks that could result from radiation exposures incurred by the first responders who
arrive at the scene, stay times at the event location would need to be limited in order to protect
human health and save lives. Since little information would be known on the type and extent of
radiation contamination shortly after the event, the following objectives were considered when
developing stay times for use in the early response phase (Group A): (1) the stay times are to be
applicable immediately after an RDD event, (2) they are independent of the location and type of
release, and (3) they are simple to implement.

 The stay times for Group A are calculated for controlling access to the RDD event
location during emergency response operations. They can be applied to provide life and property
saving measures and to set emergency worker demarcation.

 In many cases, the first responders may use respirators, which would reduce the dose
from inhalation of airborne particulates. Therefore, stay times are calculated for three different
cases: (1) without a respirator, (2) with a full-face air-purifying respirator, and (3) with a full-
face continuous-flow atmosphere-supplying respirator. The assigned protection factor of 100 and
1,000, respectively, for a full-face air-purifying respirator operating under negative pressure and
a full-face continuous flow atmosphere-supplying respirator are based on the values provided in
Appendix A to 10 CFR Part 20.

 To use the stay time tables, the first responders would need to know either the air
concentration or the ground surface concentration and have obtained data from one of the
following: (1) gross alpha measurements, (2) gross β- measurements, (3)  exposure rate
measurements, or (4) radionuclide-specific measurements.

3.1.1 Scenario Description for an RDD Event

 It was assumed that a large area (i.e., of an infinite extent, either inside or outside a
building) would be contaminated during an RDD event. Even though the distribution of
contamination would most likely be uneven, in order to develop the stay time tables, it was
assumed that the contamination was uniformly distributed. It was further assumed that receptors
would be exposed to contaminated air as well as contaminated material deposited on the ground
surface.

 45

 The first responders were assumed to stay in the event location continuously searching
for evidence and conducting investigations. The potential radiation dose would result from
(1) inhalation of airborne radioactive particulates, (2) external exposure due to air submersion,
and (3) external exposure to materials deposited on the ground surface. For the responders that
do not wear respirators, the radiation dose would also result from direct ingestion of
contaminated dust particles.

3.1.2 Parameter Assumptions for an RDD Event

 The airborne radionuclides could be due to remnants of the dispersion triggered by the
RDD event as well as the resuspension of deposited radionuclides disturbed by the first
responders. In the stay time development, it was assumed that the air concentration and ground
deposition were linearly related by a surrogate resuspension factor with a value reflecting the
above two mechanisms for air contamination.

 The following five key parameters along with their default values are used in the
calculations: (1) the resuspension factor is 1  10-6 m-1, which is used in National Council on
Radiation Protection and Measurements (NCRP) Report No. 129 (NCRP 1999; Loosmore 2003)
to simulate exposures of first responders in emergency scenarios; (2) the inhalation rate is
1.4 m3/h, which is the most likely value for outdoor activities, from Section A.3 of Biwer et al.
(2002); (3) the dose coefficients for exposures to contaminated ground surface, air submersion,
inhalation, and ingestion in this chapter, which are taken from DCFPAK302 and are based on the
ICRP-60 methodology (ICRP 1991); and (4) the dust ingestion rate of 3  10-4 m2/day for the
emergency workers without respirators, which is taken from the Federal Radiological Monitoring
and Assessment Center (FRMAC) manual (Rhodes 2003). The user has the option to choose
dose coefficients from other two database libraries, and the code provides results based on the
database library selected. Table 3.1 provides dose coefficients for the contaminants considered in
developing stay time tables. The dose coefficients include the contributions from short-lived
(half-life <30 days) progenies along with the parent radionuclides. For example, Sr-90 dose
coefficients in Table 3.1 include the contribution from the short-lived progeny Y-90.

 Table 3.2 provides the default DSRs for different exposure pathways based on unit air
concentrations without the protection of a respirator. (The surface deposition concentration is
calculated from the air concentration by using a resuspension factor of 1  10-6 m-1.) The DSRs
are used to calculate stay times assuming radionuclide concentrations remain the same over the

 47

exposure period, i.e., the concentrations are not adjusted for decay and ingrowth. It was assumed
that the ground contamination was infinite in extent.

 Although the calculation of the external dose from an infinitely large ground surface is
more representative of an outdoor situation, the dose results are either more conservative than or
comparable with those from contaminated building surfaces representative of an indoor situation,
as verified by the following study.

 For comparison with the external dose for an outdoor situation, the external dose for an
indoor situation was calculated for buildings with different geometries. It was assumed that all of
the interior surfaces of buildings were uniformly contaminated with Co-60 (a high-energy
gamma emitter). The external doses from all six contaminated surfaces (floor, ceiling, and four
walls) were added to get the total external dose. Table 3.3 shows the external dose to a receptor
located at the center of the contaminated floor (this receptor would receive the maximum dose).
The building area was varied from 36 m2 (representative of a small store) to 2,500 m2
(representative of a large warehouse), and the building height was varied from 2.5 to 6 m. In
Table 3.2, the external dose for Co-60 is 3.07E-2 mrem/h for an outdoor situation (with a
deposition concentration of 106 pCi/m2 on an infinitely large ground surface); this converts to an
annual dose of 2.69E-4 mrem/yr per pCi/m2 (7.26E-5 mSv/yr per Bq/m2). As shown in Table 3.3,
for all except one building geometry considered, the external dose is significantly lower than
2.69E-4 mrem/yr per pCi/m2. For the building geometry with a very large floor area and a small
height (area = 2,500 m2 and height = 2.5 m), the total external dose of 2.85E-4 mrem/yr per
pCi/m2 is slightly higher but comparable to 2.69E-4 mrem/yr per pCi/m2. In any case, it is very
unlikely that a receptor would be exposed to external radiation from a large contaminated
building without any shielding from multiple walls inside the building that would reduce the
radiation. On the basis of this study, it is concluded that the external dose from an infinitely large
contaminated surface (listed in Table 3.2) can be used to develop stay times that are protective
for both indoor and outdoor situations.

 Appendix 3.A provides the default stay times for the three different cases: without
protection of a respirator, with the protection of a full-face respirator, and with the protection of a
full-face continuous-flow atmosphere-supplying respirator. If multiple radionuclides are present
and the measured activity concentrations (gross alpha, beta and gamma, or exposure rates) are
the sum from all these radionuclides, the stay time based on the most restrictive radionuclide
should be used to protect the first responders. If the radionuclides are identified and the relative
concentration ratios among the radionuclides are known, the sum of fractions rule, i.e., the sum
of the ratios between the final stay time and the stay times of the individual radionuclides is less
than or equal to 1, should be used in determining the stay times.

3.2 GROUP A FOR AN IND EVENT

 The current version of the RESRAD-RDD&IND code addresses radionuclides associated
with a uranium-fueled nuclear detonation in an IND event. The default radionuclide
concentrations and dose rates are based on the areal depositions on a concrete/asphalt mix
surface near the plume centerline at 10 km downwind at 1 hour after the detonation of a 10 kt
yield IND device detonation.

 After an IND event, it is found that initially most of the dose is from direct external
exposure (Kraus and Foster 2013). Therefore, to evaluate the potential dose to first responders in
Group A, only the direct external exposure pathway is considered.

3.2.1 External Dose Rate Modeling

 According to Glasstone and Dolan’s 1977 report (Glasstone and Dolan 1977), the
external dose rate or exposure rate after a nuclear accident follows a power function behavior
with time as approximated by Equation 3.1 (Equation 9.147 in Glasstone and Dolan 1977):

                                            𝐷𝑅𝑡 = 𝐷𝑅𝑟 × 𝑡 −𝛼 ,                                (3.1)

 Where DRt is the dose rate at time t after nuclear detonation, with t representing the time
in hours after the detonation. DRr is the reference dose rate at 1 hour after an IND event and can
be estimated from the radionuclide concentrations. The reference dose rate at 1 hour after a 10 kt
uranium-fueled nuclear detonation at 10 km downwind was estimated as 1.8 Sv/hr using the dose
coefficients from DCFPAK 3.02 and the deposited radionuclide concentrations in Kraus and
Foster (2013). The alpha (𝛼) in Eq. 3.1 is a power function exponent, and its default value is 1.2
(Glasstone and Dolan 1977). Equation 3.1 is used for estimating the dose rate from 0.5 hour after
an IND event to up to 5,000 hours after the event. Equation 3.1 is valid when fallout from an
IND event is complete and the fallout material has not changed during time t (i.e., not removed
in any way such as from weathering or by washing away or being brought in by wind from other
locations or from another nuclear detonation).

 49

 There are three options available in Group A for calculating the dose rates, as described
by Equation 3.1, after an IND event.

 • First, when no dose rate measurement is available and only the yield is known, the
 dose for the desired duration or stay time for a specified total dose is calculated based
 on the known yield and power function exponent.

 • Second, when one dose rate and its measurement time are specified, the known dose
 rate and power function exponent are used to calculate the dose for a desired duration
 or stay times for a specified dose limit.

 • Third, when two dose rates and their measurement times are specified, the power
 function exponent is calculated based on the measured dose rates at two times and is
 used in calculating the dose for a desired duration or stay times for the specified dose
 limit.

 The dose rates measured at two times are used to calculate the reference dose rate and
power function exponent in Equation 3.1. Using Equation 3.1, the dose rate 𝐷𝑅𝑡1 at time t1 and
dose rate 𝐷𝑅𝑡2 at time t2 can be written as:

 𝐷𝑅𝑡1 = 𝐷𝑅𝑟 × 𝑡1−𝛼 and 𝐷𝑅𝑡2 = 𝐷𝑅𝑟 × 𝑡2−𝛼

 The power function exponent is calculated as shown in Equation 3.2 using two measured
dose rates.
 𝐷𝑅𝑡1 𝐷𝑅𝑡2 𝐷𝑅𝑡1 𝑡 −𝛼 𝑡 −𝛼
                                       =          𝑜𝑟          = 1−𝛼 = [ 1 ]
                                𝑡1−𝛼       𝑡2−𝛼        𝐷𝑅𝑡2         𝑡2         𝑡2

 𝐷𝑅𝑡
 ln (𝐷𝑅 1 )
 𝑡2
                                                −∝=          𝑡1                                (3.2)
 ln (𝑡 )

3.2.2 Evaluation of Total Dose and Stay Times

 The total dose received by a first responder from start time t1 (exposure begin time) to
exposure end time t2 is calculated by integrating the dose rate DRt in Equation 3.1 as shown
below in Equation 3.3:

 𝐷𝑜𝑠𝑒 = ∫𝑡 2 𝐷𝑅𝑡 𝑑𝑡 = 𝐷𝑅𝑟 × ∫𝑡 2 𝑡 −𝛼 𝑑𝑡

 With the stay time in Equation 3.5 and a known exposure begin time (t1) for a target dose
limit, Dose, can be derived after calculating the exposure end time, t2, for a given dose limit, as
shown below in Equation 3.4:

 The stay times for Group A were calculated for controlling access to the IND event
location during emergency response operations. They can be applied to provide life- and
property-saving measures and to set the emergency worker demarcation. The stay times are
calculated for all three options available in Group A.

 To use the stay time tables, the first responders would need to know either the yield of the
nuclear detonation, the dose rate at one measurement time, or the dose rates at two measurement
times. Parameters used in Group A include yield, alpha, dose rates, measurement times, exposure
begin time, exposure duration, and dose limits. These parameters are defined in the RESRAD-
RDD&IND User’s Manual Vol. 2, User’s Guide for RESRAD-RDD&IND Version 2 (Kamboj
et al. 2026).

 Appendix 3B gives example calculations of total dose and stay times for multiple cases
considering each of the three options for dose rate modeling coupled with different exposure
begin times.

 APPENDIX 3.A:

DEFAULT STAY TIMES FOR AN RDD EVENT

 APPENDIX 3.A:

 DEFAULT STAY TIMES FOR AN RDD EVENT

 This appendix provides default stay times for an RDD event for a responder without a
respirator, with a full-face air-purifying respirator, and with a full-face continuous-flow
atmosphere-supplying respirator.

3.A.1 Default Stay Times for an RDD Event without a Respirator

 Table 3.A.1 provides the default stay times based on different target doses without the
protection of a respirator when the surface concentration is known. Since Pu-239 has the
maximum DSR (see Table 3.2), it is used as the reference radionuclide for calculating stay times.
This table is for use only when the surface concentration (in terms of gross alpha concentration)
is known but the radionuclides are not identified.

 If the radionuclides are β- emitters (rather than alpha emitters), the stay times for the β-γ
surface concentration (listed in Table 3.A.2) should be used. (Note that stay times in Table 3.A.1
are shorter than those in Table 3.A.2.) To determine the stay times for β-γ emitters, Co-60 is used
as the reference radionuclide because it has the maximum DSR among the β-γ emitters (see
Table 3.2). One important note is that Table 3.A.2 stay times should be used only when it is clear
that β-γ emitters are the primary concern. Because of the significant differences in stay times, a
determination regarding whether β-γ emitters or alpha emitters are present should be made as
soon as possible and respirators should be employed at least until that determination is made.
Refer to Sections 3.A.2 and 3.A.3 for stay times with respirators.

 Table 3.A.3 provides the default stay times for different target doses when the air
concentration is known. Since Pu-239 has the maximum DSR (see Table 3.2), it is used as the
reference radionuclide for calculating stay times.

 Table 3.A.4 presents the default stay times for β- emitters when the external exposure
rates are known. It is assumed that an external exposure rate of 1 R would result in a dose of
0.7 rem (effective dose). Since the fractional dose contribution from submersion and groundshine
to the total dose is the least for Po-210 (see Table 3.2), it is used as the reference radionuclide for
calculating stay times based on the external exposure rate measurement.

3.A.2 Default Stay Times for an RDD Event with a Full-Face Air-Purifying Respirator

 The default stay times presented in this section apply to first responders who wear full-
face air-purifying respirators operating at a negative pressure. The assigned protection factor for
this type of respirator is 100 (Appendix A to 10 CFR Part 20), i.e., the inhalation exposure is
reduced by 100 times when such respirators are worn. In addition, the ingestion exposure is
eliminated. Table 3.A.6 provides the DSRs for different exposure pathways based on unit air
concentrations. The ground surface deposition concentration is calculated from the air
concentration by using a resuspension factor of 1  10-6 m-1. The DSRs are used to derive stay
times assuming radionuclide concentrations remain the same over the exposure period, i.e., the
concentrations are not adjusted for decay and ingrowth.

because it has the maximum DSR.

 Table 3.A.9 provides the default stay times for different target doses when the air
concentration is known. Since Co-60 has the maximum DSR (see Table 3.A.6), it is used as the
reference radionuclide for calculating stay times.

 Table 3.A.10 presents stay times for β- emitters when the external exposure rates are
known. It is assumed that an exposure rate of 1 R would result in a dose of 0.7 rem (effective
dose). Since the fractional dose contribution from air submersion and groundshine to the total
dose is the least for Po-210 (see Table 3.A.6), it is used as the reference radionuclide for
calculating the stay times based on the external exposure rate measurement.

3.A.3 Default Stay Times for an RDD Event with Full-Face Continuous-Flow Atmosphere-
 Supplying Respirators

 The default stay times presented in this section apply to first responders who wear full-
face continuous-flow atmosphere-supplying respirators. The assigned protection factor for this
type of respirator is 1,000 (Appendix A to 10 CFR Part 20), i.e., the inhalation exposure is
reduced by 1,000 times when such respirators are worn. In addition, the ingestion exposure is
eliminated. Table 3.A.12 provides the DSRs for different exposure pathways based on unit air
concentration. The surface deposition concentration is calculated from the air concentration by
using a resuspension factor of 1  10-6 m-1. The DSRs are used to calculate stay times assuming
radionuclide concentrations remain the same over the exposure period, i.e., the concentrations
are not adjusted for decay and ingrowth.

 Table 3.A.13 provides the stay times for different target doses when the surface
concentration is known. Since Ra-226 has the maximum DSR among the alpha emitters
(see Table 3.A.12), it is used as the reference radionuclide for calculating stay times. This table
should be used only when the possibility of β- emitters is eliminated and the total surface
concentration (in terms of gross alpha concentration) is known but radionuclides are not
identified.

 If the contaminants are β- emitters (rather than alpha emitters), the stay times for β-
surface concentrations (listed in Table 3.A.14) should be used. (Note that stay times in
Table 3.A.14 are shorter than those in Table 3.A.13.) Table 3.A.13 can also be used when the
emission type (alpha or β-) is not known. To calculate the stay times for β- emitters, Co-60 is
used as the reference radionuclide because it has the maximum DSR.

 Table 3.A.15 provides stay times for different target doses when the air concentration is
known. Since Co-60 has the maximum DSR (see Table 3.A.12), it is used as the reference
radionuclide for calculating stay times.

 Table 3.A.16 presents stay times for β-γ emitters when external exposure rates are
known. It is assumed that an exposure rate of 1 R would result in a dose of 0.7 rem (effective
dose). Since the fractional dose contribution of submersion and groundshine to the total dose is
the least for Po-210, it is used as the reference radionuclide for calculating stay times based on
the external exposure rate measurement.

 APPENDIX 3.B:

EXAMPLE STAY TIMES FOR AN IND EVENT

 67

 APPENDIX 3.B:

 EXAMPLE STAY TIMES FOR AN IND EVENT

 This appendix provides stay times for a few hypothetical cases for all three options
available (yield, dose rate at one measurement time, and dose rates at two measurement times).

 Since first responders may reach the event site at different times, the stay times for the
three cases at different exposure begin times are calculated: (1) exposure starts 1 hour after
detonation, (2) exposure starts 6 hours after detonation, and (3) exposure starts 24 hours after
detonation. RESRAD-RDD&IND code for an IND event can be used at other exposure begin
times. Since the dose rate measurement can be available any time after detonation, the default
stay times for two cases at two different measurement times are calculated for the three exposure
begin times: (1) 1 hour after detonation and (2) 4 hours after detonation. The code can be used at
other dose rate measurement times and exposure begin times. Similarly, for the dose rates at the
two measurement times option, the stay times in two cases at different measurement times are
calculated for three exposure begin times: (1) first dose rate measurement at 1 hour and another
dose rate measurement at 2 hours after detonation and (2) the first dose rate measurement at
4 hours and the second dose rate measurement at 8 hours after detonation. The RESRAD-
RDD&IND code can be used at other two dose rate measurement times and exposure begin
times.
