 82

 4 GROUP B: EARLY PHASE PROTECTIVE ACTION
 (EVACUATION OR SHELTERING)

 After an RDD or IND event, either calculated doses or derived operational guidelines
using PAGs are used in deciding whether to evacuate or shelter the general public as an early
phase protective action. “Early phase” is defined as the first 4 days following an RDD or IND
event. The 4-day integrated dose from a known uranium-fueled IND-deposited radionuclide mix
or from known radionuclide concentration(s) of RDD or IND radionuclides is either calculated
for comparison with the PAG of 1 rem (10 mSv) or operational guidelines for RDD
radionuclides are derived based on the 1 rem (10 mSv) PAG. The operational guidelines are
expressed as limiting concentrations in the ground surface (soil/street). If the measured ground
surface concentration is higher than the value of the operational guideline or the calculated dose
from the measured ground surface concentration or the known IND-deposited radionuclide mix
is higher than 1 rem (10 mSv), the protective actions would be justified in the early phase of an
RDD or IND event.

 Three potential exposure scenarios are considered for calculating dose or deriving
operational guidelines in Group B. The scenarios consider the different time fractions spent in an
indoor and outdoor environment by different receptors. The scenarios assume that the receptors
would spend 100% of the time (either indoors or outdoors) in the contaminated area.

4.1 ASSUMPTIONS AND METHODOLOGY

 For the early phase, it is assumed that a ground surface measuring 10,000 m2 is
contaminated after an RDD or IND event. In addition to the ground surface, the exterior of a
building (four walls and the roof) is also contaminated. It is assumed that the RDD or IND event
occurred in an urban environment. During the first four days (early phase), track-in of the
outdoor ground surface contamination would be relatively insignificant; therefore, the indoor
surfaces are assumed to not be contaminated.

 The contamination level on the roof is assumed to be the same as that on the ground
surface, and the contamination level on the exterior walls is assumed to be half of the
contamination level on the ground surface. This is a conservative assumption because the
concentration levels on exterior walls could be much lower than half of the soil/street
contamination level (Andersson et al. 2003). If measured concentration data are available for the
roof and exterior walls, the data can be used to determine the fraction of the concentration on the
ground surface, i.e., the partitioning factor, which then can be used to derive site-specific
operational guidelines or calculate site-specific radiation doses.

 The weathering correction is applied to adjust the radionuclide concentrations on
different surfaces. In addition to weathering, radiological decay and ingrowth also affect the
surface concentrations. A time-dependent resuspension factor is used to calculate the outdoor air
concentration, which is increased by a multiplication factor of 10 to account for vehicular traffic
in an urban environment (Walsh 2002). In general, the indoor air concentration is attributed to
two components: one from resuspension of interior surface contamination and the other from air
exchange with contaminated outdoor air. For the second component, the outdoor air

 83

concentration is multiplied by the indoor dust filtration factor to get the indoor air concentration.
For the first component, the indoor resuspension factor assumed to be constant at 10-6 m-1
(IAEA 2002; NRC 2001) is multiplied by the indoor surface concentration to get the indoor air
concentration. However, for the early phase, the tracked-in deposited radionuclides are assumed
to be negligible; the contribution of the first component to the indoor air concentration is zero.

 A receptor is assumed to incur radiation doses through (1) exposure to external radiation
from multiple contaminated surfaces, (2) inhalation of contaminated air, (3) exposure to external
radiation from submersion in contaminated air, and (4) ingestion of contamination deposited on
surfaces. The external dose from outside contamination to an indoor receptor is adjusted for
shielding provided by the building. The external doses from different surfaces are corrected for
surface roughness. Building surface roughnesses would depend on the types of materials used.
Rochedo et al. (1996) provides roughness correction factors for different urban building surfaces.
In the default case, it is conservatively assumed that both the roof and exterior walls have smooth
surfaces, so a roughness correction factor of 1 is applied. For the street/soil, the default
roughness correction factor is 0.82.

4.2 KEY PARAMETERS USED

 The value of the resuspension factor for the outdoor environment decreases with time, as
described by Eq. 2.2 in Chapter 2 and approaches 10-9 m-1. The value is multiplied by a factor of
10 to account for the higher resuspension due to vehicular traffic in an urban environment
(Walsh 2002).

 The indoor dust infiltration factor of 0.55 is used, which is the mean value of the uniform
distribution reported in NUREG/CR-6697 (Yu et al. 2000).

 The building shielding factor for external radiation emitted by radionuclides in the
outside ground surface varies from 0.4 (one- and two-story wood frame house) to 0.005 (in the
basement of a multistory structure) (SNL 2020). Since an RDD/IND event is assumed to occur in
an urban environment, a shielding factor of 0.2 is used in the external dose calculations, which is
the value for one- and two-story block and brick houses. An ingestion rate of 1.25  10-5 m2/h
(see Section 2.8.4) is used for direct ingestion of the contamination on the ground surface.

 The indoor inhalation rate used as default is 0.9 m3/h (21.6 m3/d), which is the mode of
distribution for the inhalation rates of residents in NUREG/CR-6697 (Yu et al. 2000). For the
outdoor inhalation rate, a default value of 1.2 m3/h is used, which is the average outdoor
breathing rate for a sedentary worker in NUREG/CR-6697 (Yu et al. 2000).

 The building considered for modeling external dose is an urban apartment (geometry
number 3) listed in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. It has a floor area of 100 m2, a ceiling height of
2.4 m, and a shielding thickness of 10 cm of concrete for walls and roof.

 84

4.3 SCENARIO DESCRIPTION

 Scenario B-1 (resident spending time indoors) assumes that the resident spends 100% of
the time indoors. Under this scenario, the resident would incur radiation doses through
(1) exposure to external radiation from the contaminated surfaces while indoors, (2) inhalation of
contaminated air while indoors, and (3) exposure to external radiation from submersion in
contaminated air while indoors. Since the resident would not be in direct contact with
contaminated surfaces located outside, there would not be any direct ingestion dose.

 Scenario B-2 (resident spending time both indoors and outdoors) assumes that the
resident spends 16.4 h/d indoors and 7.6 h/d outdoors. The value of the indoor hours is the
average of the time spent inside a residence recommended in the Exposure Factor Handbook
(EPA 2011) for different age groups. Under this scenario, it is assumed that the resident would
incur radiation doses through (1) exposure to external radiation from the contaminated surfaces
while indoors, (2) exposure to external radiation from the contaminated ground surface while
outdoors, (3) inhalation of contaminated air while indoors, (4) inhalation of contaminated air
while outdoors, (5) exposure to external radiation from submersion in contaminated air while
indoors, (6) exposure to external radiation from submersion in contaminated air while outdoors,
and (7) ingestion of contamination deposited on the ground surface while outdoors.

 Scenario B-3 (worker spending time outdoors) assumes that the worker spends 100% of
the time outdoors. Under this scenario, it is assumed that the worker would incur radiation doses
through (1) exposure to external radiation from the contaminated ground surface, (2) inhalation
of contaminated air, (3) exposure to external radiation from submersion in contaminated air, and
(4) ingestion of contamination deposited on the ground surface.

4.4 DOSE CALCULATION AND OPERATIONAL GUIDELINE DERIVATION

 The total doses or DSRs for all three scenarios (B-1 through B-3) for the first four days
are calculated for an RDD or IND event. For RDD radionuclides, DSRs are used in deriving the
operational guidelines. DSRs from individual pathways are added to get the total DSRs. The
PAGs used in deriving guidelines or for comparing with calculated doses for different scenarios
are provided in Table 2.6 in Chapter 2.

4.5 DISCUSSION

 For the default scenarios, radiation doses or DSRs are calculated assuming an RDD or
IND event occurs in an urban environment and radiological contamination is limited to the
outdoor surfaces (ground, roof, and exterior walls). Based on these assumptions, the dimensions
of an urban apartment are used for the calculations of the external dose or DSR, and a
multiplication factor of 10 is used for the calculation of the inhalation dose or DSR. If the
assumptions are not applicable and/or site-specific measurement data are available, the default
parameter values should be adjusted or replaced to obtain more realistic radiation dose or
DSR results.

 5 GROUP C: RELOCATION, PART 1 — RELOCATION FROM RESIDENTIAL,
 COMMERCIAL/INDUSTRIAL, AND OTHER AREAS

 The radiation dose calculated or the operational guidelines derived after an RDD or IND
event for Group C (Part 1) are for use in deciding whether to relocate the general public. This
group addresses residential areas (Group C1), commercial/industrial areas (Group C2), and other
areas (Group C3) as separate subgroups. The other areas could be monuments, parks, cemeteries,
or other special areas.

 The total dose or operational guidelines for Group C (Part 1) can be used to delineate
areas that could result in radiological doses exceeding the relocation protective action guides
(PAGs) (i.e., 2 rem [20 mSv] in the first year and 0.5 rem [5 mSv] per year thereafter). If the
potential dose from an area could exceed the above relocation PAGs, access to this area should
be limited for workers or members of the public. A total of eight receptors (see Table 2.6 in
Chapter 2) are considered for Subgroups C1, C2, and C3.

 Dose calculations are performed by RESRAD-RDD&IND for an IND event for which
two options are available to determine the initial radionuclide concentrations. In one option, the
initial ground concentrations are calculated based on either the yield of the detonations or a
measured dose rate with corresponding time as described in Chapter 2. The other option becomes
available when the “Edit Table” box in the interface is checked. In this option, the user can input
the measured ground concentration of individual radionuclides for use as the initial
concentrations.

5.1 RELOCATION FROM RESIDENTIAL AREAS (GROUP C1)

 Calculations for relocation from residential areas assume both urban and rural settings.
Four potential exposure scenarios are considered for dose calculation or for the derivation of
operational guidelines. Potential radiation doses (time-integrated over the exposure duration)
resulting from 13 exposure pathways are considered. The applicability of these exposure
pathways to the four scenarios evaluated is summarized in Table 2.7 in Chapter 2.

 Two scenarios are considered in an urban environment, and two scenarios are considered
in a rural environment. The building dimensions considered for external exposure are based on
an average urban house or a rural house. For the urban environment, initial outdoor
contamination is assumed on streets; for the rural environment, initial outdoor contamination is
assumed on soil. The base outdoor resuspension factor is multiplied by 10 (default value) to
account for traffic under urban conditions (see Table 2.4 in Chapter 2).

 Both the urban and rural residential scenarios considered for dose calculations or the
derivation of relocation operational guidelines do not include the food ingestion and water
ingestion pathways because potential radiation exposure from food ingestion and water ingestion
are covered by separate PAGs, which are discussed in Chapter 10 on Group G and Chapter 11 on
Group H.

 87

 To account for the differences in the time fractions spent indoors and outdoors by
different individuals, two receptors, one that would spend 100% of the time indoors and the other
that would spend some time working outdoors, are considered for each environmental setting.
The default time fractions spent indoors and outdoors by the working residents are based on the
guidance provided in the EPA Exposure Factor Handbook (EPA 2011). The other exposure
parameters used in the different exposure scenarios are listed in Table 2.8 in Chapter 2.

5.1.1 Scenario Description for Relocation from Residential Areas

 Scenario C1-1 (relocation from a residential area in an urban setting) assumes continued
use of the contaminated area in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a resident is assumed
to spend all the time indoors in the contaminated area, which is consistent with the Group B1
scenario. The building dimensions considered for external exposure are based on an average
urban house, as shown in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2.

 Scenario C1-2 (relocation from a residential area in an urban setting) also assumes
continued use of the contaminated area in an urban environment. Under this scenario, an adult
resident is assumed to spend all the time in the contaminated area, sometimes indoors and
sometimes outdoors (an average of 16.4 h/d indoors and 7.6 h/d outdoors). The building
dimensions considered for external exposure are based on an average urban house.

 Scenario C1-3 (relocation from a residential area in a rural setting) assumes continued
use of the contaminated area in a rural environment. Under this scenario, a resident is assumed to
spend all the time indoors in the contaminated area. The building dimensions considered for
external exposure are based on an average rural house, as shown in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2.

 Scenario C1-4 (relocation from a residential area in a rural setting) assumes continued
use of the contaminated area in a rural environment. Under this scenario, an adult resident is
assumed to spend all the time in the contaminated area, sometimes indoors and sometimes
outdoors (an average of 16.4 h/d indoors and 7.6 h/d outdoors). The building dimensions
considered for external exposure are based on an average rural house.

5.1.2 Dose Calculation and Operational Guideline Derivation for Relocation from
 Residential Areas

 The total doses or DSRs for all four scenarios (C1-1 through C1-4) for the first and
second years are calculated for an RDD or IND event. For RDD radionuclides, DSRs are used to
derive the operational guidelines. The PAGs used in deriving guidelines or for comparing with
calculated doses for different scenarios are provided in Table 2.6 in Chapter 2. These results
apply to residential areas and are not applicable for locations such as parks, cemeteries,
monuments, or industrial/commercial properties. The PAGs used are 2 rem (20 mSv) for the first
year and 0.5 rem per year (5 mSv/yr) after the first year. Four different receptors/scenarios are
considered in dose calculations or when deriving generic operational guideline values.
Scenarios C1-1 and C1-2 are for relocating from residential areas in an urban setting, and
Scenarios C1-3 and C1-4 are for relocating from residential areas in a rural setting.

 88

5.2 RELOCATION FROM COMMERCIAL/INDUSTRIAL AREAS (GROUP C2)

 Two potential exposure scenarios are considered for dose calculations or the derivation of
operational guidelines for relocating from commercial/industrial areas (Group C2). The
commercial/industrial area is assumed to be in an urban environment. Potential radiation doses
(integrated over the exposure duration) resulting from 13 exposure pathways are considered, as
shown in Table 2.7 in Chapter 2.

 To account for the differences in the time fractions spent indoors and outdoors by
different individuals, two receptors ⎯ one who would spend all the time indoors and the other
who would spend all the time outdoors ⎯ are considered. The exposure parameters used in
different exposure scenarios are listed in Table 2.8 in Chapter 2. The building dimensions
considered for external exposure are based on a warehouse with dimensions shown in Table 2.5
in Chapter 2, and the initial outdoor contamination is assumed on streets. The outdoor
resuspension factor is multiplied by 10 to account for traffic in urban conditions (see Table 2.4 in
Chapter 2).

5.2.1 Scenario Description for Relocation from Commercial/Industrial Areas

 Scenario C2-1 (relocation from commercial/industrial area, indoor office worker)
assumes continued use of the contaminated area in an urban environment. Under this scenario, an
indoor office worker is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 8 h/d for 250 days in 1 year)
indoors in the contaminated building.

 Scenario C2-2 (relocation from commercial/industrial area, outdoor worker) also assumes
continued use of the contaminated area in an urban environment. Under this scenario, an outdoor
worker is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 8 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) outdoors in
the contaminated area.

5.2.2 Dose Calculation and Operational Guideline Derivation for Relocation from
 Commercial/Industrial Areas

 The total doses or DSRs for both scenarios (C2-1 and C2-2) for the first and second years
are calculated for an RDD or IND event. For RDD radionuclides, DSRs are used in deriving the
operational guidelines. The PAGs used in deriving guidelines or for comparing with calculated
doses for different scenarios are provided in Table 2.6 in Chapter 2. These results apply to
commercial/industrial areas and are not applicable for locations such as residential areas, parks,
cemeteries, or monuments. The PAGs used are 2 rem (20 mSv) for the first year and 0.5 rem per
year (5 mSv/yr) after the first year. Two scenarios, C2-1 and C2-2, are considered in deriving
generic operational guidelines for relocating from commercial/industrial areas in an urban
setting.

 89

5.3 RELOCATION FROM OTHER AREAS (GROUP C3)

 Two potential exposure scenarios are considered for dose calculations or derivation of
operational guidelines for relocating from other areas. The “other area” is assumed to be a
special building or an open area located in an urban environment. It could be a monument, a
cemetery, or a park. Potential radiation doses resulting from 13 exposure pathways are
considered, as summarized in Table 2.7 in Chapter 2. To account for the differences in the time
fractions spent indoors and outdoors by different individuals, two receptors ⎯ one spending all
the time indoors and the other spending all the time outdoors ⎯ are considered. The most
common parameters used in these exposure scenarios are listed in Table 2.8 in Chapter 2. The
building dimensions considered for external exposure are based on a monument with dimensions
specified in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. The initial outdoor contamination is assumed to be on soil.
The base outdoor resuspension factor is multiplied by 10 (default value) to account for light
pedestrian activity under urban conditions (see Table 2.4 in Chapter 2).

5.3.1 Scenario Description for Relocation from Other Areas

 Scenario C3-1 (relocation from other areas, indoor worker at a monument) assumes
continued use of the contaminated area in an urban environment. Under this scenario, an indoor
worker at a monument is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 8 h/d for 250 days in
1 year) inside the contaminated building.

 Under Scenario C3-2 (relocation from other areas, outdoor worker at a monument or a
park), an outdoor worker outside a monument or in a park is assumed to spend all the time
(an average of 8 h/d for 250 days in one year) outdoors in the contaminated area in an urban
environment.

5.3.2 Dose Calculation and Operational Guideline Derivation for Relocation from
 Other Areas

 The total doses or DSRs for both worker scenarios (C3-1 and C3-2) for the first and
second years are calculated for an RDD or IND event. For RDD radionuclides, DSRs are used to
derive the operational guidelines. The PAGs used in deriving guidelines or for comparing with
calculated doses are provided in Table 2.6 in Chapter 2. These results apply to areas other than
residential and industrial/commercial areas (normal workplaces). Such areas include parks,
cemeteries, and monuments. Two scenarios, C3-1 and C3-2, are considered in calculating dose
or deriving generic operational guidelines for relocating from these areas.

5.4 SUMMARY OF DOSE CALCULATIONS AND OPERATIONAL GUIDELINES
 DERIVATION FOR RELOCATION

 In the default case, the contamination in a residential area would result in the highest dose
or the most restrictive operational guidelines; therefore, the dose/operational guideline results
concerning exposures of residents could be used for making the relocation decision.

 6 GROUP C: RELOCATION, PART 2 — CRITICAL INFRASTRUCTURE
 UTILIZATION IN RELOCATION AREAS

 The radiation doses calculated or operational guidelines derived for Group C (Part 2) are
for use in deciding whether the facilities critical to the public welfare can continue to operate if
needed after an RDD or IND event.

 The radiation doses calculated for an RDD or IND event or operational guidelines
derived for an RDD event in this group apply only to facilities in areas that exceed the relocation
PAGs and might, as a result, be closed for general use and access except for the fact that their
operation is critical to the public welfare. The results are generally applicable during the
intermediate phase. It is assumed that the continued use of such facilities would be temporary
(less than 6 months for hospitals and other health care facilities). After this time, it would be
possible to either provide the services through alternate means or implement interim control,
restoration, or decontamination measures in order to reduce contamination levels below the
relocation operational guidelines or the resultant radiation dose below the relocation PAGs. It is
expected that after their restoration or decontamination, the facilities would be treated as any
commercial/industrial facility. The critical infrastructures considered include hospitals and other
health care facilities, critical transport facilities, water and sewer facilities, and power and fuel
facilities. To calculate doses or derive operational guidelines for the use of critical infrastructure
facilities, time-integrated doses or dose-to-source ratios (DSRs) were estimated for different
critical receptors (see Table 2.6 in Chapter 2) that would work in these contaminated areas. As
for relocation, the dose calculations are available for two options for an IND event.

6.1 HOSPITALS AND OTHER HEALTH CARE FACILITIES (GROUP C4)

 The operational guidelines or doses calculated for this group allow for the continued use
of health care facilities and services that are in areas that exceed the relocation criteria. The
5 rem (50 mSv) occupational exposure PAG was applied to health care facility employees, and
the 2 rem (20 mSv) public PAG was applied to the public in making decisions for the hospitals
and other health care facilities. Three potential exposure scenarios are considered in the dose
calculations. Potential radiation doses resulting from 13 exposure pathways are considered. The
applicability of these exposure pathways to the three scenarios evaluated is summarized in
Table 2.7 in Chapter 2. All scenarios considered are in an urban environment, and the building
dimensions considered for external exposure are based on the warehouse geometry shown in
Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. The initial outdoor contamination is assumed to be on streets.

 To account for the differences in the time spent inside and outside by different
individuals, two receptors are considered, one that would spend all the time indoors and the other
that would spend all the time outdoors. A third receptor is assumed to be a patient that would
spend the initial 2 weeks after an IND event inside the hospital. The parameters used in different
exposure scenarios are listed in Table 2.8 in Chapter 2.

 93

6.1.1 Scenario Description for Hospitals and Other Health Care Facilities

 Scenario C4-1 (hospital outdoor worker) assumes continued use of the contaminated area
in an urban environment. Under this scenario, an outdoor worker is assumed to spend all the time
outdoors in the contaminated area (an average of 8 h/d for 125 days [6 months]). For the hospital
outdoor worker, the time-integrated dose over the exposure duration of 6 months from the
exposure begin time is calculated taking account of radionuclide decay and ingrowth,
weathering, and resuspension. It is assumed that the facility would be cleaned within 6 months or
alternate arrangements would be made. The outdoor worker is exposed to radiation through
exposure to outside contamination, inhaling contaminated outdoor air, and directly ingesting dust
from contaminated outdoor areas.

 Scenario C4-2 (hospital indoor worker) assumes continued use of the contaminated area
in an urban environment. Under this scenario, an indoor worker is assumed to spend all the time
indoors in the contaminated area (an average of 8 h/d indoors for 125 days [6 months]). For the
hospital indoor worker, the time-integrated dose over the exposure duration of 6 months from the
exposure begin time is calculated taking account of radionuclide decay and ingrowth,
weathering, and resuspension. It is assumed that the facility would be cleaned within 6 months or
alternate arrangements would be made. The building dimensions considered for external
exposure are based on the warehouse geometry shown in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. All applicable
exposure pathways are shown in Table 2.7 in Chapter 2.

 Scenario C4-3 (patient staying in hospital) assumes continued use of the contaminated
area in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a patient is assumed to spend all the time
indoors in the contaminated area (an average of 24 h/d for the first 2 weeks). For the patient, the
time-integrated dose over the exposure duration of 2 weeks from the exposure begin time is
calculated taking account of radionuclide decay and ingrowth, weathering, and resuspension. It is
assumed that the patient would be released within 2 weeks or alternate arrangements would be
made. The building dimensions considered for external exposure are based on the warehouse
geometry shown in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. All applicable exposure pathways are shown in
Table 2.7 in Chapter 2.

6.1.2 Dose Calculation and Operational Guideline Derivation for Hospitals and Other
 Health Care Facilities

 The total doses or DSRs for all three scenarios (C4-1, C4-2, and C4-3) for the short
duration are calculated for an RDD or IND event. For RDD radionuclides, DSRs are used in
calculating the operational guidelines. The PAGs used in deriving guidelines or for comparing
with calculated doses for different scenarios are provided in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2.

 Different scenarios were considered in calculating doses or deriving generic operational
guidelines for use of hospitals for a short duration. The scenarios considered include
occupational use by the hospital staff for 6 months and use by a patient staying for 2 weeks
immediately after an RDD or IND event. In fact, hospital buildings would most likely be cleaned
before 6 months, and patients would stay in the hospital for less than 2 weeks.

 94

 To continue using the hospitals and health care facilities beyond 6 months, the relocation
calculated doses or operational guidelines for commercial/industrial areas should be applied to
these facilities after necessary decontamination activities for the first year and after the first year.
It should be noted that occupational exposure should never exceed 5 rem (50 mSv) in a year, and
public exposure should never exceed 2 rem (20 mSv) for the first year; therefore, even if the
buildings were cleaned after 6 months, the workers who have already received a dose of 5 rem
(50 mSv) should not be allowed to work in these buildings for the rest of the year. Those who
have received a dose of less than 5 rem (50 mSv) during the first 6 months could continue
working in these buildings until the total dose meets the 5 rem/yr (50 mSv/yr) criterion.

6.2 CRITICAL TRANSPORT FACILITIES (GROUP C5)

 The operational guidelines or doses calculated for this group would allow continued use
of critical transport facilities located in areas that exceed relocation criteria. The time-integrated
dose calculated for facility employees will be compared to the 5 rem (50 mSv) occupational
exposure PAG, and the time-integrated dose calculated for the public will be compared to the
2 rem (20 mSv) public PAG in making decisions for the critical transport facilities. Four
potential exposure scenarios were considered for the dose calculations.

 Potential exposure pathways for the four scenarios evaluated are summarized in
Table 2.10 in Chapter 2. All scenarios are considered in an urban environment, and the building
dimensions considered for external exposure for facility employees and the general public are
based on the subway station geometry and the railcar geometry, respectively, as shown in
Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. The initial outdoor contamination is assumed to be on streets. The
resuspension factor is multiplied by 10 to account for pedestrian activity and traffic in an urban
environment (see Table 2.4 in Chapter 2).

 To account for differences in the time fractions spent inside and outside of a transport
facility by different people, two receptors accounting for occupational workers and two receptors
accounting for the general public are considered. For the occupational receptors, it is assumed
that one receptor would spend all the time outdoors and the other one would spend all the time
indoors. For the general public receptors, the time fractions spent indoors and outdoors are based
on the guidance provided in the EPA Exposure Factor Handbook (EPA 2011). The parameters
used for different receptors are listed in Table 2.8 in Chapter 2.

6.2.1 Scenario Description for Critical Transport Facilities

 Scenario C5-1 (critical transport facilities, ticket clerk staying inside) assumes continued
use of a contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, an indoor ticket
clerk is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 8 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) indoors in the
contaminated subway station.

 Scenario C5-2 (critical transport facilities, baggage handler staying outside) assumes
continued use of a contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a baggage
handler is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 8 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) outdoors in
the contaminated subway station.

 95

 Scenario C5-3 (critical transport facilities, passenger staying inside) assumes continued
use of a contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a passenger is
assumed to spend all the time (an average of 1.25 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) indoors in a
contaminated railcar.

 Scenario C5-4 (critical transport facilities, passenger staying both outside and inside)
assumes continued use of a contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a
passenger is assumed to spend some time (an average of 1 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) inside a
contaminated railcar and some time (an average of 0.25 h/d for 250 days in one year) outside in
the subway station.

6.2.2 Dose Calculation for Critical Transport Facilities

 The total doses or DSRs for all four scenarios (C5-1, C5-2, C5-3, and C5-4) for the first
year are calculated for an RDD or IND event. For RDD radionuclides, DSRs are used in
calculating the operational guidelines. The PAGs used in deriving guidelines or for comparing
with calculated doses for different scenarios are provided in Table 2.6 in Chapter 2.

 Different scenarios are considered in calculating doses or deriving generic operation
guidelines for continued use of critical transport facilities. The scenarios considered include
occupational use by facility workers and public use by passengers after an RDD or IND event.
For extended use of the transport facilities beyond one year, the relocation dose calculations or
operational guidelines for commercial/industrial areas should be applied to evaluate the facilities
after necessary decontamination activities.

6.3 WATER AND SEWER FACILITIES (GROUP C6)

 The operational guidelines or doses calculated for this group would allow continued use
of water and sewer facilities located in areas that exceed relocation criteria. The time-integrated
doses calculated for the facility employees will be compared to the 5 rem (50 mSv) occupational
exposure PAG, and the time-integrated doses calculated for the public will be compared to the
2 rem (20 mSv) public PAG in planning for the water and sewer facilities. Four potential
exposure scenarios are considered for the dose calculations. Potential exposure pathways for the
four scenarios evaluated are summarized in Table 2.7 in Chapter 2. All scenarios are considered
in an urban environment, and the building dimensions considered for external exposure for
facility employees and the general public are based on the warehouse geometry shown in
Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. The initial outdoor contamination is assumed to be on streets. The
resuspension factor was multiplied by 10 to account for pedestrian activity and traffic in an urban
environment (see Table 2.4 in Chapter 2).

 To account for differences in the time fractions spent inside and outside water and sewer
facilities by different people, two receptors accounting for regular occupational workers at the
facilities and two receptors accounting for the general public who happen to perform contract
work at the facilities are considered. For the occupational receptors, it is assumed that one
receptor would spend all the time outdoors and the other one would spend all the time indoors.
For the general public receptors, it is assumed that one receptor would spend an average of 2 h/d

 96

for 250 days indoors and the other would spend the same amount of time outdoors. The most
common parameters used for different receptors are listed in Table 2.8 in Chapter 2.

6.3.1 Scenario Description for Water and Sewer Facilities

 Scenario C6-1 (water and sewer facilities, facility outdoor worker) assumes continued use
of the contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, an outdoor facility
worker is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 8 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) outdoors in
the contaminated facility.

 Scenario C6-2 (water and sewer facilities, facility indoor worker) assumes continued use
of the contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a facility indoor
worker is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 8 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) indoors in
the contaminated facility.

 Scenario C6-3 (water and sewer facilities, contractor works inside) assumes continued
use of the contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a contract worker
is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 2 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) indoors in the
contaminated facility.

 Scenario C6-4 (water and sewer facilities, contractor works outside) assumes continued
use of the contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a contract worker
is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 2 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) outdoors in the
contaminated facility.

6.3.2 Dose Calculation and Operational Guideline Derivation for Water and Sewer
 Facilities

 The total doses or DSRs for all four scenarios (C6-1, C6-2, C6-3, and C6-4) for the first
year are calculated for an RDD or IND event. For RDD radionuclides, DSRs are used in
calculating the operational guidelines. The PAGs used in deriving guidelines or for comparing
with calculated doses for different scenarios are provided in Table 2.6 in Chapter 2.

 Different scenarios are considered in calculating doses or deriving generic operation
guidelines for continued use of water and sewer facilities. The scenarios considered include
occupational use by facility workers and public use by contract workers after an RDD or IND
event. For extended use of the water and sewer facilities beyond 1 year, the relocation dose
results or operational guidelines for commercial/industrial areas should be applied to evaluate the
facilities after necessary decontamination activities.

6.4 POWER AND FUEL FACILITIES (GROUP C7)

 The operational guidelines or doses calculated for this group would allow continued use
of power and fuel facilities located in areas that exceed relocation criteria. The time-integrated
doses calculated for the facility employees will be compared to the 5 rem (50 mSv) occupational

 97

exposure PAG, and the time-integrated doses calculated for the public will be compared to the
2 rem (20 mSv) public PAG in making decisions for the water and sewer facilities. Four
potential exposure scenarios are considered for the dose calculations. Potential exposure
pathways for the four scenarios evaluated are summarized in Table 2.7 in Chapter 2. All
scenarios are considered in an urban environment, and the building dimensions considered for
external exposure for facility employees and the general public are based on the warehouse
geometry shown in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. The initial outdoor contamination is assumed to be on
streets. The resuspension factor is multiplied by 10 to account for pedestrian activity and traffic
in an urban environment (see Table 2.4 in Chapter 2).

 To account for differences in the time fractions spent inside and outside of power/fuel
facilities by different people, two receptors accounting for regular occupational work at the
facilities and two receptors accounting for the general public who happen to perform contract
work at the facilities are considered. For the occupational receptors, it is assumed that one
receptor would spend all the time outdoors and the other one would spend all the time indoors.
For the general public receptors, it is assumed that one receptor would spend an average of 2 h/d
for 250 days indoors and the other would spend the same amount of time outdoors. The most
common parameters used for different receptors are listed in Table 2.8 in Chapter 2.

6.4.1 Scenario Description for Power and Fuel Facilities

 Scenario C7-1 (power and fuel facilities, facility outdoor worker) assumes continued use
of the contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, an outdoor facility
worker is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 8 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) outdoors in
the contaminated facility.

 Scenario C7-2 (power and fuel facilities, facility indoor worker) assumes continued use
of the contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a facility indoor
worker is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 8 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) indoors in
the contaminated facility.

 Scenario C7-3 (power and fuel facilities, contractor works inside) assumes continued use
of the contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a contract worker is
assumed to spend all the time (an average of 2 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) indoors in the
contaminated facility.

 Scenario C7-4 (power and fuel facilities, contractor works outside) assumes continued
use of the contaminated facility in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a contract worker
is assumed to spend all the time (an average of 2 h/d for 250 days in 1 year) outdoors in the
contaminated facility.

 98

6.4.2 Dose Calculation and Operational Guideline Derivation for Power and Fuel
 Facilities

 The radiation dose calculations for an RDD or IND event or operational guidelines for an
RDD event for this group (i.e., power and fuel facilities) would be the same as for water and
sewer facilities.

6.5 SUMMARY OF DOSE CALCULATIONS OR OPERATIONAL GUIDELINES
 DERIVATIONS FOR RELOCATION

 In the default case, for hospitals and other healthcare facilities, the outdoor worker
scenario resulted in the maximum radiation dose or most restrictive operational guidelines for
workers, and the patients staying at the hospital scenario resulted in the maximum radiation dose
or the most restrictive operational guidelines for the public. For critical transport facilities, the
baggage handler scenario resulted in the maximum radiation dose or the most restrictive
operational guidelines for workers, and the scenario in which the passenger stays both inside and
outside resulted in the maximum radiation dose or most restrictive operational guidelines for
most radionuclides for the public. For water and sewer/power and fuel facilities, the facility
outdoor worker scenario resulted in the maximum radiation dose or most restrictive operational
guidelines, and the contract worker staying outdoor scenario resulted in the maximum radiation
dose or most restrictive operational guidelines for the public.
