 100

 7 GROUP D: TEMPORARY ACCESS TO RELOCATION AREAS
 FOR ESSENTIAL ACTIVITIES

 The radiation doses calculated for an RDD or IND event or operational guidelines
derived for RDD radionuclides for this group (i.e., Group D) are meant to define protective
actions or restrictions necessary to allow for temporary access to relocation areas. The public or
employees of businesses may need temporary access to residences or commercial, agricultural,
or industrial facilities to retrieve essential records or equipment, conduct maintenance to protect
the facilities, prevent environmental damage, attend to animals, or retrieve pets. The radiation
doses incurred from temporary accesses within a year should not exceed the relocation PAGs,
which are the bases of the derived operational guidelines (for RDD radionuclides). For Group D,
the operation guidelines are expressed in terms of stay times or limiting ground surface
concentrations in the relocation area.

 The stay times derived for RDD radionuclides are the total amounts of time in a year
during which the public or employees may access the contaminated areas without the concern of
incurring a total radiation dose greater than the relocation PAGs. They are derived for individual
radionuclides or multiple radionuclides combined depending on their ground surface
concentrations in the contaminated area and the exposure begin time and considering dose
contributions from their decay progenies. The exposure frequency (d/yr) and daily exposure time
(h/d) are not used in the derivation. Although the stay times are derived considering continuous
exposure with a single trip, in practice, the total amount of time can be distributed over multiple
trips that are taken after the exposure begin time.

 For a multi-day access with the same exposure pattern each day, i.e., the same amount of
time staying inside or outside a facility or a residence in the contaminated area each day,
operational guidelines are derived in terms of limiting ground surface concentrations. These
operational guidelines are derived with the specified daily indoor and outdoor exposure times
(h/d) and depend on the exposure frequency (d/yr), i.e., the number of days of the multi-day trip.
The potential radiation dose incurred during the multi-day access would not exceed the
relocation PAG when the ground surface concentration in the contaminated area is less than the
derived limit.

 When measured ground surface concentrations in the contaminated area are available, the
daily exposure pattern, i.e., the indoor and outdoor time and exposure frequency (d/yr), is
specified, and radiation doses rather than operational guidelines are calculated for comparison
with the relocation PAG. This is the case when IND radionuclides are concerned.

 Operational guidelines/radiation doses are derived/calculated for four different receptors,
as discussed in the following sections. In the default case, the 0.5 rem per year relocation PAG is
applied as the dose criterion. An alternative dose criterion can be applied to derive operational
guidelines or can be used for comparison with the calculated radiation doses.

 101

7.1 WORKER ACCESS TO BUSINESSES FOR ESSENTIAL ACTIONS (GROUP D1)

 The radiation doses calculated or operational guidelines derived for this subgroup are
intended to aid in the implementation of protective actions that would allow workers access to
industrial, commercial, or other workplaces for limited periods of time to retrieve essential
materials or to perform essential functions (e.g., facility maintenance, animal care, and security
checks). Two potential exposure scenarios with 13 exposure pathways are considered in
deriving/calculating the operational guidelines/radiation doses. The exposure pathways are
summarized in Table 2.7 in Chapter 2. Both scenarios consider access to a workplace in an urban
environment, and the building dimensions used for calculating external exposures are based on
the warehouse geometry shown in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. The contamination is considered to
spread over the outdoor streets and roof and exterior walls of the warehouse, as well as the floor
and walls inside the warehouse. Because of the low traffic expected under the limited access
conditions, the value of the resuspension factor calculated with Equation (2.2) is used directly to
calculate the outdoor air concentration, i.e., the multiplication factor is set to 1.

 To cover the range of radiation doses associated with different time fractions spent inside
and outside of the contaminated buildings by different workers, two receptors are considered —
one that would spend all the time indoors and the other that would spend all the time outdoors.
The parameters used in different exposure scenarios are listed in Table 2.8 in Chapter 2.

7.1.1 Scenario Description for Worker Access to Businesses for Essential Actions

 Scenario D1-1 (outdoor worker accessing an essential business location) assumes access
to the contaminated area in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a worker is assumed to
spend all the time in the contaminated area outdoors. The outdoor worker would receive
radiation doses from external exposure to outside contamination (primarily the ground and, to a
much lesser extent, the air), inhalation of contaminated outdoor air, and direct ingestion of dust
from contaminated outdoor areas.

 Scenario D1-2 (indoor worker performing essential business inside the building) assumes
access to the contaminated area in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a worker is
assumed to spend all the time in the contaminated area inside a contaminated building. In the
default case, the building dimensions considered for external exposure are based on the
warehouse geometry shown in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. The indoor worker is exposed to external
radiation from the contaminated street, the contaminated exterior walls and roof, and the
contaminated interior walls and floor, also, to a much lesser extent, the contaminated indoor air,
as well as internal radiation due to inhalation of contaminated indoor air and direct ingestion of
dust from the contaminated floor (see Table 2.7 in Chapter 2).

7.1.2 Dose Calculation and Operational Guideline Derivation for Worker Access to
 Businesses for Essential Actions

 The total dose to a worker returning to the relocation area is calculated based on the
exposure parameters specified for that worker, including the number of hours staying inside or
outside per day, the number of days in the relocation area, the indoor or outdoor inhalation rate,

 102

the dust ingestion rate, and the exposure begin time. The exposure starts at the specified
exposure begin time. For RDD radionuclides, the exposure begin time is the time elapsed after
the measurement time. For IND radionuclides, both the exposure begin time and the
measurement time are times since the detonation of an IND. The exposure begin time should not
be earlier than the measurement time of the ground surface concentrations of IND radionuclides
in the relocation area, if the measured concentrations are specified for dose calculations. If rather
than measured IND radionuclide concentrations, the external dose rate and its measurement time
are specified for the code to determine radionuclide concentrations based on a uranium-fueled
IND-deposited radionuclide mix, the exposure begin time may be earlier than the measurement
time. For dose calculations, radiological ingrowth and decay and weathering are considered to
adjust radionuclide concentrations on different surfaces as a function of time.

 General operational guidelines for RDD radionuclides in terms of stay times are derived
based on a single trip to the contaminated area, representing the time limit a receptor can stay
there without incurring a radiation dose exceeding the relocation PAG. In application, the stay
time can be used as a restriction to the total amount of time of multiple trips during which a
person could remain in the contaminated area to conduct necessary activities. The stay times are
calculated for different initial concentration levels (corresponding to the measurement time) of
individual radionuclides or for multiple radionuclides with known initial concentrations in the
contaminated area. It should be noted that the stay times normally are not inversely proportional
to the initial concentrations because the dose rate changes with time.

7.2 PUBLIC ACCESS TO RESIDENCES FOR RETRIEVAL (GROUP D2)

 The radiation doses calculated for an RDD or IND event or operational guidelines for an
RDD event for this group are to aid in the implementation of protective actions that would allow
public access to residential areas for limited periods of time to retrieve essential properties such
as records, pets, or resources, or to conduct important maintenance activities. Two potential
exposure scenarios with 13 exposure pathways are considered for the dose
calculations/derivations of operational guidelines. The exposure pathways are summarized in
Table 2.7 in Chapter 2. Both scenarios consider access to a residence in an urban environment,
and the building dimensions used for calculating external exposures are based on the urban house
geometry shown in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. The contamination is considered to spread over the
outdoor streets, roof, and exterior walls of the urban house, as well as the floor and walls inside
the house. Because of the low traffic expected under the limited access conditions, the value of
the resuspension factor calculated with Equation (2.2) is used directly to calculate the outdoor air
concentration, i.e., the multiplication factor is set to 1.

 To cover the range of radiation doses associated with different time fractions spent inside
and outside of contaminated residences by the public, two receptors are considered — one that
would spend all the time indoors and the other that would spend all the time outdoors. The
parameters used for the two different exposure scenarios are listed in Table 2.8 in Chapter 2.

 103

7.2.1 Scenario Description for Public Access to Residences for Retrieval

 Scenario D2-1 (public accessing a residential area and staying outside) assumes access to
a contaminated residential area in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a resident is
assumed to spend all the time in the contaminated area outdoors. The resident would receive
radiation doses from external exposure to outside contamination (primarily the ground and, to a
much lesser extent, the air), inhalation of contaminated outdoor air, and direct dust ingestion
from contaminated outdoor areas.

 Scenario D2-2 (public accessing residential area and staying inside) assumes access to a
contaminated residential area in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a resident is
assumed to spend all the time in the contaminated area indoors. The building dimensions
considered for external exposure are based on the urban house geometry shown in Table 2.5 in
Chapter 2. The resident is exposed to external radiation from the contaminated street, the
contaminated exterior walls and roof, and the contaminated interior walls and floor, also, to a
much lesser extent, from the contaminated indoor air, as well as internal radiation due to
inhalation of contaminated indoor air and the direct ingestion of dust from the contaminated floor
(see Table 2.7 in Chapter 2).

7.2.2 Dose Calculation and Operational Guideline Derivation for Public Access to
 Residences for Retrieval

 The total dose to a resident returning to the relocation area after an RDD or IND event is
calculated based on the exposure parameters specified for that resident, including the number of
hours staying inside or outside per day, the number of days in the relocation area, the indoor or
outdoor inhalation rate, the dust ingestion rate, and the exposure begin time.

 General operational guidelines for RDD radionuclides in terms of stay times are derived
based on a single trip to the contaminated area, representing the time limit a receptor can stay
there without incurring a radiation dose exceeding the relocation PAG. In application, the stay
time can be used as a restriction to the total amount of time of multiple trips during which a
person could remain in the contaminated area exclusively indoors or outdoors to conduct
necessary activities. The stay times are provided for different initial concentration levels of
individual radionuclides or for multiple radionuclides with known initial concentrations in the
contaminated area. It should be noted that the stay times normally are not inversely proportional
to the initial concentrations because the dose rate changes with time.

 104

 8 GROUP E: TRANSPORTATION AND ACCESS ROUTES

 The operational guidelines or dose calculations for Group E are intended to assist in
determining whether contaminated bridges, streets and thoroughfares, or sidewalks and
walkways can be open for unrestricted or restricted access. The relocation PAGs (i.e., 2 rem in
the first year and 0.5 rem per year thereafter) were used as the basis for deriving operational
guidelines or as the basis for comparison with the calculated doses for unrestricted access. A
PAG of 5 rem was used for restricted access because it may be necessary to use the
transportation routes in a relocation area to commute between non-relocation areas or to provide
emergency/recovery services to the relocation area.

 The derivation of operational guidelines or the calculation of doses assumes regular or
periodic access to the transportation route. They are applicable during the intermediate response
phase after an RDD or IND event. Different scenarios are considered for deriving the operational
guidelines or calculating doses. They address continued use of bridges, streets and thoroughfares,
and sidewalks and walkways. In considering the use of bridges, streets, and thoroughfares; it is
assumed that the receptor would be inside a vehicle while driving through these areas and would
not come in direct contact with the contamination on the ground. The difference in the continued
use of bridges and streets and thoroughfares is in the external exposure: two supplementary
surface sources, representing the bridge railings, were added to the contaminated road source in
the bridge scenario. A methodology that accounts for the time-integrated concentration inside a
vehicle that passes through a contaminated area was used to assess the inhalation dose.

8.1 BRIDGES (GROUP E1)

 The operational guidelines derivation or dose calculations for this group are carried out
by considering continued (unrestricted) use of bridges by the general public or restricted use by
emergency workers. The 5 rem occupational exposure PAG is applied to emergency workers,
and the 2 rem first-year and 0.5 rem subsequent-year PAGs are applied to the general public for
operational guideline derivation. The time-integrated potential radiation doses over the exposure
duration resulting from external radiation (from the contaminated bridge), inhalation, and air
submersion exposure are considered. The time-integrated dose calculated over the exposure
duration of 1 year for the general public is to be compared with the 2 rem (20 mSv) first-year
PAG, and the time-integrated dose calculated over the exposure duration of 1 year after the first
year (i.e., second year and beyond) for the general public is to be compared with 0.5 rem (5 mSv)
subsequent-year PAGs. The radiation doses resulting from ingesting contaminated material
deposited inside the vehicle are not included. Both the general public and the emergency worker
scenarios are considered in an urban environment. The external exposure is calculated based on a
vehicle passing over a bridge with the geometry shown in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. The initial
contamination is assumed to be on the bridge. The resuspension factor is multiplied by 10 and
100, respectively, for restricted and public use (more traffic when access is unrestricted) to
account for traffic in an urban environment (see Table 2.4 in Chapter 2). The parameters used in
both exposure scenarios are listed in Table 2.8 in Chapter 2.

 105

8.1.1 Scenario Description for Bridges

 Scenario E1-1 (emergency worker drives over bridges under restricted conditions)
assumes use of the contaminated bridges by emergency workers in an urban environment. Under
this scenario, an emergency worker is assumed to spend an average of 1 hour per day for
250 days in 1 year passing over the contaminated bridges (multiple passages per day in
his vehicle).

 Scenario E1-2 (a member of the general public drives over bridges under unrestricted
conditions) assumes continued (unrestricted) use of the contaminated bridges by the general
public in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a member of the general public is assumed
to spend an average of 0.02 hour per day for 250 days in 1 year passing over contaminated
bridges twice daily in a vehicle. (The bridge is 500 m long, the vehicle speed is 50 km/h, and the
time taken to cross the bridge once is 0.01 hour; therefore, the twice-daily time/day is 0.02 hour.)

8.1.2 Dose Calculation and Operational Guideline Derivation for Bridges

 The total DSRs for both scenarios for the first year and the DSRs for Scenario E1-2 for
the second year are calculated for RDD radionuclides for operational guideline derivation. For
IND radionuclides, doses are calculated using the radionuclide concentrations from the default
IND event, a 10-kt uranium bomb, or from user-provided radionuclide concentrations. For RDD
radionuclides, doses are also calculated from the user-provided radionuclide concentrations.

 The operational guidelines for RDD radionuclides for the use of bridges for
Scenario E1-1 are derived using the occupational PAG (5 rem for the first year), and the
operational guideline for Scenario E1-2 is derived using the general public PAGs (2 rem for the
first year and 0.5 rem per year after the first year).

8.2 STREETS AND THOROUGHFARES (GROUP E2)

 The operational guidelines derivation or dose calculations for this group are carried out
by considering continued (unrestricted) use of streets and thoroughfares by the general public or
restricted use by emergency workers. The 5 rem occupational exposure PAG is applied to
emergency workers, and the 2 rem first-year and 0.5 rem subsequent-year PAGs are applied to
the general public for operational guideline derivation. The time-integrated potential radiation
doses over the exposure duration resulting from external radiation (from the contaminated streets
and thoroughfares), inhalation, and air submersion exposure are considered. The time-integrated
dose calculated over the exposure duration of 1 year for the general public is to be compared
with the 2 rem (20 mSv) first-year PAG, and the time-integrated dose calculated over the
exposure duration of 1 year after the first year (i.e., second year and beyond) for the general
public is to be compared with the 0.5 rem (5 mSv) subsequent-year PAGs. The radiation dose
resulting from ingesting contaminated material deposited inside the vehicle is not included. Both
the general public and the emergency worker scenarios are considered in an urban environment.
The external exposure is calculated based on a vehicle passing over a street with the geometry
shown in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. The initial contamination is assumed to be on the street. The
resuspension factor is multiplied by 10 and 100, respectively, for restricted and public use (more

 106

traffic when access is unrestricted), to account for traffic in an urban environment (see Table 2.4
in Chapter 2). The parameters used in both exposure scenarios are listed in Table 2.8 in
Chapter 2.

8.2.1 Scenario Description for Streets and Thoroughfares

 Scenario E2-1 (emergency worker drives through streets under restricted conditions)
assumes use of the contaminated streets by emergency workers in an urban environment. Under
this scenario, an emergency worker is assumed to spend an average of 1 hour per day for
250 days in 1 year passing through the contaminated streets (multiple times per day in a vehicle).

 Scenario E2-2 (member of the general public drives through streets under unrestricted
conditions) assumes continued (unrestricted) use of the contaminated streets by the general
public in an urban environment. Under this scenario, a member of the general public is assumed
to spend an average of 0.02 hour per day for 250 days in 1 year passing through the
contaminated streets twice daily in a vehicle. (The contaminated street is 500 m long, the vehicle
speed is 50 km/h, and the time taken to pass through the contaminated street once is 0.01 hour;
therefore, the twice-daily time/day is 0.02 hour.)

8.2.2 Dose Calculation and Guideline Derivation for Streets and Thoroughfares

 The total DSRs for both scenarios for the first year and the DSRs for Scenario E2-2 for
the second year are calculated for RDD radionuclides for operational guideline derivation. For
IND radionuclides, doses are calculated using the radionuclide concentrations from the default
IND event, a 10-kt uranium bomb, or from user-provided radionuclide concentrations. For RDD
radionuclides, doses are also calculated from the user-provided radionuclide concentrations.

 The operational guidelines for RDD radionuclides for the use of bridges for
Scenario E2-1 is derived using the occupational PAG (5 rem for first year), and the operational
guideline for Scenario E2-2 is derived using the general public PAGs (2 rem for the first year
and 0.5 rem per year after the first year).

8.3 SIDEWALKS AND WALKWAYS (GROUP E3)

 The operational guidelines derivation or dose calculations for this group are carried out
by considering continued (unrestricted) use of sidewalks and walkways by the general public or
restricted use by essential service workers. The 5 rem occupational exposure PAG is applied to
essential service workers, and the 2 rem first-year and 0.5 rem subsequent-year PAGs are applied
to the general public for operational guideline derivation. The time-integrated dose calculated for
the emergency worker is to be compared with the 5 rem (50 mSv) occupational exposure, and the
time-integrated dose calculated over the exposure duration of 1 year for the general public is to
be compared with the 2 rem (20 mSv) first-year PAG, and the time-integrated dose calculated
over the exposure duration of 1 year after the first year (i.e., second year and beyond) for the
general public is to be compared with 0.5 rem (5 mSv) subsequent-year PAGs. Three exposure
scenarios are considered for dose calculations. Potential radiation doses resulting from external

 107

radiation, inhalation, ingestion of dust particles, and air submersion exposure pathways are
considered. All scenarios are considered in an urban environment, and the external exposure is
calculated based on walking on a street with the geometry shown in Table 2.5 in Chapter 2. The
initial outdoor contamination on sidewalks and walkways is assumed to be the same as on the
streets. The resuspension factors are multiplied by 10 for restricted use and for public use in
situations where sidewalks or walkways are away from streets, and are multiplied by 100 for
public use in situations where sidewalks or walkways are near the streets, to account for traffic in
urban conditions (see Table 2.4 in Chapter 2). The parameters used in all three exposure
scenarios are listed in Table 2.8 in Chapter 2.

8.3.1 Scenario Description for Sidewalks and Walkways

 Scenario E3-1 (essential service worker uses sidewalks and walkways under restricted
conditions) assumes use of the contaminated sidewalks and walkways by essential service
workers in an urban environment. Under this scenario, an essential service worker is assumed to
spend an average of 1 hour per day for 250 days in 1 year on the contaminated sidewalks or
walkways near streets.

 Scenario E3-2 (volunteer collects donations near streets) assumes continued
(unrestricted) use of the contaminated sideways or walkways by people collecting donations in
an urban environment. Under this scenario, a member of the general public is assumed to spend
an average of 1 hour per day for 250 days in 1 year on contaminated sideways or walkways
near streets.

 Scenario E3-3 (vendor sells merchandise away from streets) assumes continued
(unrestricted) use of the contaminated sideways or walkways by vendors selling merchandise in
an urban environment. Under this scenario, a member of the general public is assumed to spend
an average of 8 hours per day for 250 days in 1 year on the contaminated sidewalks or walkways
away from streets.

8.3.2 Dose Calculation and Operational Guideline Derivation for Sidewalks and
 Walkways

 The total DSRs for three scenarios for the first year and the DSRs for Scenarios E3-2 and
E3-3 for the second year are calculated for RDD radionuclides for operational guideline
derivation. For IND radionuclides, dose is calculated using the radionuclide concentrations from
the default IND event, a 10-kt uranium bomb, or from user-provided radionuclide
concentrations. For RDD radionuclides, dose is also calculated from the user-provided
radionuclide concentrations.

 The operational guidelines for RDD radionuclides for the use of bridges for
Scenario E3-1 is derived using the occupational PAG (5 rem for first year), and the operational
guidelines for Scenarios E3-2 and E3-3 are derived using the general public PAGs (2 rem for the
first year and 0.5 rem after the first year).

 108

 9 GROUP F: RELEASE OF REAL PROPERTY FROM RADIOLOGICALLY
 CONTROLLED AREAS

 Group F operational guidelines govern the release of property from radiological control.
Property includes personal property (Subgroup F-1), debris and waste (Subgroup F-2), hazardous
waste (Subgroup F-3), and real property (Subgroup F-4). The operational guidelines for
Subgroup F-4 (release of buildings and lands) are discussed in this chapter. The operational
guidelines for Subgroup F-1 (release of contaminated vehicles) are discussed in Chapter 12.
Subgroups F-2 (release of debris and nonhazardous waste) and F-3 (release of hazardous waste)
are outside the scope of RESRAD-RDD&IND.

 In particular, the guidelines for real property — Subgroup F-4, which includes buildings
and land — are designed to facilitate the optimization process outlined in the protective action
guides (PAGs) for radiological dispersal device (RDD) and improvised nuclear device (IND)
incidents (Federal Register, 73 FR 149; DHS 2008).

 Unlike those of other subgroups, the F-4 guidelines do not prescribe a single numerical
criterion (such as a specific dose or risk level) for making protective action decisions. Instead,
they are applied within an optimization framework that considers multiple factors. These include
the type, magnitude, and distribution of contamination; the radionuclides involved; intended
future uses of the affected property; the urgency of recovery operations; public welfare and
social considerations; the cost and feasibility of cleanup options; and potential ecological
impacts.

 Group F-4 operational guidelines are provided as reference values (e.g., soil and
building-surface concentrations or risks) that can be used as a starting point for evaluating
optimization process attributes and impacts relative to a range of dose- or risk-based benchmarks
(e.g., 500, 100, 25, or 4 millirem per year [5, 1, 0.25, or 0.04 mSv per year]; comparative lifetime
risk ranges; and others) that could be considered as part of recovery-phase options. Thus, they
are not regulatory dose limits or criteria, but serve as concentration values that provide
perspective and aid in the optimization analyses.

 The operational guidelines for Subgroup F-4 were developed for use in releasing real
properties ⎯ soils and buildings ⎯ located within radiologically controlled areas after an RDD
or IND event. Operational guidelines that were developed for an IND event include only a subset
of radionuclides (Table 2.2 in Chapter 2) that have half-lives greater than 30 days but less than
400 days. The operational guidelines are developed for Am-241, Ce-141, Ce-143, Cf-252,
Cm-244, Co-58, Co-60, Cs-137, Ir-192, Mn-54, Po-210, Pu-238, Pu-239, Ra-226, Ru-103,
Ru-106, Sr-89, and Sr-90. Note that Cs-137 and Sr-90 are common in both RDD and IND
events. Because the distribution of radionuclides on different contaminated surfaces would have
changed from the original state as a result of any cleanup/remediation activities, the default ratios
of radionuclide concentrations among different contaminated surfaces assumed for Groups C−E
were not used. The operational guidelines for soil were derived by using the RESRAD-ONSITE
code (Yu et al. 2001) and considering only soil contamination. The operational guidelines for
buildings were derived by using the RESRAD-BUILD code (Yu et al. 2022) and considering that

 109

only building contamination existed on the interior floor, on the inside and outside of the
surrounding four walls, and on the roof.

 The operational guidelines for soil and buildings can be applied separately to release a
contaminated land area or building facility for unrestricted use if the actual contamination exists
only in the soil or on the building materials. If both the soil and the building materials are
contaminated, then the sum of fractions rule must be applied with the measured concentrations
for the soil and building materials to ensure that the PAG will not be exceeded after the
properties are released.

 Because of the uncertainty pertaining to the location of an RDD or IND event and the
affected population, probabilistic analyses were conducted to derive the operational guidelines.
In the probabilistic analyses, distribution functions were assigned to the RESRAD-ONSITE and
RESRAD-BUILD input parameters that were identified as sensitive in terms of influencing the
radiation dose results. Parameters not identified as sensitive were assigned RESRAD-ONSITE or
RESRAD-BUILD default values. To derive the operational guidelines, the 50th-percentile peak
DSRs and the mean plus two standard deviations of the peak DSRs were used, along with dose
criteria of 100 and 4 mrem/yr (1 and 0.04 mSv/yr). These operational guidelines were derived
considering the uncertainty of the location of a potential RDD or IND event and the building
types involved in an RDD or IND incident. In a real case, the probabilistic methodology
described in this chapter can be used to derive incident- and site-specific operational guidelines.
These operational guidelines are intended for use as a starting point for consideration within the
optimization process. Deterministic approaches using RESRAD-ONSITE or RESRAD-BUILD
codes (or other models) can also be used, particularly when more detailed site- and incident-
specific data are available.

 Appendix 9.A provides comparisons of the derived operational guidelines with published
screening criteria for release of real properties or contaminated materials. Appendix 9.B presents
distribution functions assigned to RESRAD-ONSITE and RESRAD-BUILD input parameters.
Appendix 9.B also discusses the different building characteristics assumed for deriving the
building operational guidelines and their impacts on the DSRs.

9.1 RELEASE OF SOILS

 To account for the uncertainty regarding the environmental setting of an RDD or IND
event and the use of the affected area, two resident and two worker scenarios were analyzed to
derive the soil release criteria. The two resident scenarios considered reflect two environmental
settings, rural and urban. For the rural setting, a subsistence farmer was assumed so that all
RESRAD-ONSITE exposure pathways (i.e., external radiation; inhalation of radon and dust; and
ingestion of soil, plant, meat, milk, fish, and water) could be analyzed. For the urban setting,
exposures through the ingestion of plant, meat, milk, fish, and water are unlikely in the late phase
of response after an RDD or IND event; therefore, only external radiation, inhalation of dust, and
ingestion of soil were analyzed. Both residential receptors were assumed to follow the living and
food consumption patterns of the general public. To consider different uses of the affected area,
two worker scenarios were selected to contrast the resident scenarios. The two worker scenarios,
one indoor and one outdoor, would bound the potential dose associated with industrial use of the

 110

affected area. All dose analyses were accomplished by using a probabilistic approach in which
the sensitive physical and behavioral parameters were assigned distribution functions so that the
distribution of potential doses could be analyzed. The sensitive parameters had been identified in
previous studies (Yu et al. 2000) and characterized as having a great influence on the calculated
dose results. Appendix 9.B lists the distribution functions used in the dose analyses. For
parameters that were not identified as sensitive, the RESRAD-ONSITE default values were used.
The dose analysis spans a time frame of 1,000 years due to considering long-term effects
resulting from leaching of radionuclides to groundwater. For IND events, the same
methodologies for RDD events can be used, but the dose analysis spans a short time frame of
only 2 years due to the short half-life of the IND radionuclides considered.

9.1.1 Scenario Description for Release of Soils

 9.1.1.1 Resident Scenarios

 In the subsistence farmer scenario, a farmer was assumed to settle on the contaminated
area after the release. The farmer built a house, grew plant foods, and raised livestock for
consumption. In addition, he drilled a well at the downgradient edge of the contaminated area
and pumped out groundwater for use. Occasionally, he fished in a nearby pond and consumed
the catch. All RESRAD-ONSITE exposure pathways were selected for dose calculations,
including external radiation; inhalation of dust particles and radon; and ingestion of plant foods,
meat, milk, soil, and groundwater.

 The area of contamination was assumed to be 10,000 m2. Over time, after an RDD or
IND event, the contaminants were assumed to be homogeneously mixed within the top 15 cm of
soil for the subsistence farmer scenario as an assumed result of weathering and human activities,
including root growth, plowing, shrinking and swelling cycles, rain drop impacts, or freezing-
thawing of the top layer of soils. Note that for some radionuclides, such as Co-58, Co-60,
Cs-137, and Ir-192, this 15 cm thickness may be a conservative estimate, especially for areas not
undergoing agricultural activities such as plowing and cultivating. National Council on Radiation
Protection and Measurements (NCRP) Report No. 129 (NCRP 1999) suggested a thickness of
5 cm as an effective depth of deposition contributing to resuspension. If data are available, the
actual thickness (and area) can be used in RESRAD-ONSITE to calculate site-specific doses and
operational guidelines.

 The sensitive physical parameters associated with the local environment and soils were
assigned distribution functions representing conditions throughout the country. The distribution
functions were obtained from Kamboj et al. (2020).

 On the basis of the area assumption and RESRAD-ONSITE’s default assumptions, 50%
of the plant foods, 50% of the fish, 50% of the meat and milk, and 100% of the drinking water
(if radionuclides reach the groundwater table) consumed by the farmer would be contaminated.
In the dose calculation, the assigned inhalation rate was a triangular distribution, with a
minimum of 4,380 m3/yr (0.5 m3/h), a maximum of 13,100 m3/yr (1.5 m3/h), and a mode of
8,400 m3/yr (0.96 m3/h). The soil ingestion rate also assumed a triangular distribution, with a
minimum, maximum, and mode of 0 g/yr (0 mg/d), 36.5 g/yr (100 mg/d), and 18.3 g/yr

 111

(50 mg/d), respectively. The inhalation and ingestion rates were consistent with the
recommendation from NUREG/CR-6697 (Yu et al. 2000) for the general public. The indoor time
fraction was assigned to a continuous distribution function (Yu et al. 2000; EPA 2011).
According to the distribution, the 50th-percentile value is 0.625, corresponding to 15 h/d. To
obtain realistically conservative dose estimates, the outdoor time fraction was also assigned a
continuous distribution function and was negatively correlated with the indoor time fraction such
that the sum with the indoor time fraction was close to 1. In other words, the receptor was
assumed to spend 100% of the time in the contaminated area.

 For the urban resident scenario, only the external radiation, the inhalation of dust
particles and radon, and the ingestion of soil pathways were considered. Ingestion of plant, meat,
milk, and fish, as well as the use of contaminated water, were excluded. All input parameters
used for dose calculations were the same as those used for the residential farmer scenario.

 9.1.1.2 Worker Scenarios

 Aside from residential use, industrial use of the contaminated area that could potentially
expose many workers was also considered. For the worker scenarios, exposure pathways
considered for dose calculations were external radiation, inhalation of dust particles and radon,
and ingestion of soil, which was the same as those for the urban resident scenario. However, the
time fraction a worker spent in the contaminated area was less than that spent by a resident. The
workers were assumed to be in the area 250 d/yr instead of 365 d/yr. The duration of work per
day assumed a continuous distribution, as recommended by NUREG/CR-6697 (Yu et al. 2000)
for workers, which was based on the distribution for full-time workers in plants, factories, or
warehouses (EPA 2011). The 50th-percentile value is 0.365, corresponding to 8.76 hours per
workday. To obtain bounding estimates of potential exposures, it was further assumed that a
worker worked either 100% indoors (indoor worker scenario) or 100% outdoors (outdoor
worker scenario).

 Because of different activity patterns, workers were assigned a higher inhalation rate than
residents. The triangular distribution assumed a minimum, maximum, and mode of 4,380 m3/yr
(0.5 m3/h), 16,790 (1.92 m3/h), and 12,264 m3/yr (1.4 m3/h), respectively (Yu et al. 2022). The
soil ingestion rate was the same as that for residents, which was the EPA’s recommendation for
adults (EPA 2011). The other input parameters were assigned values or distribution functions
that were the same as those for the residential scenarios.

9.1.2 Dose Calculation and Operational Guideline Derivation for Release of Soils

 The Latin hypercube sampling technique was used in sampling input parameter values.
A total of 1,500 sets of dose results were obtained (a specification of 500 observations and
3 repetitions).

 Operational guidelines were obtained by using the 50th-percentile values from the peak
DSR distributions with dose criteria of 100 and 4 mrem/yr (1 and 0.04 mSv/yr). The values for
the worker scenario are those for outdoor workers because the radiation doses the outdoor
workers receive would be greater than those received by indoor workers if they work the same

 112

amount of time in the contaminated area. The default operational guidelines for soils should be
based on the residential farmer scenario to ensure the maximum protection for human health.
However, the values can be reduced to reflect the actual and likely land use of the contaminated
area.

 The operational guidelines were derived by considering only exposures from
contaminated soils; potential exposures from contaminated buildings were not included.
Therefore, when applying the operational guidelines, it is important to make sure that the
assumption associated with the guidelines is met by the real contamination situation. For
instance, if both the soil and the building are contaminated, then the sum of fractions rule must
be used to determine compliance with operational guidelines. See Section 9.3 for a more detailed
discussion.

 The operational guidelines were derived through a probabilistic analysis to account for
the uncertainty in (1) the RDD or IND event location, (2) the physical conditions of the
contaminated area, and (3) the exposure patterns of the receptor. Conservative measures, such as
assuming the residents spend 100% of the time in the contaminated area, were incorporated in
the guideline derivation. In reality, when site-specific information is available, the level of
conservatism can be reduced by conducting a site-specific analysis using the RESRAD-ONSITE
code.

9.2 RELEASE OF BUILDINGS

 Two types of buildings were considered when deriving the operational guidelines:
residential buildings and commercial buildings. To account for the differences in the building
characteristics, three residential buildings (urban apartment, suburban house, and small rural
house) and two commercial buildings (large warehouse and small store/office) with different
building dimensions and shielding thicknesses were analyzed. The characteristics of the
buildings are described in Appendix 9.B.

 Contamination was assumed to exist on the interior floor, the inside and outside of the
four surrounding walls, and the roof. The contamination levels on all surfaces were assumed to
be equal, in order to safeguard against the uncertainty associated with cleanup/remediation
activities. A receptor staying inside a contaminated building (either a resident or a worker) was
assumed to incur radiation doses through (1) external radiation from the floor, interior and
exterior walls, and the roof; (2) inhalation of contaminated particles suspended from the floor
and interior walls; (3) ingestion of deposited dust particles; (4) external radiation from
submersion in contaminated air; (5) inhalation of radon released from the floor and interior walls
contaminated with radon precursors; and (6) external radiation from deposited dust particles.

 To account for the uncertainty in the type of receptor and in the receptor’s activities, a
probabilistic approach was used to quantify the distribution of potential radiation doses. The
RESRAD-BUILD parameters that were identified as being sensitive in terms of having great
influence on the dose results were assigned distribution functions obtained from Kamboj et al.
(2020). Appendix 9.B lists the distribution functions used in the analyses. The other parameters

 113

were assigned the RESRAD-BUILD default values. The most sensitive parameters on the basis
of the probabilistic analysis results are also discussed in Appendix 9.B.

9.2.1 Scenario Description for Release of Buildings

 9.2.1.1 Resident Scenario

 The receptors considered for residential buildings were residents of the buildings. The
indoor time fraction they spent in the buildings was assigned a continuous distribution function
(Yu et al. 2000; EPA 2011). According to the distribution, the 50th-percentile value is 0.625,
corresponding to 15 h/d. This assumption is consistent with that used for deriving soil
operational guidelines. The inhalation rate of the residents was assumed to have a triangular
distribution ranging from 12 to 35.9 m3/d (0.5 to 1.5 m3/h), with a mode at 23 m3/d (0.96 m3/d),
which is characteristic for the general public (EPA 2011). The indirect ingestion rate of
deposited dust particles was assigned a loguniform distribution with a minimum of 2.8E-5 m2/d
and a maximum of 2.9E-4 m2/d (Kamboj et al. 2020). According to the distribution, the average
ingestion rate of deposited dust particles is 1.1 cm2/h, which is very close to 1 cm2/h reported in
different studies (Healy 1971; Kennedy et al. 1981; ANSI/HPS 2013). Although the receptors
may stay at any location inside the building, for external dose calculations, they were assumed to
be at the center of the buildings.

 9.2.1.2 Worker Scenario

 The receptors considered for commercial buildings were workers working inside the
buildings. The time fraction they spent inside a building per workday was assumed to follow a
continuous distribution, as recommended in Kamboj et al. (2020). The 50th-percentile value is
0.365, corresponding to 8.76 hours per day. They were further assumed to work 250 days per
year. Their inhalation rate was assumed to be greater than that of residents, with a triangular
distribution ranging from 12 to 46 m3/d (0.5 to 1.92 m3/h) and a mode at 33.6 m3/d (1.4 m3/h).
The ingestion rate of dust particles was the same as that for residents, which is considered
conservative for use in risk assessments. Like the residents, the workers were assumed to be
positioned at the center of the commercial buildings for external dose calculations.

9.2.2 Dose Calculations and Operational Guideline Derivation for Release of Buildings

 The Latin hypercube sampling technique was used in sampling input parameter values.
The dose calculation was repeated 1,500 times (500 observations and 3 repetitions) for the three
different residential buildings and two different commercial buildings, and the dose results were
analyzed for statistical distribution.

 The operation guidelines were obtained by using the 50th-percentile and mean + 2
values, respectively, from the distributions of dose-to-source ratios and the dose criteria of
100 and 4 mrem/yr (1 and 0.04 mSv/yr). Measured concentrations on building surfaces, no
matter whether they are from the floor, interior walls, exterior walls, or roof, should be compared

 114

with the guideline values. A building can be released for unrestricted use after confirmation that
the contamination levels on all surfaces are less than the guideline values. To reduce the
conservatism associated with the release, a site-specific analysis can be conducted with measured
concentration data by using the RESRAD-BUILD code to demonstrate that the release dose
criterion is not exceeded.

 The operational guidelines were derived by considering only exposures from
contaminated buildings; potential exposures from contaminated soils outside the buildings were
not included. Therefore, when applying the operational guidelines, it is important to make sure
that the assumption associated with the guidelines is met by the real contamination situation.

9.3 APPLICATION OF THE SUM OF FRACTIONS RULE

 The operational guidelines were derived by considering a single contamination source in
soils and buildings independently. In reality, it is very likely that residual contamination would
exist both in the buildings and in the soils surrounding the buildings. Under such conditions, the
operational guidelines would need to be reduced to ensure that the total dose a potential receptor
would receive does not exceed the dose criterion. The revised operational guidelines (OG) would
need to satisfy the following expression:

 (OGsoil ) revised (OGbuilding ) revised
 + 1
 OGsoil OGbuilding

 APPENDIX 9.A:

  COMPARISON OF RELEASE OF REAL PROPERTY FROM RADIOLOGICALLY
 CONTROLLED AREA (GROUP F-4) OPERATIONAL GUIDELINES
 WITH PUBLISHED RELEASE OR CLEARANCE CRITERIA

 This appendix compares the Group F-4 operational guidelines with published screening
criteria for the release of real properties or contaminated materials. Tables 9.A.1 and 9.A.2 list
the comparison of the operational guidelines for soil with the U.S. Environmental Protection
Agency’s (EPA’s) soil screening levels (EPA 2000) and the International Atomic Energy
Agency’s (IAEA’s) Safety Guide No. RS-G-1.7 for clearance of material containing
radionuclides (IAEA 2004) for RDD and IND radionuclides. While the IAEA’s guideline values
are dose-based, the EPA’s screening levels are risk-based, which would protect human health
against a lifetime cancer risk of 1E-06. The dose criterion used to derive the IAEA’s
guidelines/standards is 1 mrem/yr. Note that the IAEA’s RS-G-1.7 guide also uses 100 mrem/yr
for radionuclides of natural origin and for low-probability events leading to higher radiation
exposures.

 The operational guidelines for residential and commercial buildings were compared with
the American National Standards Institute/Health Physics Society (ANSI/HPS) N13.12 standards
(ANSI/HPS 2013) for release of surface contaminated materials for RDD and IND radionuclides.
They are listed in Tables 9.A.3−9.A.6. The dose limits used in ANSI/HPS N13.12 standards
range from 1 to 10 mrem/yr.

 APPENDIX 9.B:

PARAMETERS USED IN DOSE ANALYSES

 125

 APPENDIX 9.B:

 PARAMETERS USED IN DOSE ANALYSES

 The distribution functions used in dose analyses are listed in this appendix. Tables 9.B.1
and 9.B.2 list the distribution functions used by RESRAD-ONSITE to consider soil
contamination. Depending on the dominant pathways, the most sensitive parameters for different
radionuclides are not the same. The most sensitive physical parameters include the soil/water
distribution coefficient (Kd), mass loading for inhalation, external gamma shielding factor,
contaminated zone erosion rate, density of the contaminated zone, saturated zone hydraulic
conductivity, saturated zone hydraulic gradient, unsaturated zone thickness, depth of roots, and
transfer factors for plants, meat, and milk. Exposure parameters such as time fraction indoors,
inhalation rate, soil ingestion rate, and drinking water intake rate also affect the dose results.

 Table 9.B.3 lists the distribution functions used by RESRAD-BUILD to consider
building contamination. It was found that in building occupancy scenarios, for strong gamma
emitting radionuclides (Co-60, Cs-137, and Ir-192), the indoor time fraction was the most
sensitive parameter and had the most influence on the calculated dose results. For other
radionuclides, in addition to the indoor time fraction, the building air exchange rate, removable
fraction, fraction of removable material released to air, and lifetime of removable fraction were
also identified as sensitive parameters.

 130

 10 GROUP G: FOOD CONSUMPTION

 Group G operational guidelines are designed to aid in decision making about the need for
placing restrictions on the consumption of potentially contaminated foods (excluding water) or
on agricultural activities during and following an RDD or IND incident. The water consumption
operational guidelines are discussed in Chapter 11.

 Four subgroups considering the safety of food consumption after an RDD or IND event
are included in Group G. The food concentrations and radiation doses calculated after an RDD or
IND event and the operational guidelines derived for RDD radionuclides for the subgroups are
intended to complement and supplement the derived intervention levels (DILs) published by the
U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) (FDA 1998).

 The first subgroup involves calculations of the limiting radionuclide concentrations or
planning values (PVs) in food using the FDA DILs methodology. To differentiate from the FDA-
published DILs, the derived limiting concentrations in food are called planning values (PVs).
Whenever there is a DIL published by the FDA for a radionuclide, the DIL is used in the
RESRAD-RDD&IND code for that radionuclide. The PVs are used only when there is not a DIL
published by the FDA.

 The second and third subgroups involve calculations of the concentration in food
resulting from contamination in surface soil that supports the production of the food. The second
subgroup concerns foods (vegetables and milk) that are growing or produced by livestock that
feed on forage that are growing in agriculture fields during the plume phase of an RDD or IND
event, whereas the third subgroup concerns food (leafy vegetables, non-leafy vegetables, meat,
and milk) that are planted or produced by livestock that feed on forage regrowing in agriculture
fields shortly after the plume phase of an RDD or IND event. The projected radionuclide
concentrations in food are intended for comparison with the limiting concentrations (i.e., PVs or
DILs) in food from the first subgroup to define protective actions. When the “General
Guidelines” option is selected for RDD radionuclides, the PVs or DILs are set as the target
radionuclide concentrations in plant food and livestock products to derive the operational
guidelines for the subgroup (2 or 3), which are the limiting radionuclide concentrations in
surface soils.

 The fourth subgroup involves the calculation of radionuclide concentrations in food that
are linked to contamination in soil after the plume phase and the calculation of radiation doses
that would be incurred over 1 year associated with consuming the food (leafy vegetables, non-
leafy vegetables, meat, and milk). The projected radiation dose is intended for comparison with a
food PAG (500 mrem/yr as default) to define protective actions. As with Subgroup 3,
Subgroup 4 also involves deriving operational guidelines for RDD nuclides in terms of limiting
concentrations in surface soils of the impacted agriculture field. However, rather than using PVs
or DILs as the target radionuclide concentrations in food (plant food and livestock products), a
radiation dose of 500 mrem/yr is used as the target annual dose for the consumption of the
contaminated food.

 131

 It is important to note that the calculation results of Group G are for use in determining
the safety associated with consuming agricultural products (leafy vegetables, non-leafy
vegetables, meat, and milk) produced from an impacted area. The radiation exposure that would
result from working in the contaminated field to cultivate crops and/or raise livestock is not
considered. Therefore, Group G results should be used in conjunction with Group C results to
determine whether farmers can continue to live in the affected area to work on the farm to
produce food that is safe for consumption.

10.1 SUBGROUP 1 LIMITING CONCENTRATION IN FOOD FOR THE EARLY
 PHASE

 For Subgroup 1, the DILs derived by the FDA (FDA 1998), or the PVs when DILs are
not available, are used as operational guidelines. The same methodology used by the FDA for
developing the DILs is used to derive PVs, with one difference — updated dose coefficients are
used.

 The FDA DIL for a specific radionuclide was calculated by using Equations 10.1−10.3:

 PAG
 DIL =
                                               f  FI  DC                                    (10.1)
 FI = Annual Intake Rate  IPC (10.2)

 Ln(0.01) 
 IPC = min 1 or −
   
 (10.3)

where:

 DIL = derived intervention level (Bq/kg);

 DC = dose coefficient, the radiation dose received per unit of radioactivity ingested
 (mSv/Bq)

 f = contamination fraction of the food ingested

 IPC = ingestion period correction (yr)

 λ = radionuclide decay constant (yr-1)

 FI = food intake, quantity of food consumed in an appropriate period of time
 determined by the IPC (kg); and

 PAG = protective action guide for food ingestion (mSv).

 Two PAGs — 500 mrem (5 mSv) for committed effective dose and 5 rem (50 mSv) for
committed dose equivalent to an individual tissue or organ — and receptors of six different age

 132

groups (3 months, 1 year, 5 years, 10 years, 15 years, and adult [>17 years]) were used by the
FDA (Note: The protection action guide from the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency [EPA]
for food consumption is 500 mrem for the first year after a radiological emergency incident.) The
FDA DILs were initially calculated for nine radionuclides (I-131, Cs-134, Cs-137, Ru-103, Ru-
106, Sr-90, Pu-238, Pu-239, and Am-241), among which Cs-137, Sr-90, Pu-238, Pu-239, and
Am-241 are RDD radionuclides. The food ingestion rates were based on the “entire diet” for
each age group. The entire diet included eight different food classes — dairy, egg, meat, fish,
produce, grain, beverage, and miscellaneous. The “dairy” class included fresh milk, and the
“beverage” class included “tap water.” In deriving DILs, it was assumed that 30% of the food
intake was contaminated (i.e., f = 0.3 in Equation 10.1). An exception was made for I-131 in the
diets of the 3-month and 1-year age groups, for which the entire intake over a 60-day period was
assumed to be contaminated. Table 10.1 provides the annual intake rates for the six age groups
considered in deriving DILs.

 Dose coefficients used by the FDA were from the International Commission on Radiation
Protection (ICRP) Publication 56 (ICRP 1989). In the derivation of DILs, a correction for
radiological decay was not applied. However, the intake period was selected to be either 1 year
or the time required for a radionuclide to decay to ~1% of its initial activity, whichever is
smaller. As a result, for short-lived radionuclides, the intake period considered could be less than
1 year, and the yearly intake rate was adjusted by the intake period correction factor (IPC in
Equation 10.2) to get the total quantity of food consumed, FI. None of the radionuclides
concerned for an RDD event in the RESRAD-RDD&IND code has a half-life short enough to
require this correction. Appendix D in the FDA report (FDA 1998) describes in detail how DILs
were calculated and lists the values of the dose coefficients used. Among all the DILs of
different age groups and PAG combinations that were calculated, the most limiting one was
recommended by the FDA for use after a nuclear incident.

 Later, the FDA calculated DILs for 15 more radionuclides by using the same equations.
Among all the radionuclides considered by the FDA (Appendix E, FDA 1998), six are RDD
radionuclides and seventeen are IND radionuclides, with Pu-239 being both an RDD and IND
radionuclide. Table 10.2 lists the FDA DILs for these radionuclides.

 In deriving the DILs, the FDA followed the Table 10.1 Dietary Intake
principles and general guidance provided by ICRP Rates for Different Age
Publication 40 (ICRP 1984). The ICRP has since updated Groups
its general concepts on intervention and has documented
them in ICRP Publication 63 (ICRP 1991). The PVs for the Annual Intake
RDD/IND radionuclides without a DIL are derived based Age Group Rate (kg/yr)
on the FDA methodology described previously, but with
the updated concept (ICRP 1991) and the dose coefficients 3 months 418
 1 year 506
of ICRP-72 (ICRP 1995) or DCFPAK3.02. The dietary 5 years 660
intake rates used for different age groups are the same as 10 years 779
those listed in Table 10.1. The most restrictive value was 15 years 869
selected to be the final limiting concentration in food for Adult 943
Subgroup 1.

 133

 The final DILs/PVs are listed as food concentration limits for the early phase in the text
report generated by the RESRAD-RDD&IND code after it completes the calculations for
Group G. Because Subgroups G-2 and G-3 involve the production of plant food and livestock
products for consumption and the code estimates and reports the concentrations of both parent
radionuclides and their progenies in different food items, the DILs/PVs of progeny radionuclides
are also listed in the text report to facilitate the decision making on consuming these food items.
Measured radionuclide concentrations in plant food or animal products produced from the
affected area after an RDD or IND event should be compared with the final DILs/PVs to
determine whether interdiction is necessary for consumption of the food.

10.2 SUBGROUP 2 FOOD CONCENTRATIONS AND SOIL OPERATIONAL
 GUIDELINES FOR THE EARLY PHASE

 The calculated food concentrations or derived soil operational guidelines for Subgroup 2
are intended for use in the early phase of a response after an RDD or IND event. Vegetables and
milk that are produced shortly after an RDD or IND event are the primary concerns for public
consumption. To derive surface soil concentration limits as operational guidelines, the
relationship between surface soil concentrations and the concentrations in vegetables and
fodder/grass that have been growing during the plume phase of the RDD event and the
concentrations in milk that is produced by livestock feeding on these fodder/grass are established
as shown in Figure 10.1.

 Figure 10.1 Flow Diagram Showing Relationships among Concentrations in
 Vegetables/Milk and Concentrations in Soil for Subgroup 2 Scenarios

 The following equations are used to model radionuclide concentrations in different types
of vegetation and livestock products based on the concentrations in surface soil, which are
functions of the time (t) of concern:

 or harvest, and k index is used at the time of harvest;

 p = index for plant type, 1 for vegetables and 2 for fodder/grass;

 𝐶𝑖,𝑠𝑜𝑖𝑙 = deposited concentration of radionuclide i in surface soil (pCi/m2);

 𝑡𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒 = the base time, corresponding to the initial concentrations of radionuclides
 deposited to the ground surface from the plume, which is also the beginning
 time for the modeling (hr);

 𝑓𝑑,𝑝 = deposition retained on foliage for plant type p;

 𝑌𝑝 = productivity of plant type p (kg/m2);

 𝑡𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑧𝑒 = time period from the base time to the time livestock graze in the field (d);

 𝑡ℎ𝑎𝑟𝑣𝑒𝑠𝑡 = time period from the base time to the time vegetables are harvested (d);

 𝐼𝐷𝑖𝑗 (∆𝑡) = ingrowth and decay factor = ratio of the activity of progeny j to the initial
 activity of parent nuclide i after a period of ∆𝑡 (d);

 ∆𝑡 = elapsed period of time between the two time points of concern (d);

 𝑓𝑟 = fraction of foliar deposition remained at the grazing or harvest time;

 𝐹𝐼𝑝=2,𝑞=1 = daily fodder intake rate by milk cows (kg/d);

 𝐹𝑄𝑅𝑗,𝑞=1 = milk transfer factor for radionuclide j = ratio of the concentration of
 radionuclide j in milk to the daily intake rate of radionuclide j via fodder
 ingestion by livestock (pCi/kg)/(pCi/d);

 𝑡𝑚𝑎𝑟𝑘𝑒𝑡 = time period from the time livestock graze in the field to the time milk is
 delivered to the market (d); and

 136

 𝑡𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑠𝑢𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 = time period from the time vegetables are harvested to the time they are
 consumed (d).

 The base time corresponds to the initial concentration of radionuclides that are deposited
to the ground surface/foliage when the foliage-to-plant translocation is modeled, as shown in
Figure 10.1. It is determined by the option (direct or indirect) with which the initial
concentrations are specified. With the direct option, radionuclide concentrations at the
measurement time are specified. The specified measured concentrations are used as the initial
concentrations, and the measurement time is the base time, i.e., as t = 0 in the modeling. The
direct option is the only option available in the RDD component; in the IND component, the
direct option is activated by checking the box for “Edit Table” in the interface and then entering
the measured concentrations for specific IND nuclides in the table below. The indirect option is
only available in the IND component; it is the default option for IND radionuclides. With this
default option, the external dose rate and the corresponding measurement time after the
detonation of an IND are specified in the interface. The RESRAD-IND&IND code then uses
these inputs to estimate the initial concentrations of radionuclides present in the plume induced
by the detonation of an IND that are deposited to the ground surface, corresponding to 1 hour
after the detonation. In this case, the base time is the time the initial deposition is assumed
completed (1 hour after the detonation), and the estimated deposited concentrations are the initial
concentrations used in the modeling. In addition to estimating the initial deposited
concentrations, the code also estimates the nuclide concentrations on the ground surface at the
measurement time and lists them in the interface. However, these listed concentrations are not
used in the modeling.

 For Subgroup 2, plant/fodder are assumed to have been growing in agriculture fields
during an RDD or IND event. Because of the passage of the plume, the deposition of
radionuclides retained on foliage could be comparable to the deposition on surface soil, making
uptake through foliar deposition the primary mechanism that contributes to plant food/fodder
contamination. By default, plant food/fodder is assumed to be harvested/grazed by livestock
immediately after the passage of the plume; if there is a lag in time, radiological decay and
ingrowth are considered to adjust the equilibrium concentrations in plant food/fodder due to
foliar deposition. The concentrations in fodder are then used to estimate the equilibrium
concentrations in milk produced by livestock with the nuclide-specific milk transfer factors. The
concentrations in plant food at the harvest time and the concentrations in milk at the production
time are then adjusted by considering radiological decay and ingrowth over the time to market or
to consumption to get the concentrations in plant foods and milk at the consumption time, which
are listed in the text report generated by the RESRAD-RDD&IND code. The projected
concentrations in plant food and milk at the consumption time are intended for comparison with
the PVs/DILs to support protective actions. If the projected food concentrations are greater than
the PVs or DILs, then restriction should be considered regarding the consumption of crops or
animal products that are harvested or produced shortly after the plume phase from the RDD- or
IND-impacted agricultural area.

 To derive the operational guidelines for each RDD radionuclide, an initial (at the
measurement time) unit concentration is assumed for the radionuclide in surface soil to evaluate
the resulting concentrations of each nuclide (including parent and progenies) in the decay chain
in each type of food at the consumption time. The ratio of the progeny concentration in each type

 137

of food to its PV or DIL is then calculated and used to derive the limiting initial concentration of
the parent nuclide in surface soil. The smallest limiting initial concentration among the values
derived concerning each progeny in the decay chain is selected to be the final operational
guideline for the parent RDD nuclide. A final operation guideline is derived for each type of
food. These derived final operational guidelines are presented in the text report, if the “General
Guidelines” option is selected in the interface as the result type when running the RESRAD-
RDD&IND code.

 In the default case, the plant food (considered as leafy vegetables) is assumed to have a
productivity of 2 kg/m2 (Yu et al. 2001), while the productivity of fodder/grass is assumed to be
0.7 kg/m2. The deposited concentrations retained on the foliage are assumed to be 20% and 50%
for vegetables and fodder, respectively, of the initial deposited concentrations in soil. To be
conservative, the level of deposition on the foliage is assumed to stay the same up to the graze
time (for fodder) or the harvest time (vegetables); the default value for “Fraction of Deposition
Remaining at Harvest Time” is 1. The animal product is modeled as milk; all the fodder/grass
consumed by milk cows (a default consumption rate is 55 kg/d) are assumed to be contaminated.
The milk transfer factors used in the modeling are also from Yu et al. (2001). For each RDD
radionuclide, two operational guidelines are reported based on the projected concentration in
vegetables and milk. If both types of food could be produced from the same impacted field, the
more conservative (i.e., smaller) value between these two should be selected for making
decisions in the early response phase after an RDD event.

10.3 SUBGROUP 3 FOOD CONCENTRATIONS AND SOIL OPERATIONAL
 GUIDELINES FOR THE INTERMEDIATE PHASE

 In Subgroup 3, the concentrations in different types of plant food and fodder are related
to the deposition concentrations in surface soil by considering that plant foods are cultivated and
the fodder regrows after the plume phase. The new fodder is then grazed by livestock resulting in
the production of contaminated meat and milk. Under such considerations, the uptake through
the foliar deposition mechanism is much less significant than that considered in Subgroup 2 for
the same level of deposition in surface soil because, rather than receiving deposition of
radioactivity directly from a passing plume, the radioactivity on foliage would result from the
deposition of airborne radionuclides, which would result from the resuspension of radionuclides
in surface soil, as shown in the flow diagram in Figure 10.2.

functions of time (t):

 The base time corresponds to the initial concentrations of radionuclides that are deposited
to the ground surface and is the beginning time of the modeling. The designation of base time in
Subgroup 3 modeling is the same as in Subgroup 2 modeling, based on the option (direct or
indirect) with which the initial concentrations are specified, as discussed in Section 10.3.

 To project radionuclide concentrations in different types of food at the time of
consumption, further modeling is needed with the concentrations in plant food, 𝐶𝑗,𝑝 , at the
grazing time or harvest time and the volume concentrations in soil, 𝐶𝑗,𝑣𝑠𝑜𝑖𝑙 , at the grazing time, as
shown in Figure 10.2. Figure 10.3 depicts the modeling process.

 𝑆𝐼 = daily soil ingestion rate of livestock (kg/d);

 𝐹𝑄𝑅𝑗,𝑞 = meat (q=1) or milk (q=2) transfer factor for radionuclide j = ratio of the

 ∆𝑡 = elapsed time between the two time points of concern (d); and

  𝑡𝑠𝑡𝑜𝑟𝑎𝑔𝑒,𝑝 , 𝑡𝑠𝑡𝑜𝑟𝑎𝑔𝑒,𝑞 = storage time of food item p or q for human consumption (d).

 For Subgroup 3, the DILs or PVs from Subgroup 1 are used to determine protective
measures, i.e., to decide whether a contaminated field can be used to produce food that is safe for
consumption after the plume phase of an RDD or IND event. Therefore, the estimated
concentration of each radionuclide in leafy vegetables, non-leafy vegetables, meat, and milk at
the consumption time — estimated based on the initial contamination in the field — should be
compared with its DIL or PV. The estimated concentrations of radionuclides are listed in the text
report generated by the RESRAD-RDD&IND code for IND radionuclides. For RDD
radionuclides, the concentrations are listed only for the “measurement-based guideline” option.

 The operational guidelines, i.e., the limiting initial concentration of each radionuclide
deposited in surface soil, are derived for RDD radionuclides for the “General Guidelines” option.
The procedure to derive the operational guidelines is the same as that described in the previous
section (Section 10.4) for Subgroup 2.

 The default parameter values used in the modeling of plant food, fodder, meat, and milk
concentrations are listed below:

 1. The growing period for leafy vegetables is 0.25 year; for grain and non-leafy
 vegetables, it is 0.17 year; and for fodder, it is 0.08 year.

 2. The ingestion rate of fodder by meat cows is 68 kg/d; by milk cows, it is 55 kg/d.

 3. The ingestion rate of soil by meat and milk cows is 0.5 kg/d.

 4. The storage times for grain and non-leafy vegetables, leafy vegetables, meat, and
 milk are 14, 1, 20, and 1 day, respectively.

 5. The depth of the soil mixing layer is the plow depth of 0.15 m.

 6. The depth of roots is the same as the plow depth, 0.15 m.

       7. The soil density is 1.5 g/cm3 = 1,500 kg/m3.

 The values of the root uptake transfer factors (𝐵𝑗,𝑣 ), the fodder/soil intake-to-meat
transfer factor (𝐹𝑄𝑅𝑗,1 ), and the fodder/soil intake-to-milk transfer factor (𝐹𝑄𝑅𝑗,2 ) are the same
as the values used in the RESRAD-ONSITE (Yu et al. 2001) and RESRAD-OFFSITE (Yu et al.
2020) codes, so are the default values of the above input parameters except for the depth of roots.
The default depth of the roots is 0.9 m in RESRAD-ONSITE and RESRAD-OFFSITE, but in

 143

RESRAD-RDD&IND, it is set to the same depth as the soil mixing layer, 0.15 m. This allows
estimates of more conservative (i.e., higher) concentrations in food products and the derivation
of more restrictive (i.e., lower) operational guidelines for surface soil.

10.4 SUBGROUP 4 RADITAION DOSES AND SOIL OPERATIONAL GUIDELINES
 FOR THE INTERMEDIATE-TO-LATE PHASE

 Subgroup 4 also deals with the ingestion of contaminated food produced with
contaminated soil in an RDD- or IND-impacted area after the plume phase. The potential
radiation dose associated with consuming each or multiple types of contaminated food is
projected for comparison with a food PAG of 500 mrem/yr (5 mSv/yr). For RDD radionuclides,
the soil operational guidelines are derived based on the food PAG rather than on DILs or PVs, as
the soil operational guidelines of Subgroup 3 are derived. The radiation doses and operational
guidelines of Subgroup 4 are intended for use during the intermediate-to-late phases of the
response after an RDD or IND event.

 Although it is likely that contaminated food could be produced from an impacted field at
different times throughout a year and contain different levels of radioactivity, to be conservative
in dose calculations, representative radioactivity levels are set to be those in the plant food from
the first harvest and those in the meat and milk produced by livestock grazing on fodder that are
mature in the first growth period after the plume phase. That is, the radionuclide concentrations
in food products projected for Subgroup 3 are used to estimate the annual radiation dose from
ingestion of contaminated food. To evaluate how radiation exposure through food ingestion
would decrease over time, the input parameter “exposure begin time” can be varied over the
1-year period.

 Radiation doses associated with ingesting different types of food can then be calculated
with the representative concentrations in the food at the time of consumption:
 𝑚

 𝑘=1

 𝑚

 𝑘=1

where:

  𝐷𝑖𝑛𝑔,𝑖,𝑝 , 𝐷𝑖𝑛𝑔,𝑖,𝑞 = annual dose from ingestion of leafy vegetables (p = 1), non-leafy vegetables
 (p = 2), meat (q = 1), or milk (q = 2), attributed to parent radionuclide i that
 is in surface soil at the base time (mrem/yr);

 𝐹𝐼𝑝 , 𝐹𝐼𝑞 = annual ingestion rate of leafy vegetables (p = 1), non-leafy vegetables
 (p = 2), meat (q = 1), or milk (q = 2) (a density of 1 kg/L is assumed for

 𝑓𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑡 = contaminated fraction of food ingested;

 𝐷𝐶𝐹𝑖𝑛𝑔,𝑘 = ingestion dose coefficient of radionuclide k, a decay progeny of radionuclide
 i (mrem/pCi).

 When the ingestion dose results are presented in the text report, the radiation doses
contributed by each radionuclide (including parent and progeny) in each type of food are listed.
To derive operational guidelines (only for RDD radionuclides with the “General Guidelines”
option), radiation doses contributed by each radionuclide in a decay chain are added as shown in
Equations 10.17 and 10.18. The total dose from the entire decay chain is then used to derive the
limiting initial soil concentration of the parent nuclide that would result in a total dose of
500 mrem/yr, i.e., the PAG value. Six operational guidelines are derived for each parent nuclide,
considering the ingestion of only one type of food (leafy vegetables, non-leafy vegetables, meat,
or milk), the ingestion of both leafy vegetables and non-leafy vegetables, and the ingestion of all
four types of food products.

 Besides the input parameters discussed in Section 10.4, the modeling of ingestion dose in
Subgroup 4 requires the use of ingestion rate parameters for different types of food. An average
adult is considered as the public receptor who is assumed to ingest 160 kg/yr of grain and non-
leafy vegetables, 14 kg/yr of leafy vegetables, 92 L/yr of milk, and 63 kg/yr of meat. Of the food
ingested per year, 30% is assumed to be contaminated, which is the same as the assumption used
by the FDA to derive DILs. These annual consumption rates are consistent with the RESRAD-
ONSITE and RESRAD-OFFSITE default values.
