 11 GROUP H: WATER CONSUMPTION

11.1 INTRODUCTION

 For Group H, the RESRAD-RDD&IND code calculates radiological dose from ingested
given water concentrations for all age groups. It can also calculate the corresponding water
concentrations at specified PAG dose criteria for different age groups. The methodology for dose
calculations for drinking water consumption is presented in this chapter. The calculated water
concentrations are in terms of limiting concentrations of drinking water for different age groups.
The age groups considered include fetus, breastfed infant, infant, 1 year, 5 years, 10 years,
15 years, and adult. The limited water concentrations are developed by considering the
consumption of water through the water ingestion pathway. The ingestion of drinking water from
affected areas would not result in a radiation dose exceeding the corresponding drinking water
PAGs, if a single radionuclide concentration in the drinking water is below the Group H
calculated limiting concentration. If multiple radionuclides are present, the sum of fractions rule
is applied in determining whether the radiation dose exceeds PAGs or not.

 The limiting water concentrations were derived by using a methodology consistent with
the one used by the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) for developing the derived
response levels (DRLs) for drinking water after a radiological contamination incident that affects
drinking water supplies (EPA 2017). It assumes the contamination remains present for a 1 year
period (for a fetus, it is 280 days). It considers potential radiation exposure only from water
ingestion and is based on a drinking water PAG of either 5 mSv/yr (500 mrem/yr) committed
effective dose (CED) to the general population or 1 mSv/yr (100 mrem/yr) to a sensitive group,
whichever is more limiting. The drinking water PAG is used mainly for planning and decision
making by local or State officials during the intermediate phase of radiological emergencies
when surface water sources are vulnerable to contamination from the deposition of radioactive
material from the atmosphere (EPA 2017). The PAG specifies a radiation dose to avoid via
drinking water exposure projected over 1 year. Note that the water concentration would often not
remain at the peak contamination levels for a 1 year duration.

 Age-dependent water ingestion rates and ingestion dose conversion factors (DCFs) were
used in calculating drinking water ingestion doses and limiting drinking water concentrations. It
is important to note that Group H water concentrations were developed by considering radiation
exposure only through the water ingestion pathway. They can be used to determine whether
ingestion of water would result in an unacceptable dose. Because radiation exposure that would
result from other exposure pathways was not considered, Group H limiting water concentrations
are used to determine whether restrictions on water ingestion or agricultural activities should be
imposed on a contaminated area where residents are allowed to stay after an RDD or IND event.

11.2 DRINKING WATER CONSUMPTION DOSE CALCULATIONS

 The derivation of a drinking water concentration operational guideline that will result in
an applicable PAG dose criterion was based on the methodology described in the EPA PAG
Manual (EPA 2017). The drinking water operational guideline is called the derived response

 147

level (DRL) in the EPA PAG Manual. It is used to determine the impact to drinking water
supplies after a radiological contamination incident.

 The DRLa for a specific radionuclide for age group a was calculated by using
Equation 11.1:
 𝑃𝐴𝐺𝑎
                                  𝐷𝑅𝐿𝑎 =                                                       (11.1)
 𝑇𝑎 × 𝐼𝑛𝑔𝑎 × 𝐷𝐶𝐹𝑎

where:

 DRLa = derived response levels (Bq/L or pCi/L) for age group a;

 DCFa = dose conversion factor, the radiation dose received per unit of radioactivity
 ingested (mSv/Bq or mem/yr) for age group a;

 Ta = time period in days for drinking water for age group a (note this is not a user
 input parameter, but is fixed at 365 days for all age groups except for a fetus
 where it is 280 days);

 Inga = water intake rate (L/day) for age group a; and

 PAGa = protective action guide (mSv or mrem) for drinking water ingestion for age
 group a.

 The dose for a specific radionuclide for age group a due to drinking water ingestion was
calculated by using Equation 11.2:

                                  𝐷𝑎𝑇 = 𝐶 × 𝐼𝑛𝑔𝑎 × 𝑇𝑎 × 𝐷𝐶𝐹𝑎                                   (11.2)

where:

 DaT = dose (mSv/yr or mrem/yr) due to ingestion of radionuclide to age group a;

 C = radionuclide concentration in drinking water (Bq/L or pCi/L). It was assumed that
 water concentration will not change over the exposure period (i.e., no decay or
 ingrowth is considered).

 Two PAGs were used in the default calculation: 1 mSv/yr (100 mrem/yr) for the sensitive
age group and 5 mSv/yr (500 mrem/yr) for the general population. In the calculation, age-
dependent drinking water intake rates and dose conversion factors were used. The age groups
considered were fetus, breastfed infant, infant, 1 year, 5 years, 10 years, 15 years in the sensitive
age group, and adult (> 17 years).

 The default water intake rates used are the recommended values in the EPA PAG Manual
(EPA 2017). Table 11.1 lists the water intake rates for males and females in different age groups.
The values are based on the mean drinking water ingestion rates in Table 3.1 of the FGR 13
report (EPA 1999).

 The ingestion rates for males are higher than the values for females; therefore, the EPA
used the intake rates for males to represent each age group in the calculations. For pregnant

value in the adult category [age 20–75 years]) was used. The daily default water intake rates used
in different age groups are: infant – 0.191 L/d, 1 year – 0.223 L/d, 5 years – 0.542 L/d, 10 years
– 0.725 L/d, 15 years – 0.9 L/d, adult – 1.643 L/d, pregnant women – 1.643 L/d, and nursing

 In the RESRAD-RDD&IND code, the user can select from dose conversion factor (DCF)
options: STD-1196-2022 (DOE 2022), DCFPAK 3.02, or ICRP-72 (ICRP 1995). No matter
which of these options is selected, the ingestion DCFs used for a fetus are from International
Commission on Radiation Protection (ICRP) Publication 88 (ICRP 2002), and for a breastfed
infant, the ingestion DCFs from ICRP Publication 95 (ICRP 2005) are used. If the DCFs for the
fetus and breastfed infant are not available, then the infant dose conversion factors are used for
both the fetus and the breastfed infant. For other age groups, the ingestion DCFs are taken from a
user-selected DCF database: DOE-STD-1196-2022 (DOE 2022), DCFPAK3.02, or ICRP-72
(ICRP 1995).

 If multiple radionuclides are present in the water supply, the measured concentration of
each radionuclide should be divided by its respective limiting water concentration and summed.
If the sum of the ratios for all radionuclides is less than 1, then the total dose does not exceed the
PAG value.

 If multiple radionuclides are present in the water supply, the RESRAD-RDD&IND code
calculates the total dose for different age groups from all contaminants present, as shown in
Equation 11.3:

 𝐷𝑎𝑇,𝑇𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 = ∑ 𝐷𝑎𝑇,𝑖 = ∑ 𝐶𝑖 × 𝐼𝑛𝑔𝑎 × 𝑇𝑎 × 𝐷𝐶𝐹𝑎,𝑖 (11.3)

 149

where:

 DaT,Total = total dose from all radionuclides in the water supply (mSv or mrem) for age
 group a;

 DCFa,i = dose conversion factor (mSv/Bq or mem/yr) for radionuclide i for age group a;

 DaT,i = dose (mSv/yr or mrem/yr) due to ingestion of radionuclide i to age group a;

 Ci = concentration of radionuclide i in drinking water (Bq/L or pCi/L).

 The calculated total dose should not exceed the PAG value for each group.

 12 ADDITIONAL EXPOSURE SCENARIOS AND OPERATIONAL GUIDELINES

 During the development of operational guidelines for Groups A through H for responses
to an RDD event, some additional exposure scenarios were analyzed and the corresponding
operational guidelines were derived. These potential exposure scenarios included flushing
contaminated streets, cleaning contaminated vehicles, and releasing contaminated vehicles. The
dose assessment and operational guideline derivation methodologies discussed in this chapter
can be applied to both RDD and IND incidents. However, they are not included in the current
version of RESRAD-RDD&IND code (Version 2).

12.1 STREET FLUSHING

12.1.1 Introduction

 Potential radiation exposures resulting from flushing radionuclides off a contaminated
street after an RDD event were analyzed by considering three destinations for the flushed
radionuclides. The first destination was a publicly owned treatment works (POTW). The
radionuclides and contaminated dust particles were assumed to be carried by the flushing water,
enter the underground sewage lines, and then travel to a POTW where wastewater is collected
and treated. The second destination was a surface water body. Rather than entering a sewage
line, the radionuclides were assumed to infiltrate or run off to a stormwater collecting system and
be discharged to a stream, lake, or river. The last destination was a detention pond. The
radionuclides were assumed to be carried by the flushing water that runs to a nearby stormwater
detention pond and to settle at the bottom of the detention pond by being adsorbed into the
surface soils. Potential exposure scenarios associated with each destination were developed, and
radiation doses were analyzed.

12.1.2 Scenario Descriptions

 12.1.2.1 POTW Destination

 For simplicity and conservativeness, radionuclides that are flushed to a POTW were
assumed to concentrate in the sludge that is generated from wastewater treatment. Potential
radiation exposures would result from handling and using the sludge material. Four exposure
scenarios (for four receptors) that are likely to give higher doses were considered. The four
scenarios are as follows:

 1. Onsite residents — residents of houses built on agricultural fields applied with
 sludge;

 2. Incinerator neighbors — neighbors of a sludge incinerator;

 151

 3. Sludge application workers — agricultural workers who operate equipment to apply
 sludge to agricultural lands; and

 4. POTW workers — workers at a POTW involved in sampling, transport, and biosolids
 loading operations.

 The exposure pathways considered were external radiation; inhalation of dust particles
and radon; and ingestion of plant food, meat, milk, aquatic food, soil, and water. Table 12.1 lists
the pathways considered for each scenario.

Table 12.1 Exposure Pathways Considered for Various Sewage Sludge Exposure
Receptors/Scenariosa

 Exposure Pathways

 Inhalation Ingestion
 Sewage Sludge Exposure External of Dust of Plant Ingestion Ingestion Ingestion Ingestion Ingestion Inhalation
 Receptors/Scenarios  Radiation Particles Foods of Meat of Milk of Fish of Soil of Water of Radon

  worker

a Note: An “x” indicates that the exposure pathway was considered.

 12.1.2.2 Surface Water Destination

 When flushing water runs off or infiltrates a stormwater collecting system, it is most
likely to be discharged to a surface water body, such as a stream, lake, or river. Potential
radiation exposures could result from using the surface water body for recreational purposes,
such as swimming, fishing, and boating.

 Upon entering the surface water body, concentrations of radionuclides would be greatly
diluted. Because the flushing activity is a one-time event and would not last long (ranging from a
few hours to a few days), the contamination of surface water is expected to be temporary.

 Further dilution would occur between the stormwater discharge point and the location
where potential exposures could occur. Therefore, the surface water concentrations at the
exposure location are expected to be very small. The resulting radiation exposures would be
much smaller than those associated with a POTW or detention pond destination where
radionuclides are collected and concentrated in a finite volume and the exposure duration of
potential receptors is longer.

 152

 Based on the above discussion, dose analyses were not conducted for the surface water
body destination. However, for making decisions, regulations and other risks should be
considered.

 12.1.2.3 Detention Pond Destination

 When a stormwater detention pond is located near a radioactively contaminated street,
radionuclides flushed off of the street could flow to a detention pond with runoff water. They
could settle and adsorb into soil at the bottom of the pond and form a secondary contamination
area. Potential human radiation exposures could occur if the detention pond is also used for
recreational or sporting activities after the water drains out. Radiation exposures could occur
through external radiation, inhalation of dust particles, and ingestion of soil.

12.1.3 Main Assumptions and Parameters

 12.1.3.1 POTW Destination

 The daily amount of sludge generated by a POTW was developed based on information
for a medium-sized POTW that has a daily wastewater treatment rate of 13 million gallons. The
amount of sludge generated each day was estimated to be about 43,500 kg (43.5 metric ton).
The area of the contaminated street was assumed to be 4,000 m2 (200 m  20 m). Additional
assumptions include (1) 100% flushing efficiency whereby all radionuclides are flushed off
the street and (2) 100% collection of radionuclides at the POTW. 1 pCi/m2 deposited on
the contaminated street will result in the activity concentration of 4,000 pCi/43,500 kg =
9.2E-05 pCi/g in sludge. For smaller facilities that will generate less sludge, the resultant activity
concentration would be higher.

 In the onsite resident and sludge application worker scenarios, sludge is applied directly
to agricultural or reclamation land and then mixed into the top fifteen centimeters through tilling
and natural processes such as plant root action. Application occurs one time. RESRAD-ONSITE
code (Yu et al. 2001) is used for analyzing these scenarios.

 The activity concentration (i.e., specific activity) of the source term for RESRAD-
ONSITE is that of the soil-sludge mixture, rather than the sludge alone. The average
concentration of a radionuclide in soil depends on its initial concentration in the sludge, the
continuous processes of radionuclide decay and ingrowth, the amount of sludge deposited in one
application, the extent of tillage, and other factors. A simple expression for it assumes that sludge
of specific activity, Asludge (Bq/kg or pCi/g), is applied at a rate of S (metric tons/hectare) and
mixed into the top depth of d (m) of soil that has the density r (kg/m3). The specific activity in
the soil is then = Asludge × S/(d × r) for the land application. If the initial specific activity in dry
sludge is 1 pCi/g of any single radionuclide, the assumed application rate is 10 metric tons dry
sludge matter per hectare per year, or 1 kg/m2, the tillage depth is assumed to be 15 cm, and the
soil density value of r = 1,520 kg/m3 is the mean of the soil density distribution presented in
NRC (2001b), then the resultant soil activity = 0.16 Bq/kg (0.0044 pCi/g). The sludge
concentration of 9.2E-5 pCi/g will result in a soil concentration = 4.05E-07 pCi/g.

 153

 For the resident scenario, residents are exposed directly to external gamma emissions
from sludge-containing soil. They inhale resuspended dust and outdoor radon and, since the
home is built on land where sludge has been applied, indoor radon as well. With respect to the
ingestion pathways, the residents drink well water and grow vegetables (50%), fruit (50%), and
fodder (100%) and raise a few animals for personal consumption (100% of meat and milk). They
also may inadvertently ingest small amounts of soil. It is assumed that 90% of the drinking water
for humans comes from a well located at the downgradient edge of the source field and about
10% comes from uncontaminated sources (e.g., from a nearby town’s treated waterworks)
consumed while the resident is away from the property. Human ingestion of contaminated
surface water was considered to be unlikely, given the availability of well water, but there may
be exposure through fish caught from a local river or lake; fifty percent of the annual diet of fish
is from contaminated surface water. The food produced onsite is grown in soil that has been
treated with sludge and that is irrigated with groundwater and surface water that contains
radionuclides washed out of the sludge/soil mix. The soil ingestion pathway is included, but a
pica child who exhibits excessive hand-mouth activity or deliberate consumption of soil is not
considered. These ingestion assumptions are generally conservative. Parameters are in general
consistent with the parameters used in the Interagency Steering Committee on Radiation
Standards (ICORS) sewage sludge report (ISCORS 2005) except for the soil concentration and
area of contamination. Only deterministic analysis is done. To get a realistic estimate of dose,
parameters are kept at mean/median values from the distribution used in the sewage sludge
report (ISCORS 2005).

 For the sludge application worker scenario, it is assumed that a sludge application worker
drives or works on a truck, tractor, or other vehicle that dispenses liquid, de-watered, or dried
sludge on fields at a constant rate. The sources of exposure are the field itself and, to a lesser
extent, the sludge loaded on the truck. Radiation exposures are primarily by way of direct
gamma irradiation and through the inhalation of dust. The vehicle itself provides distance and
some isolation and shielding from the source material. Exposures are calculated for the time
sludge is being applied. The cab of the truck is simply a box. The sludge application rate and
tilling depth are the same as for the onsite resident scenario. Rather than performing a time-
integral as the truck traverses the field in a raster or spiral pattern, the driver is assumed to be
located at the center of the full field; both external and inhalation doses would come mainly from
the immediate vicinity of the truck and would change little from place to place within the field.
So, the dose would almost entirely be determined by the time the driver spends working there. It
is approximated that the worker will spend 30 minutes applying sludge in a 10,000 m2 area and
that the worker is exposed to same soil concentration as in the resident scenario. The amount of
sludge generated is 43,500 kg, and using the application rate of 10 metric tons dry sludge matter
per hectare, the exposure time = 43,500/10,000 × 30 minutes = 130.5 minutes = 2.175 hours.
Parameters are generally consistent with the parameters used in the sewage sludge report
(ISCORS 2005) except for the soil concentration, the area where sludge is applied, and the
exposure duration. Only deterministic analysis is done. To get the realistic estimate of dose,
parameters are kept at mean/median values from the distribution used in the sewage sludge
report (ISCORS 2005).

 The incinerator neighbor scenario considers the potential for exposure of a member of the
public residing near a typical sewage sludge incineration facility. The incinerator burns de-
watered sludge generated in a POTW facility after an RDD event, and the resulting particulate-

 154

containing exhaust gas is released from the top of a stack as a plume, some of which settles onto
the neighbor’s property. An exposed individual resides on a small farm located at the downwind
point of maximum average radionuclide air concentration at ground level and receives dose from
external exposure, inhalation, and ingestion. The incinerator will operate at nearly 100% capacity
for the time it will burn sludge generated from an RDD event. The dose rate calculation is similar
to what was done in the ISCORS (2005) report. First, the activity per day of radionuclide vented
from the stack for each kilogram of unit specific activity of dry sludge burned per day is
calculated using the radionuclide-specific control efficiency of the incinerator. Then, the air
dispersion model CAP88-PC is used to determine the concentration in air at the point of
exposure for a given value of the stack release rate, chi/Q. Finally, RESRAD-OFFSITE
(Yu et al. 2020) computes the dose to the neighbor from all exposure pathways. Parameters are
generally consistent with the parameters used in the sewage sludge report (ISCORS 2005) except
for the sludge concentration and the time it will take to burn the sludge generated after an RDD
event. Only deterministic analysis is done.

 The POTW worker scenario examines three representative jobs that may give rise to
relatively higher exposures to the sludge. These involve sludge sampling and sample transport to
the lab for analysis, sludge transport on an open conveyor belt, and biosolids loading operations
(e.g., filling trucks with sludge using a front-end loader in a large, enclosed room). For the
sludge sampling scenario, an exposure duration of 5 minutes was assumed for dose calculation.
For sludge transport, a worker was assumed to be standing close to the conveyor belt for 1 hour
when the sludge was being processed. The exposure duration for the biosolid loading scenario
was assumed to be 8 hours (all the sludge generated in a day was loaded in a truck). The DSRs
are linearly scalable with the total activity in the sludge. For all three sub-scenarios, exposures
are due primarily to direct gamma exposure and radon (if radon precursors are present). For
biosolids loading, dust inhalation is also possibly important. Parameters are generally consistent
with the parameters used in the sewage sludge report (ISCORS 2005) except for the sludge
concentration and exposure duration. Only deterministic analysis is done. RESRAD-BUILD
code (Yu et al. 2022) is used for the POTW worker scenarios.

 12.1.3.2 Detention Pond Destination

 Table 12.2 lists the parameter values used by RESRAD-ONSITE in dose calculations.
Soil concentrations were calculated based on the assumptions that (1) 100% of the radionuclides
flushed off the contaminated street flow to the detention pond; (2) after getting to the detention
pond, 90% of the radionuclides drain out with water to a stormwater collecting system; (3) the
remaining radionuclides settle at the bottom of the detention pond covering an area of 2,500 m2;
and (4) radionuclides concentrate on the top 1 cm of soil. The soil concentration would depend
on the size of the contaminated area and contamination thickness. When the contamination
thickness is fixed, a larger area would result in a lower concentration. An area of 2,500 m2 was
selected as a realistic, conservative assumption. The selection of a 1-cm thickness reflects the
fact that the accumulation of water in the detention pond would result in infiltration of water and
radionuclides to deeper soils.

 155

 radioactivity is flushed off, 10% is detained, and the
 secondary source has the above area and thickness.

12.1.4 Results

 12.1.4.1 POTW Destination

 Dose results for the various sewage sludge scenarios are listed in Table 12.3. They were

scenarios. For Cf-252, Co-60, Cs-137, Ir-192, Po-210, Ra-226, and Sr-90, the onsite resident
scenario has the highest doses. From the worker scenarios, the sludge loading worker scenario
gives the maximum DSR among all worker scenarios and is the only worker scenario considered
in dose calculations.

12.1.5 Conclusions on Street Flushing

 Potential radiation exposures resulting from flushing a contaminated street after an RDD
event were analyzed by considering the potential destinations of the radionuclides and the
various exposure scenarios pertaining to the destinations. The largest dose results among the
various exposure scenarios should be selected for deriving screening criteria (operational

 159

guidelines). Table 12.5 lists the calculated DSRs from flushing a contaminated street of
4,000 m2. To obtain dose estimates for an actual RDD event, the results should be multiplied by
the actual measured concentrations for the contaminated street. Table 12.5 also lists the derived
operational guidelines for a contaminated street on the basis of the 100 mrem/yr dose limit (DL).

 Three key assumptions are tied to the development of the operational guidelines listed in
Table 12.5. They are (1) the contaminated street area is 4,000 m2, (2) the flushing efficiency is
100%, and (3) all flushed radionuclides can be carried by water to either a POTW or a detention
pond. Most likely, these assumptions would ensure that the radiation doses estimated for each
destination scenario are conservative, thereby yielding conservative operational guidelines.

 Table 12.5 Summary of DSRs and Operational Guidelines for Street

 However, if the real situations are quite different from the assumptions, the operational
guidelines should be adjusted. For a contaminated street with a different area, the operational
guidelines should be adjusted linearly with the ratio of 4,000 m2 to the area. For a flushing
efficiency smaller than 100%, the operational guidelines should be adjusted linearly with the
ratio of 100% to the efficiency. The remaining radioactivity level on the street surface would
need to be compared with the operational guidelines developed for the street vendor scenario
before the street can be released for unrestricted use. If the final destination of the flushed
radionuclides is known, rather than using the operational guidelines listed in Table 12.5, the
DSRs pertaining to the final destination (also listed in Table 12.5) should be used to derive the
guideline values. The DSRs of the detention pond destination should be adjusted linearly with
(1) the ratio of 2,500 m2 to the size of the detention pond and (2) the fraction of the flushed
radionuclides that would end up in the detention pond. The DSRs of the POTW destination

 160

should be adjusted linearly with the fraction of the flushed radionuclides that would end up in the
detention pond.

contaminants go to the POTW destination for the onsite resident, incinerator neighbor, and
sludge loading worker scenarios, respectively. Equation 12.8 shows the operational guideline
derivation when the contaminants go to the detention pond.

12.2 CLEANING CONTAMINATED VEHICLES

12.2.1 Introduction

 Derivation of operational guidelines for washing contaminated vehicles is presented in
this section. Vehicles with an exterior surface contamination level less than the operational
guidelines can be washed with water to reduce the contamination level to meet the vehicle
release guidelines because potential radiation exposures to the radionuclides removed by water
would not result in a dose greater than 100 mrem/yr to any receptor. If the exterior contamination

 161

level is greater than the operational guidelines, the vehicles would need to be cleaned by a
different method, or if they are washed, the used water would need to be collected and treated
before the final disposal.

 Assuming that the used water is not collected, a wide range of exposures to contaminants
removed by washing the exterior surfaces of a vehicle are possible. The exposures would depend
on the destinations of the contaminants, which can be identified by the destinations of the used
water. The water could carry contaminants to either an underground sewer line or stormwater
collecting system. If a detention pond were located nearby, they could be carried by water and
runoff to the detention pond. They could also adhere to the ground surface and become a
secondary contamination source. The destinations of the removed contaminants would depend on
where the vehicle is cleaned. Cleaning a vehicle can also involve vacuuming its interior surfaces.
After the interior is cleaned, the vacuum bag is assumed to be collected and sent to an approved
or appropriate facility for disposal so that radiation exposure to the removed contaminants is
controlled and limited.

 Potential radiation exposures to contaminants removed by washing vehicles were
analyzed. The radiation doses were estimated for the various exposure scenarios considered
applicable to each contaminant destination. For exposure scenarios that were analyzed in
deriving the operational guidelines for flushing contaminated streets, the dose estimates were
made on the basis of the calculated results. The amount of contaminants that would be removed
through washing was calculated by considering a passenger bus and a midsized car, which were
considered in deriving the operational guidelines for vehicle release. The dimensions of the
vehicles and the distributions of contaminants inside and outside the vehicles were also made
consistent with those assumed in the vehicle release analyses.

 The vehicle cleaning analyses focused on potential exposures to the contaminants after
they were removed from the vehicles. Potential exposures associated with the cleaning activities
to remove contaminants were not analyzed. In situations where contamination levels are
significant, the washing of vehicles would be done under controlled conditions and (1) the
cleaning workers would wear protective equipment or clothing to reduce potential radiation
exposures, if necessary, when cleaning the vehicle; and (2) the exposures of the cleaning workers
would be controlled and monitored by using dosimeters.

12.2.2 Main Assumptions and Parameters

 12.2.2.1 Cleaning Locations

 Considering the destinations of the washed-off contaminants, cleaning was assumed to
take place in one of the three following locations:

 1. On a private driveway or along a street. The vehicle was parked on a driveway of a
 suburban house or along a street in an urban area. Because the ground surface is
 slightly sloped at the edge of a driveway or street, water would flow toward a
 drainage hole and enter an underground stormwater or sewage collecting system. If a
 detention pond were located near the street, the water could flow to the detention

 162

 pond. The ground area that the water flows over would not be large. Secondary
 contamination of the ground surface would be possible, as some contaminants may
 adsorb to the ground surface and not travel with the water.

 2. In a large parking lot. The vehicle was parked in a parking lot that has a capacity for
 a large number of vehicles. Because the ground surface of the parking lot is flat,
 water could flow in different directions and spread over a large area before entering a
 drainage system or reaching a nearby detention pond. As a result, the secondary
 contamination, if formed, could cover a large area.

 3. In a car washing facility. The vehicle was washed and cleaned at a car washing
 facility. The washing facility was designed to control the drainage of water and to
 collect and discharge the water to a municipal sewer system. The control design
 prevents overflow of the water; therefore, secondary contamination of the ground
 surface would be negligible.

 12.2.2.2 Cleaning Process

 The following assumptions were made regarding the cleaning process:

 • 100% of the contamination from both inside and outside of the vehicle was removed.

 • The vacuum bag that collected the dust particles from inside the vehicle was sent to
 an approved or appropriate facility for disposal.

 • 10% of the washed-off contaminants deposited and were retained on the ground
 surface, where secondary contamination was considered. The rest (90%) was carried
 by water and entered an underground stormwater or sewage collecting system or ran
 off to a nearby stormwater detention pond.

 • When the vehicle was cleaned in a washing facility, 100% of the washed-off
 contaminants were carried by water to a sewage system.

 • The area of secondary contamination equaled 100 m2 when the cleaning took place on
 a driveway or along a street. The area of secondary contamination was 1,000 m2 when
 the vehicle was cleaned in a large parking lot.

12.2.3 DSRs and Discussions

 12.2.3.1 Landfill Destination

 The vacuum bag that contained the contaminated dust particles collected from cleaning
the interior of the vehicle may be qualified for disposal in a municipal landfill. The qualification
implies that the radioactivity level contained in the bag would be below applicable standards for
landfill disposal. Otherwise, it would be sent to a disposal site designated for radioactive wastes
and be handled by certified radiation workers. Potential receptors for the landfill destination

 163

include the landfill disposal workers and residents living close to the landfill (landfill neighbors).
The volume of the vacuum bag was expected to be very small compared with the volume of the
waste materials that would be disposed of in the same disposal cell. The shielding provided by
the waste materials, the on-top capping, as well as the engineering liner were expected to
minimize the release of contaminants to the surrounding environment, thereby reducing the
potential radiation exposures of the disposal workers and landfill neighbors.

 According to previous assumptions regarding the distribution of contaminants, the total
radioactivity deposited inside the vehicle would be a small fraction of the total radioactivity
deposited outside the vehicle (3% vs. 97%). Furthermore, the exposure scenarios associated with
the removed outside contamination do not involve installing shielding materials to prevent the
contaminants from reaching the potential receptors (see discussions in the following paragraphs);
as a result, radiation exposures for scenarios concerning removed outside contamination were
expected to be greater than for scenarios concerning removed inside contamination.

 Based on the reasons given, potential exposures of the landfill workers and nearby
residents were not evaluated further.

 12.2.3.2 POTW and Surface Water Body Destinations

 The contaminants flushed off from outside the vehicle could be carried by water and
enter a sewer line or an underground stormwater collecting system. If collected by a stormwater
collecting system, they would be discharged to a surface water body. If the contaminants enter a
sewer line, they would be collected at a POTW and assumed to concentrate in the sludge
material generated from treating the wastewater. Potential radiation exposures could result from
handling and using the sludge material.

 According to the analyses of radiation exposures from flushing a contaminated street,
radiation exposures associated with a surface water body destination would be much smaller than
the exposures associated with a POTW destination. Among the different groups of receptors
analyzed for the POTW destination, the onsite residents (residents of houses built on agricultural
fields formerly applied with sludge), the incinerator neighbors (neighbors of a sludge
incinerator), and the sludge loading workers would receive higher exposures than the other
groups of receptors. Therefore, this study considers only these three critical groups of receptors.

 Potential radiation doses resulting from flushing 1 pCi of radioactivity to a POTW are
listed in Table 12.6. The results were taken from the analyses of radiation exposures from
flushing a contaminated street.

 F – POTW = activity fraction of contaminants that go to the POTW,

 166

 TSLAct = actual time spent in loading sludge (hours),

 TSLdefault = default time spent in loading sludge (8 hours).

 12.2.3.3 Detention Pond Destination

 Flushed contaminants that were carried by water and flowed to a detention pond could
settle and adsorb to soil at the bottom of the detention pond. Potential radiation exposures could
occur if the detention pond were also used for recreational or sporting activities after water
drains out. Potential radiation doses resulting from flushing 1 pCi of radioactivity to the
detention pond are also listed in Table 12.6. They were obtained from analyses of radiation
exposures from flushing a contaminated street. The exposures occurred through external
radiation, inhalation of dust particles, and ingestion of soil. Equation 12.12 shows the dose
calculations for the detention pond receptor from cleaning multiple vehicles of any size. The
DSRs in the equation are from Table 12.6 for the detention pond destination.

𝐷𝑃(𝑅𝐹)𝑑𝑒𝑓𝑎𝑢𝑙𝑡 × 𝐸𝑇(𝐷𝑃𝑅)𝐴𝑐𝑡 ÷ 𝐸𝑇(𝐷𝑃𝑅)𝑑𝑒𝑓𝑎𝑢𝑙𝑡 (12.12)

where:

 D(DPR)vc = dose (mrem/yr) from vehicle cleaning for detention pond receptor,

 DSR(DPR)vc,i = dose-to-source ratio (mrem/yr per pCi) for radionuclide i to detention pond receptor,

 167

 12.2.3.4 Ground Surface Destination

 Secondary contamination would result when contaminants flushed off from the vehicle
adsorb to the ground surface while traveling with water. To assess potential radiation exposures,
the area of the secondary source was assumed to be either 100 m2 or 1,000 m2, depending on
where the washing activities were conducted. Radiation exposure was evaluated for a person
who lives or works close to the washing location (e.g., driveway, street, or parking lot) and has
the opportunity of getting close to the secondary contamination source. Potential exposure was
considered to result from direct external radiation and inhalation.

 The RESRAD-BUILD computer code (Yu et al. 2022) was used in dose calculations for
the direct external exposure pathway. It was assumed that the contamination was uniformly
distributed on the ground surface and that receptors would spend 500 hours in a year close to the
contaminated area. Because of contaminant absorption on the ground surface, potential
resuspension of the contaminants in the air was assumed to be slow and stable. The initial
resuspension factor of 1  10-6 m-1 used in deriving operational guidelines for Groups A−E was
reduced by a factor of 10 to account for the slow release from a large area. Because the
contaminated area is also smaller (in this case, 100 m2 and 1,000 m2) compared with the area
assumed in Groups A−E analyses (10,000 m2), the resuspension factor was further corrected by
an area factor. The area factor was calculated using the RESRAD-ONSITE computer code (Yu
et al. 2001). A respirable fraction of 0.1 was assumed. The inhaled contaminated dust particles
that were greater than respirable size were assumed to reach the gastrointestinal tract and result
in ingestion doses. To consider the influence of radiological ingrowth and decay, the radiation
doses obtained with the initial ground surface and air concentrations were multiplied by an
average decay factor over 1 year to get the final dose results.

 Table 12.7 lists the parameters used in dose calculations for the secondary source with a
small and large area (100 m2 and 1,000 m2). Tables 12.8 and 12.9 provide the DSRs (mrem/yr
per pCi).

radiation exposures estimated among the different destinations should be used with an applicable
dose limit to derive the final operational guidelines for vehicle cleaning.

12.2.5 Conclusions on Cleaning Contaminated Vehicles

 The operational guidelines for vehicle cleaning were developed by considering different
locations where the cleaning activities would take place and different destinations where the
removed contaminants would be carried. Generally speaking, the parameter values and
assumptions used in the dose calculations are quite conservative; therefore, the conservativeness
of the derived operational guidelines can be guaranteed. In reality, when the location of the

 174

cleaning activities and the destination of the contaminants are known, other than using the
default operational guidelines listed in Table 12.13, operational guidelines can be derived using
the pertaining dose results listed in Tables 12.11 and 12.12 as shown in Equations 12.15–12.20.

12.3 RELEASE OF CONTAMINATED VEHICLES

12.3.1 Introduction

 The operational guidelines discussed in the following sections were derived for use in
releasing vehicles contaminated during an RDD event. Vehicles with a contamination level less
than the operational guideline were considered very unlikely to result in a radiation dose greater
than 100 mrem/yr to the driver and passengers and can be released without further
decontamination.

 Preliminary analyses considering different types of vehicles were conducted, and the
results showed that radiation dose rates received by drivers and passengers were not much
different for different types of vehicles because the larger the dimensions of a vehicle, the larger
the exposure distance between the receptor and the radiation sources. As a result, in the final
analyses, only two types of vehicles that are most commonly used by the general public for daily
activities that also capture the variability of the time spent inside the vehicles by different
receptors were considered. These are a passenger bus and a midsized car.

 It was assumed that all outside surfaces of the vehicle were contaminated during an RDD
event and some contamination was deposited inside the vehicle (mostly on the floor) as a result
of air exchange or being tracked inside by shoes. Radiation doses were calculated for a vehicle
passenger and a vehicle driver by using the RESRAD-BUILD computer code (Yu et al. 2022).
They were calculated for a 1-hour exposure duration because it is very likely that the source
concentrations would change quickly due to the action of wind and air exchange. Radiation
doses over the first year, however, were also calculated. They were based on a conservative
assumption that the amount of contaminants deposited on the interior and exterior surfaces of the
vehicle would not change over time and that the radioactivity would decrease through
radioactive decay.

 The operational guidelines were derived on the basis of a dose limit of 100 mrem/yr.
Sensitive parameters that affect the dose results were identified. The most restrictive operational
guidelines were compared with published values from ANSI/HPS N13.12 (ANSI/HPS 2013).

12.3.2 Scenario Description

 Two types of vehicles were analyzed: a passenger bus and a midsized car. The vehicles
were modeled as a box with six surfaces. It was assumed that (1) all outside surfaces of the
vehicles were contaminated at the same level during an RDD event, (2) the contamination level
on the interior surfaces was 10% of the level on the exterior surfaces, and (3) the contamination
was uniformly distributed. In a preliminary analysis, a convertible vehicle was considered for
which the contamination level on the interior surfaces was the same as the contamination level

 175

on the exterior surfaces. However, because the areas of the exterior contaminated surfaces were
much smaller than those for a nonconvertible vehicle, the resulting radiation doses for a
convertible vehicle were not necessarily greater than those for a nonconvertible vehicle.
Therefore, a separate dose calculation for convertible vehicles was not conducted in the final
analysis.

 Potential radiation doses resulting from seven exposure pathways were included in the
analyses: (1) external exposure directly from the exterior surfaces, (2) external exposure to
materials deposited on the interior surfaces, (3) external exposure due to air submersion,
(4) inhalation of airborne radioactive particulates, (5) inhalation of aerosol radon progeny (when
radon precursors are present), (6) inadvertent ingestion of radioactive materials directly from the
interior surfaces, and (7) inadvertent ingestion of materials deposited on objects brought into the
vehicle due to the resuspension of contaminants from the interior surfaces. To simplify the
analysis, external exposure to materials deposited on the interior surfaces was calculated by
considering all contaminants as deposited on the floor that has a surface area equivalent to the
bottom dimensions of the vehicle. The RESRAD-BUILD computer code (Yu et al. 2022) was
used in the dose analyses, and both short-term (hourly) and long-term (1-year) doses were
calculated.

12.3.3 Main Assumptions and Parameters

 For dose calculations, all exterior surfaces (roof, floor, and four sides) were contaminated
at the same concentration levels. It was further assumed that the concentration on the interior
floor was 10% of that on exterior surfaces. Table 12.14 lists the characteristics of the vehicles
used in the analysis.

 Table 12.14 Vehicle Characteristics Used in
 Deriving Operational Guidelines for Vehicles

 Parameter Bus Car

                          Length, m                          12.1     3
                          Width, m                            2.6     1.7
                          Height, m                           2       1.2
 Air exchange rate, h-1 5 3
 Fraction of contamination on the 0.1 0.1
 interior floor of the vehicle

 A passenger bus was assumed to be 12.1 m long, 2.6 m wide, and 2 m high (dimensions
were taken from the Mack-Blackwell Transportation Center’s report on large school bus design
[Gattis and Howard 1998]). Radiation doses were calculated for different receptors sitting at
different positions in the bus. The receptor sitting at the center of the bus (at a height of 0.5 m
from the floor) was found to receive the maximum external dose. Radiation doses were also
calculated for a bus driver located 0.5 m away from three contaminated surfaces (the bottom, the

 176

front, and one of the two sides bordering the front). The exposure duration assumed for the bus
driver was greater than that assumed for the other passenger receptors.

 A midsized car was assumed to be 3 m long, 1.7 m wide, and 1.2 m high (dimensions
were taken from NUREG-1717, Appendix A, Table A.1.2 [NRC 2001a]). Radiation doses were
calculated only for the driver because preliminary analyses showed that radiation doses received
by the driver and the passengers were about the same.

 12.3.3.1 External Pathway

 For both the passenger bus and midsized car, it was assumed that external radiation from
radionuclides deposited on exterior surfaces of the vehicle was attenuated by the steel material
that made the body parts. For high-energy gamma emitters, this shielding would not change the
external dose significantly. The steel material was assumed to have a thickness of 0.07 cm. For
external radiation that comes from the radionuclides deposited on the interior floor, no shielding
was assumed between the contaminants and the receptor.

 12.3.3.2 Ingestion Pathway

 The receptor was considered to ingest contaminants through two mechanisms: direct and
indirect. The direct mechanism considers ingestion of contaminants that were deposited on the
interior surfaces of the vehicle. (Contaminants may stick to the receptor’s hand when the
receptor touches the interior surfaces, such as the steering wheel, dashboard, or seat, and be
accidentally ingested.) The indirect mechanism considers ingestion of contaminants that were
deposited on objects that were brought into the vehicle (e.g., food items) after the RDD event;
the deposition results from resuspension of the contaminants on the interior surfaces. For both
direct and indirect ingestion, an ingestion area of 1 cm2 was assumed. For RESRAD-BUILD to
calculate the radiation dose from direct ingestion, the corresponding fraction of the ingestion area
(1 cm2) to the total area that the receptor may touch (assumed to be the total interior surface area)
needs to be calculated and input into the code (see Table 12.15).

 12.3.3.3 Inhalation, Deposition, and Immersion Pathways

 The air concentration inside the vehicle was calculated assuming a resuspension factor of
1  10 m-1 (IAEA 2002; NRC 2001b; EPA 1991). The resuspension factor is defined as the
 -6

ratio of the air concentration to the initial concentration on contaminated surfaces. To implement
this assumption in RESRAD-BUILD, specific values were assigned to some input parameters
pertaining to the contaminated floor (see Table 12.15).

 For exterior contaminated sources, it was assumed that they would not contribute to the
inside air concentration. Parameters used for deriving operational guidelines for vehicles are
listed in Table 12.15. The rest of the parameters were not changed from their default values in
the RESRAD-BUILD code.

 177

12.3.5 Conclusions on Release of Contaminated Vehicles

 To account for the uncertainties regarding the extent of contamination, the exposure
patterns, and the interactions between the contamination sources and the surrounding
atmosphere, conservatism was incorporated in the dose calculations. Table 12.23 lists the
sensitive parameters that have great impact on the dose results and the operational guidelines.

 Table 12.23 Sensitive Parameters That Have Great Impacts on the Vehicle Release
 Operational Guidelines

 Bus Bus Car Car
 Parameter (long term) (short term) (long term) (short term)

 Exposure duration, d 365 1 365 1
 Time spent in the vehicle over 2,000 (driver) 1 592 1
 the exposure duration, h 623 (passenger)
          Resuspension factor, m-1                1.0E-06           1.0E-06       1.00E-06      1.00E-06
 Ratio of activity concentrations 0.1 0.1 0.1 0.1
 (interior/exterior surfaces)
          Inhalation rate, m3/d                     33.6              33.6          33.6          33.6

 Table 12.24 compares the most restrictive operational guidelines for vehicle release with
published screening values from ANSI/HPS N13.12 (ANSI/HPS 2013) concerning the clearance
of surface-contaminated materials. It should be noted that the screening values from ANSI
N13.12 were derived to provide a reasonable expectation that doses would be generally less than
1 mrem/yr. Furthermore, the exposure scenarios considered in deriving the material clearance
screening values are different from those considered in deriving the operational guidelines for
vehicles. For instance, ANSI N13.12 considers scenarios related to the clearance of personal
properties.

 The operational guidelines listed in Table 12.24 are for individual radionuclides,
assuming no other radionuclides are present. In case multiple radionuclides are found on the
surface of a vehicle, the sum of fractions rule should be applied in releasing the vehicle. Separate
values are provided for cars and buses where the former are illustrative of values that might be
used to establish protective action recommendations for personal-use vehicles and the latter for
mass transit-type vehicles.
